You ask no questions; a newcomer speaks only when spoken to; and observes a due diffidence in the choice of a seat, respectfully keeping at a distance from the fire. Moreover, criticism is ruled out. The woman next you may be a gaol-bird, a pickpocket, a hardworking office cleaner, an itinerant char or street vendor, or, as often happened, just a prostitute. It makes no difference. You never comment—you observe, listen and try to understand.

That night, it seemed to me, every type was present. Women in the raw, emotionally speaking, expressing themselves without reserve, in a tongue that has a knife behind each word. The majority of my fellow lodgers were young, pretty and attractively dressed. They all wore sunset silk stockings from the very cheapest kind to a better variety. Their slim little trocks were well cut, and their coats, save for the quality, might have been bought in the West End. There was a sprinkling of elderly women and a few middle-aged. They all sat about and talked and laughed, and occasionally one of the girls would go out and return with chocolate, fruit, or a bottle of lemonade. Intoxicants must not be brought into a public lodging house. It is one of the few rules which the L.C.C. insist should be observed.

===Chinese===
我进去时屋里已挤满了人，但没人盯着我看；我问门边一个女人可不可以坐，也没有谁介意。她快快活活地招呼我，请我过去取暖。在赤贫者的世界里，道德参差多样，礼数却只有一套。你不发问；新来的人只有别人搭话时才开口；挑座位时恪守本分，恭敬地与火炉保持距离。而且，指摘是被排除在外的。你旁边的女人可能是坐过牢的、掏包的、卖力气的办公室清洁工、打零活的钟点佣工或街头小贩，也常常就是个妓女。这毫无分别。你从不议论——你观察，倾听，尽力去理解。

那天晚上，在我看来，各路人马都到齐了。女人处在情感上的赤裸状态，毫无保留地表达自己，用的那种话，每个字后面都藏着一把刀。同宿者大多数年轻、漂亮、衣着入时。她们都穿着夕阳色的丝袜，从最廉价的一种到略好的一种。她们纤瘦的小连衣裙剪裁得不错，那几件外套，若不论料子，简直像在西区买的。也点缀着几位老妇和几个中年女人。大家围坐着谈笑，不时有个姑娘出去一趟，带回来巧克力、水果或一瓶柠檬水。酒类不得带入公共寄宿旅舍。这是伦敦郡议会坚持必须遵守的少数几条规矩之一。

===Original===
A decrepit old creature in black, who might have been hundreds of years old, rose from her seat by the fire and shuffled towards me. She was an “official” of the house, paid by the proprietor, and kept a close espionage on all his clients.

“Have you paid for your bed?” she asked. It is a parrot cry, which punctuates all the emotional stress and storm occasionally let loose in that queer kitchen.

“It’s one and tuppence,” she quavered. “Go to the window up the courtyard and ask for bed number 28. Don’t forget it’s one and tuppence.”

You can imagine how thankfully I fingered the two coppers I had received for my last box of matches. In the cruel cold of that night, the warm, close kitchen seemed a paradise. Another old woman sat at a table by the open window up in the courtyard. She took my money and I waited for a ticket or some form of receipt. I need not have troubled, however. There seems to be some subtle form of communication between the window and the aged crone, for if ever some poor thing tries to get a bed on the nod, insisting to “Ida” that she has deposited the cash, the ruse always fails.

“You haven’t paid your money!” That high-pitched, quavering voice must sound to many like the trump of doom.

===Chinese===
一个穿黑衣的衰朽老太婆——看着有好几百岁了——从炉边的座位上起身，蹭到我面前来。她是这屋子的「公职人员」，由老板付钱，替他严密监视所有客人。

「你的床钱付了吗？」她问。这是句鹦鹉学舌式的口头禅，给那间怪厨房里不时倾泻而出的情感重压与风暴打着标点。

「一先令两便士，」她颤声道。「到院子里那扇窗去，要 28 号床。别忘了，一先令两便士。」

你可以想象，我摩挲着卖掉最后一盒火柴换来的那两枚铜板时，心里多么感激。在那晚凛冽的严寒里，温暖而人气旺盛的厨房俨然天堂。院子里那扇敞开的窗边，另一张桌子旁坐着个老妇人。她收了我的钱，我等着一张票或什么收条。其实不必操心。窗子与那老虔婆之间似乎有某种隐秘的通气法子：若有哪个可怜虫想赊一张床，一口咬定对「艾达」（Ida）说钱已交了，这把戏从来不能得逞。

「你还没交钱！」那尖利发颤的嗓音，落在许多人耳朵里，想必跟末日号角差不多。

===Original===
The kitchen was still crowded when I returned, and I listened to a babel of voices. Very few of the women were Londoners, they mostly seemed to hail from the provinces. Quite a number from Liverpool, some from Wales and Ireland with bonny Scotland holding its own. A sturdy young woman with bright eyes was industriously marking up her face. She wore the inevitable sunset stockings and her patent shoes, brightly polished, were painfully thin.

“I’ve had awful bad luck today,” she said. “But I’m going to have another shot before I turn in. There’ll be time before the pubs close to go up to the park, and maybe, I’ll get a man to buy me a drink.”

A dark faced, bobbed haired girl said she would go too, though, as she explained, she was rather tired, and had already walked from Hendon, where, among other things, she had been shying for coconuts. With the lavish generosity of her type, she handled round large pieces which we all accepted. The two went off together to try their luck, I hoped sincerely they already had the price of their beds. There was something inexpressibly tragic in the thought that these two young things had to go to the park, not for a drink alone, but to earn a pitifully small sum for the hire of their bodies.

===Chinese===
我回到厨房时，屋里还是挤挤挨挨，我听着一片嘈杂的人声。这些女人极少是伦敦本地人，多数像是外省来的。相当一批来自利物浦，有些来自威尔士和爱尔兰，秀丽的苏格兰也不甘示弱。一个眼睛明亮的结实姑娘正在勤勤恳恳地往脸上扑粉描眉。她照例穿着夕阳色丝袜，漆皮鞋擦得锃亮，薄得让人心疼。

「我今天倒霉透了，」她说。「可我睡下前还要再试一把。酒馆关门前来得及上公园去，兴许能碰上个男人请我喝一杯。」

一个黑脸膛、短头发的姑娘说她也去，不过她解释说，自己已经相当累了——她是一路从亨登（Hendon）走过来的，在那儿掷套圈套椰子，还干了些别的。她以她那类人特有的豪爽大方，把大块的椰肉分给大家，我们都接了。两人结伴去碰运气了，我诚心诚意地盼着她们已经攒够了床钱。想到这两个年轻的生命不得不去公园——不光是为了一杯酒，而是为了挣那么一点可怜的钱、出租自己的身体——这里头有一种说不出的悲凉。

===Original===
Several girls came in to “arrange their faces,” and one, a very slender, piquant creature, took out of her Dorothy bag an entire set of silk underclothing, which she had washed at the public baths and brought into the kitchen to dry. She held them before the coke fire, chatting the while of her experiences.

“Any luck, dearie?” said a soft-voiced Irishwoman.

“No, luck’s right out,” said the slim one. “I tell you I’m getting fed up with bits and scraps of things. I haven’t had a whole night with a man for six weeks.” She made the statement with a complete frankness that had not the least touch of obscenity, and her sentiment was generally applauded. You must understand that the attitude of these young people towards sex cannot be described as immoral; nor is it immoral. It is the result of the will to live; they are unable to keep themselves in any other manner. They have their own code of ethics, a rigid one, which demands an irrevocable decision not to let a pal down, and never in any circumstances whatsoever to interfere with other people’s business or give away their affairs. Business, generally speaking, seemed to be very dull, from what I gathered. A Lancashire lass with a strong burr sighed piteously for Liverpool.

“I’d go back there tonight, if I only had the fare,” said she.

“Maybe you’ll get enough to go up for the Grand National,” said the cheery little lady still drying her underwear before the fire. “I wonder now, if I was to meet the Prince of Wales, do you think he’d give me a pound if he wanted me?”

One of the elderly women—an office cleaner as I afterwards learned—answered the naive query.

“He couldn’t give you a pound, my dear, however much he wanted to. The Royal family never have no money of their own; they pay everything through their secretaries. You’d just have to send in the bill.”

“Not half,” said the cheery girl. “*I* wouldn’t give him away to Queen Mary.”

===Chinese===
又有几个姑娘进来「拾掇脸面」，其中一个身段纤瘦、俏皮伶俐的，从多萝西小提包（Dorothy bag）里掏出一整套丝质内衣——她在公共浴堂洗的，带进厨房来烘。她把内衣举在焦炭炉前，一边聊着自己的经历。

「有运气吗，亲爱的？」一个嗓音柔软的爱尔兰女人问。

「没有，运气跑得精光，」瘦姑娘说。「告诉你们，这点滴零碎的买卖我受够了。我六个礼拜没跟一个男人过过整夜了。」她说得全然坦白，没有一丝淫秽气，她的情绪得到了普遍赞同。你必须明白，这些年轻人对性的态度，不能称之为不道德；它也确实不是不道德。它是求生意志的结果；她们无法以别的方式养活自己。她们有自己的一套伦理，而且严苛：绝不能亏待同伴，这一点无可反悔；任何情形下绝不干涉别人的事、不出卖别人的底细。据我听来的，生意总的来说很清淡。一个兰开夏口音浓重的姑娘哀哀地叹着想回利物浦。

「今儿晚上我就回去——要是有车钱的话。」她说。

「兴许你能挣够去瞧全国大赛马会的钱，」那位还在炉前烘干内衣的快活小太太说。「我倒是在想，要是我碰上威尔士亲王，他要是想要我，你说他会给我一镑吗？」

一位老妇人——后来我知道她是办公室清洁工——回答了这个天真的问题。

「他给不了你一镑，亲爱的，任凭他多想要也不成。王室从来没有自己的钱；什么都得通过秘书付账。你只管把账单寄去就是。」

「可不，」快活姑娘说。「*我*才不会把他捅给玛丽王后呢。」

===Original===
It was at this moment that the emotional tornado broke upon the kitchen. These atmospheric disturbances are not uncommon among the homeless. Circumstances force them to lead highly concentrated lives in that they must seize on the moment when they find it—the moment which gives them the shelter of a roof, however pitifully impermanent.

The door was flung wide open, and a good-looking young woman in a dilapidated fur coat and battered feathered hat burst in. She flung a brown paper parcel on the floor with a gesture of tragedy.

“There it is,” she cried, “my old man’s washing—I tell you he won’t want it again for sixteen months.”

She was all strung up as she spoke, and then, suddenly, she collapsed, and crouching down on the bench beside the parcel began to cry. I have never seen a woman cry as she did. The most emotional outburst of the women of the middle class is reticent, almost austere, by contrast. It seemed an intrusion somehow, to witness such devastating grief. Her body shook with big sobs, interspersed with coughs, sneezings and other primitive methods of expression. All conventional barriers were down—the woman was raw, bleeding, utterly unable to hold herself in.

===Chinese===
就在这时，情感的龙卷风劈头砸进了厨房。这类大气扰动在无家者中并不罕见。境况逼着他们过一种高度浓缩的生活：逮着了机会就必须抓住——那给他们一片屋顶容身的时刻，哪怕这容身可怜到朝不保夕。

门被猛地推开，一个穿破旧皮大衣、戴压塌的羽毛帽的漂亮年轻女人冲了进来。她把一个牛皮纸包往地上一掼，那姿势像一整出悲剧。

「就在那儿，」她喊道，「我男人的换洗衣裳——我告诉你们，往后十六个月他都用不着它了。」

她说这话时浑身绷得笔直，随即，突然瘫了下去，蹲在纸包旁边的长凳上哭起来。我从没见过一个女人这样哭。相形之下，中产阶级女人最激烈的情感爆发也算是含蓄的、近乎冷峻的了。目睹这样摧毁性的悲恸，总觉得像是一种闯入。她的身子随着一阵阵恸哭发抖，中间夹着咳嗽、喷嚏和别的原始的表达方式。一切惯常的堤防都倒了——这女人是赤裸的、流血的，全然把自己持不住。

===Original===
I cannot repeat her language. It is not printable, but as I listened it did not shock me. I understood the violence of the feeling that moved her. I understood also why she had to cry out then and there without leaving one method of relief sealed up. Tomorrow she might be homeless. There might be no place in which to weep, vituperate or despair. It is the tragedy of the destitute that when there is a roof above their heads they must seize the chance to give voice to their emotions—emotions that we can take our time to think about, nicely to express and delicately to restrain. For consider—can you cry out in the street, shriek your agony to the pavements, raise your streaming eyes to the sky? Such demonstration comes within the definition of “a disturbance,” and she who shows her heart rent and bleeding, runs the risk of arrest—not to mention gaol. I am not here pleading that women should be allowed violently to weep in public places; I only say that emotional intemperance is inevitable if a woman has no home.

Presently she began to tell her story.

“The devils from Scotland Yard have got my Arthur for sixteen months. They came into court this morning and told—bloody lies.”

“Lies, is it,” said the Irishwoman. “Sure God Almighty’s truth ’ud choke ’em, the—bastards.”

“He’ll be six months in Wandsworth and nine in the Isle of Wight,” moaned the wife.

“But he knows the ropes; he’s done a stretch before, remember,” said a friend. “Besides, it’s not as if you didn’t know what it was like; you’ve done your bit, too, my girl.”

“That’s so, and everybody likes my Arthur, but—but I want him—oh, I want him!”

===Chinese===
她的言语我无法复述。那是不堪刊印的，可我听着，并不震惊。我懂得推动着她的那股感情的烈度。我也懂得她为什么必须当场放声哭出来，不留一种发泄的法子封着不用。明天她可能就无家可归了。可能再没有地方供她哭泣、咒骂或绝望。这就是赤贫者的悲剧：头顶一旦有了屋顶，就必须抓住机会把情感喊出来——那些情感，我们可以从容地想一想、好好地措个辞、体面地节制住。想想吧——你能在街上放声痛哭、把你的苦楚尖叫给人行道、把泪眼滚滚的脸仰向天空吗？这样的举动要被归入「扰乱秩序」的定义，哪个女人把碎裂流血的心示于人前，就冒着被抓的风险——坐牢还在其次。我并不是在这里主张应当允许女人在公共场所放声痛哭；我只是说：一个女人若没有家，情感上的失度是不可避免的。

过了一会儿，她开始讲她的事。

「苏格兰场那帮魔鬼判了我家亚瑟十六个月。他们今天早上上法庭，说的——全是该死的谎话。」

「谎话，可不是嘛，」爱尔兰女人说。「上帝的真理还不噎死他们，那帮——杂种。」

「他要在旺兹沃思蹲六个月，再在怀特岛蹲九个月，」做妻子的呜咽道。

「可他懂门道；他从前蹲过，别忘了，」一个朋友说。「再说，又不是你不知道那滋味；你也蹲过你的份儿呢，姑娘。」

「是啊，人人都喜欢我家亚瑟，可是——可是我要他——哦，我要他啊！」

===Original===
She hugged the spoiled packet of washing in her arms, and then threw it across the room. “Take it, Sally,” she said to a pretty girl. “I can’t bear to touch it now he’s gone from me.”

She sobbed on and on, and no one ventured near her. It is not manners to interfere. Some of the women continued their conversation in undertones, others waited, listening sympathetically, and then at last the Irishwoman chipped in.

“It’s not my business, I know, dear, and I’ve no call to speak to you, but I wouldn’t cry if I was you, it’ll hurt your stomach something cruel.”

The woman stared with streaming eyes, the racking sobs continued. At this moment the aged crone by the fireside felt it time to sound the official note.

“You haven’t paid for your bed,” she said.

A look of furtive distress crossed the woman’s face—I suppose she felt the street was very near.

“I haven’t paid yet, Ida,” she said, “but I’ve got the money—only do let me have my cry out, or it won’t come.”

Gradually she grew quieter; suddenly she started to her feet, began to laugh at the top of her bent, and, producing a parcel from under her coat, handed round a selection of pigs’ trotters.

Evanescent feeling? Easy tears? Hysterical outburst? Not a bit of it. Wait until you have no home to cry in, and then you will understand. Wait until you have walked about the streets, cut off from your kind as completely as though you were in a desert. Wait until, by a rare piece of luck, you get the money to pay for a bed, and can claim something of that community of interest and affection which goes by the name of home. Then, no sooner is your foot beyond the threshold, be it the kitchen of Kennedy Court, a doss house in the Waterloo Bridge Road, or any of the public lodging places, you will let fling, and laugh and scream and scoff, releasing the pent-up emotions of long and weary hours.

===Chinese===
她把那包揉皱了的换洗衣裳搂在怀里，随即又把它扔到屋子那头。「拿去吧，萨莉，」她对一个漂亮姑娘说。「他既然离开我了，我碰都不想碰它。」

她恸哭不止，没有人敢靠近她。插手干预是不合礼数的。有的女人压低声音继续聊，其余的等着，同情地听着；末了，还是那位爱尔兰女人开了口。

「这不关我的事，我知道，亲爱的，我也没资格跟你说话——可我要是你，我就不哭，哭起来胃可要狠狠遭罪的。」

那女人泪眼淋淋地瞪着，一阵阵揪心的恸哭没有停。这时，炉边的老虔婆觉得该亮出公家的调门了。

「你的床钱还没付。」她说。

女人脸上掠过一丝藏掖不住的窘迫——我猜想，她感到了街道已经近在咫尺。

「还没付呢，艾达，」她说，「可钱我有——只求你让我把这场哭哭完，不然它出不来。」

她渐渐平静了些；猛然间她一跃而起，放声大笑起来，笑了个够，又从大衣底下摸出一个纸包，把挑好的几只猪蹄分给大家。

转瞬即逝的感情？廉价的眼泪？歇斯底里的发作？全不是。等到你自己没有家可以哭泣，你就懂了。等到你也在街头走动，与同类隔绝得像在沙漠里一样，你就懂了。等到你好不容易碰上一回运气，凑足了付一张床的钱，能够认领那么一点名叫「家」的利益与温情的共同体，你就懂了。那时，只要你的脚一迈过门槛——不管是肯尼迪院的厨房、滑铁卢桥路的宿夜所，还是随便哪家公共寄宿处——你都会豁出去，笑、喊、嘲骂，把漫长疲惫的时辰里积压的情感一齐释放出来。

===Original===
I want to make it quite plain that my friend of the pigs’ trotters was not in the least degree under the influence of drink. Throughout my adventures in the underworld, I did not meet a drunken woman. There is a fixed idea also that those poor little prostitutes, with their pitiful earnings of pence and shillings (it is an event if they should get a pound), spend a large proportion of their income in alcoholic refreshment. This is not so. The girls I met at Kennedy Court, like those I met at Camden Town, the Old Kent Road and all over London, are very moderate in their spirituous tastes. There is a freehanded dispensation of chocolate, and they are extravagant in the matter of fruitdrops and lemonade, but they rarely buy drink for themselves, and there is not much cigarette smoking. The causes for this abstinence may be economic, though, personally, I do not believe that shortage of money affects the manner in which that money is spent. If a woman wants drink, be she in the possession of fourpence, four shillings or four pounds, drink she will have. But, as I say, it is not a craving from which these pretty young prostitutes suffer.

===Chinese===
我要说清楚：我这位猪蹄朋友丝毫没有沾酒。我在底层世界的全部历险中，没有遇见过一个喝醉的女人。还有一种成见，说那些可怜的小妓女，挣的是便士先令的可怜钱（挣到一镑就算件大事了）——却把收入的一大半花在酒上。事实并非如此。我在肯尼迪院见到的姑娘们，同我在卡姆登镇、老肯特路和整个伦敦见到的一样，对杯中之物相当节制。巧克力倒是出手大方，水果糖和柠檬水上也挥霍，但她们很少给自己买酒，香烟也抽得不多。这种节制的原因也许是经济上的；不过我个人并不相信缺钱能改变花钱的方式。一个女人若想喝酒，不论她手里是四便士、四先令还是四镑，酒总是要喝的。但正如我所说，这种渴求，这些漂亮年轻妓女并不受它的苦。

===Original===
The level of discussion in any public lodging house is not high. Small interest is shown in politics, and, save to a few women who have obviously drifted from different social strata, literature is a sealed book. Fashion is a fruitful topic of interest, and the passion for crossword puzzles, or the immediate equivalent, runs very high. Local gossip always holds the attention. So-and-so’s mother’s adventure with the lodger—the prospect of a job for a young sister—plans for the future when your own or a friend’s man comes out of gaol. Over and over again these things are talked over, and, save when an emotional tornado breaks up the calm, the conversation is leisurely, one might say spacious.

Only once did I meet a woman actuated by any interest in ideas. Ideas do not easily flourish on a starvation diet, and all the energies of the outcast are bent towards the problem of board and bed. This particular woman was about five and forty, tall, well built, with a face that had been beautiful and was still arresting. Ida had just shifted a newcomer to bed before she wished to go there—a discipline from which I also suffered, though I managed to evade the clutching hand a little longer.

The complainant testified long, loud and very bitterly.

“She’s always interfering, that old woman! You can’t get a thing in this place unless you pay for it through the nose; she won’t let you have a drop of hot water unless you give her some coppers, and if you don’t tip the old cat she reports you, and you may find yourself chucked out.”

“Its all very well to complain,” said the older woman, “but it’s your own fault, every one of you. You sit there and talk about her when she’s out of the room, and when she comes back you haven’t a word to say. You ought to go in a body and make a complaint to the man who employs her and makes his money letting beds to us. That’s what’s the matter with women the world over; they grumble among themselves, and when it comes to showing fight, they turn tail and run away. It’s always been the same story; it’s like that in life as well as in this place. That’s why men will beat us every time.”

===Chinese===
公共寄宿旅舍里的谈话水准不高。政治引不起多少兴趣；文学更是本合着的书，除非对那几个显然是从别的社会阶层漂落下来的女人。时装是常青的话题，填字游戏——或当下等价的东西——的热潮极高。本地的闲话永远抓得住耳朵：某家妈妈同房客的风流事——小妹妹找到差事的前景——自家男人的或朋友家男人的出狱以后的打算。这些事翻来覆去地谈；除非情感的龙卷风把平静掀翻，谈话是从容的，甚至可以说是宽舒的。

只有一次，我遇到一个对「想法」本身有兴趣的女人。想法在饥饿的口粮上不易生长，落魄者的全部精力都弯向了铺板和床的问题。这位妇人四十五上下，高个子，身板结实，一张脸曾经很美，如今依然夺目。艾达刚刚把一个新来的、还没打算睡的女人打发上床——这纪律我也领教过，虽然我多躲了一会儿那只抓人的手。

投诉者作证作了很久，嗓门大，口气极苦。

「她老是横插一杠子，那老太婆！在这地方你休想白得一样东西，不狠狠掏钱不行；不给她几个铜板，一滴热水也不给你；不孝敬那老货，她就告你的状，没准儿你就给轰出去了。」

「抱怨谁不会，」那位年长的女人说，「可这是你们自己的错，你们每一个人的错。她不在屋里，你们坐着讲她；她一回来，你们屁也不敢放一个。你们该结伙去投诉雇她的那个人——靠租床给我们挣钱的是他。天底下的女人坏就坏在这里：关起门来嘀嘀咕咕，真要较真动武，就掉头跑了。从来都是这一套；这屋里如此，人生里也如此。所以男人回回都能赢我们。」

===Original===
She swept out of the kitchen on the closing words, and someone mentioned that she was not coming back that night. She hadn’t any money.

“Poor Alice! It’s a shame—she’s always so generous with her cash. I’d have lent her a bob myself, only as like as not she’d bite my head off. It’s all very well for her to talk—people are afraid of her tongue; there’s something about Alice that you can’t get over, she’s different to us.”

I never discovered the story of Alice. I do not believe her life holds any dark mystery or hideous secret. I should think she had drifted from the professional classes for purely economic reasons, and that her tragedy, like so many others, is merely lack of means.

===Chinese===
说完最后一句，她一阵风地卷出了厨房；有人提到，她今夜不回来了。她一个钱也没有。

「可怜的爱丽丝！真作孽——她的钱向来花得大方。我倒真想借她一先令，只怕十有八九要被她抢白一顿。她说话硬气就硬气吧——人家都怕她那张嘴；爱丽丝身上有股东西你迈不过去，她跟我们不一样。」

爱丽丝的故事，我始终没有打听到。我不信她的生活里藏着什么黑幕或可怕的隐私。我想，她不过是因为纯粹的经济原因，从职业阶层漂落了下来；她的悲剧，同许许多多人的一样，仅仅是没钱。

===Original===
Destitution in most cases brings a furtive manner, an air that arouses immediate suspicion. I found myself acquiring that same manner after a very little while, so that when I was faced by an official I answered as though I were afraid (indeed, I sometimes was), and instead of entering a room in my usual fashion, I would slink round the doorway and sit humbly on the edge of a chair. By these, my own experiences, I learned how easy it is to form cruelly wrong conclusions from certain obvious facts. A straight glance of the eye, a clear tone in the voice, an assured and ready bearing; these are but the manifestations of a well-fed life. Take away your regular meals, your comfortable bed, your sense of security, and you will find yourself like any other outcast, slinking along the pavement, shrinking from attention, utterly void of that self-confidence which is the hall mark of success.

I succeeded in evading Ida’s tender care for some time, but at last the hour struck. She beckoned me with her claw-like hand.

“Now, Miss Twenty Eight,” said she, “it’s time you went to bed.”

===Chinese===
赤贫在多数情形下会带来一种鬼祟的举止，一种立刻招人起疑的神气。没过多久，我发现自己也沾上了这同一种举止：面对官员时，我答话答得像是害怕（有时也真怕）；进屋不再像平日那样，而是贴着门边溜进去，谦卑地坐在椅子沿上。凭这些亲身经历，我明白了：从某些显眼的事实得出残忍错误的结论，是多么容易。眼神的正直、声调的清朗、举止的笃定从容——这些不过是吃饱穿暖的生活的外现。拿走你的一日三餐、你舒适的床、你的安全感，你会发现自己同任何一个落魄者一样，贴着人行道溜，见人注目就缩，全然没有那种自信——成功的标志。

有一阵子我成功躲开了艾达的温存照管，但钟点终究到了。她用她那只爪子似的手招呼我。

「来吧，二十八号小姐，」她说，「该上床了。」

===Original===
I followed her along the courtyard, through a sinister-looking door and up a flight of stone steps, on to a landing, from which opened a room which led into two others. My sleeping place was on the first floor. The floors above were planned much in the same way. Three beds stood in a row with one bed across the foot. Mine was next to the wall, farthest from the door. The floor was clean, but that is all that can be said in regard to cleanliness. My bed was very hard, lumpy and badly stained. The sheets obviously had been slept in many times, there were no pillow cases, and the blankets were so short that you had to choose between cold feet and icy shoulders. There was no washing accommodation in the room where I slept, but at the end of the adjoining room there was a washstand which apparently had to serve all the inmates on that floor. The only other means of washing is in a scullery, off the courtyard, where tin lavatory basins are provided, and only cold water is laid on.

Consider for a moment what this means. A woman goes to bed with all the day’s dirt and fatigue upon her. In the morning if she desires to wash—and who does not?—she has to choose between dabbing herself in a limited supply of water or going downstairs across the courtyard and into the scullery. Which means that she must dress to go downstairs—you carry your wardrobe with you—and undress again on a flagged floor in the cruel cold of a winter’s morning. The man who goes to a public lodging house is very differently placed. He can have a hot bath, and, if he wishes, wash his shirt or pants and dry them in a hot-air closet in a few minutes. Woman, whose physical formation calls for more scrupulous cleanliness than man, is shut off from access to soap and water unless she is prepared to stand the unpleasant conditions above described.

===Chinese===
我跟着她穿过院子，进一扇阴森森的门，登上一段石阶，到一个楼梯口，那儿开着一个房间，通着另外两间。我的睡处在一楼。上面几层的格局大同小异。三张床排成一列，床脚横着第四张。我的靠墙，离门最远。地板是干净的——论清洁，能说的也只有这一句。我的床硬得很，疙疙瘩瘩，污渍斑斑。床单显然被睡过许多回，没有枕套，毯子短得你必须二选一：冻脚，还是冰肩膀。我睡的房间里没有任何盥洗设施，隔壁房间尽头有一张洗脸架，看样子要伺候整层楼的人。另一处洗漱的地方在院子外的洗涤房，备着白铁盆，而且只通冷水。

想一想这意味着什么。一个女人带着一整天的污垢与疲惫上床。早晨她若想洗一洗——谁不想呢？——她要么在有限的一点水里沾一沾，要么下楼穿过院子进洗涤房。而这就意味着她必须穿戴齐整才能下楼——你的衣柜是随身背着的——然后在一月寒冬清晨的石板地上再脱下来。男人进公共寄宿旅舍，处境天差地别。他能洗个热水澡，愿意的话，还能把衬衫衬裤洗了，放进热风烘衣柜几分钟烘干。女人——她的身体构造本要求比男人更讲究的清洁——却被挡在肥皂和水之外，除非她准备忍受上述那些不堪的条件。

===Original===
This lack of washing accommodation is not confined to Kennedy Court. Women’s public lodging houses are all deficient in this respect, though the establishments run by religious bodies are generally better equipped.

For the accommodation I have outlined—the use of a soiled bed, cold water and the lodging house kitchen—the charge is one shilling and twopence a night. This is an economic rent; eight and twopence a week for the use of a bed, is sufficient to provide clean sheets, proper bathrooms and human conditions. But even on the plea of good business I have been unable to get anyone to move in this matter. Thus, within a stone’s throw of the most luxurious part of London, you have a condition of things differing in essentials very little from the slums. And what I have said as to Kennedy Court holds good in varying degrees about the other lodging houses.

The process of undressing for the outcast is simple. A dim gas mantle gave an irritating light which enabled me darkly to follow the movements of my room mates. The general custom is to sleep—in the winter at all events—in all your clothes, removing your hat and shoes for the sake of courtesy. The younger girls take off their outer garments sometimes, but the older hands cling tightly to every stitch; a course—as I was subsequently to discover—highly to be commended.

===Chinese===
盥洗设施的匮乏不限于肯尼迪院。女子公共寄宿旅舍在这方面一概短缺，宗教团体办的几家通常设备好些。

我上面描写的这些待遇——一张脏床、冷水、旅舍厨房——收费每夜一先令两便士。这是一笔划算的租金：每周八先令两便士的床位费，足够提供干净床单、像样的浴室和人的境况了。可即便打着生意的算盘劝说，我也没能让任何人动一动。于是，在离伦敦最奢华的地段一箭之遥的地方，你看到的情况，就实质而言与贫民窟相差无几。肯尼迪院的这些话，以轻重不同的程度，同样适用于其他各家寄宿旅舍。

落魄者的脱衣程序很简单。一盏昏暗的煤气灯罩发出恼人的光，让我得以影影绰绰地看清同屋人的动作。通行的习惯是和衣而睡——至少冬天如此——出于礼貌才脱掉帽子鞋子。年轻姑娘们有时脱去外衣，老手们则紧抱着每一针每一线不放；这一手——我后来发现——大可推崇。

===Original===
I produced a nightgown from my brown paper parcel, and placed my clothes at the end of the bed, from whence at intervals they slipped on to the floor, to be recaptured by my groping hand, only to slide off once again. It must have been about two o’clock before the last vacant bed was filled. Downstairs in the kitchen high jinks were in progress; they were singing songs, dancing and generally enjoying life.

“It’s an awful noise, dear, isn’t it?” said the latest comer. She spoke with refinement, so much refinement that she was almost “naice.”

“One does not sleep in such a place as this from choice,” she continued. “I have never been here before but once.”

“It isn’t very comfortable,” I agreed, and watched her divest herself of two coats, two skirts, and other articles of apparel, all in duplicate.

“No, I don’t care to go into the room downstairs, and let all those women see me. They’re not fit to associate with, dear. But, as I say, in these hard times, one can’t afford four and sixpence for a night’s lodging; things have changed since the war.”

By this time she was in her petticoat. She stared at me curiously from the other side of the bed. I don’t think she altogether liked the look of me, for she solemnly re-invested herself in two of everything, and complacently got in between the sheets, fully clothed.

“I have a flat of my own, dear, beautifully appointed, electric light and hot and cold. But nowadays tradespeople are so tiresome; and I’ve had to leave it. You see I can’t go back because they insist I must settle their bills. That,” she said, with a spacious gesture, “is why I wear my two costumes.”

Presently the old crone extinguished the defective gas mantle, the noise died away from the kitchen downstairs, and Kennedy Court composed itself to slumber. I slept, but fitfully. I had not dreamt there were so many women without habitation or home, and the knowledge that, purely through force of circumstances, and by no individual merit, I was in possession of both, rankled—a sore injustice. Why should these young girls, these elderly women, be cut off from those things without which the soul cannot flower? Why should I, and so many hundreds like me, sleep softly and securely while their dragging feet walked the pavement, or, at best, found soiled shelter for the night?

===Chinese===
我从牛皮纸包里取出睡衣，把衣裳放在床尾；它们时不时滑到地上，被我摸索着抓回来，转眼又滑下去。大约到两点钟，最后一张空床才算睡满。楼下厨房里正在狂欢：唱歌的唱歌，跳舞的跳舞，一派享受人生。

「吵得凶吧，亲爱的？」最晚来的那位说。她谈吐斯文——斯文得简直到了「耐斯」（naice，拿腔拿调的 nice）的地步。

「谁也不是自愿到这种地方来睡的，」她接着说。「我从前只来过一回。」

「是不大舒服，」我应道，看着她一层层脱下两件外衣、两条裙子，还有别的成双成对的衣物。

「嗯，我不乐意进楼下那间屋，让那一屋子女人看我。她们不配与我往来，亲爱的。不过嘛，时世艰难，一夜四先令六便士的宿处也住不起了；打完仗，什么都变了。」

这时她脱得只剩衬裙。她从床那头好奇地打量我。我看她不大喜欢我这副模样，因为她郑重其事地重新把每样衣裳都穿上两件，然后心满意足地钻进被窝——衣冠齐整。

「我自己有一套公寓，亲爱的，陈设漂亮，电灯、冷热水俱全。可如今这些商家真烦人；我只好离开了。你瞧，我回不去，因为他们非要我结清账单不可。这个，」她说着，做了一个雍容的手势，「就是我穿两套衣裳的缘故。」

不多时，老虔婆熄了那盏不济的煤气灯罩，楼下厨房的喧闹沉了下去，肯尼迪院安定入眠。我睡了，但睡得断断续续。我从没想到，竟有这么多女人没有居所、没有家；而我，纯粹出于境遇的推挤、毫无半点个人的功德，竟两样俱全——这个认知像一根刺扎在心头：一桩生疼的不公。为什么这些年轻的姑娘、这些年长的女人，就该被切断了那些东西——没有它们灵魂便无法开花的东西？为什么我，还有成百上千个像我一样的人，就该睡得柔软安稳，而她们拖着沉重的脚步走街，或者至多在污渍斑斑的地方挨过一夜？

===Original===
The tragedy of the outcast came very close to me next morning. Ida woke us up about nine, and from under the clothes my room mates emerged, like full fledged chrysalis, completely clothed. The lady of the flat was not very cheerful. Like the rest, she dressed herself entirely in bed—an art that cannot easily be learned—and then, when the second coat was buttoned and she crawled from the dirty sheets, she began to cry.

“Aren’t you well?” I asked, and felt the utter feebleness of the words.

“Oh, my dear,” she said, “it’s the walking about, the walking about. Day after day, it’s always the same.”

And this is the sort of thing that goes on among the homeless. Walking about until the body aches and the mind becomes half doped. Is it any wonder that to get shelter at night the destitute do desperate things? This woman was not, I think, a prostitute, save at such times when self-preservation drove her to get money anyhow. I should say she had once been a shop assistant, or, perhaps, kept a lodging house. One seemed to trace her steady declension, slipping from room to room, at a cheaper and cheaper rent, and always leaving something behind, until at last, her whole wealth on her back, she is faced with destitution.

Soon one of the costumes will have to go. It will be necessary to sell it for food or shelter. And then her boots will begin to disintegrate, her remaining costume will grow dirty, she will be unable to change her underwear, and finally, perhaps she will be discovered in the street in a state of collapse. Not improbably, she will be charged at the police station for being without visible means of support. If she is lucky she will be sent to the workhouse; if things are against her, she may go to prison. In any case the interregnum will be a short one; and she will emerge into the light of day to resume the walking about, the never-ending, monotonously-grinding walking about.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨，落魄者的悲剧贴到了我的脸上。九点来钟艾达把我们叫醒，同屋的人从被窝里钻出来，像羽翼已丰的蝶蛹，衣裳早已齐齐整整。公寓太太不大快活。她同大家一样，在床上穿好了全部衣裳——这门手艺可不容易学——第二件外衣扣好、从脏床单里爬出来时，她哭了。

「您不舒服吗？」我问，一面感到这话彻底的无力。

「哦，亲爱的，」她说，「是这走啊走啊，走啊走啊。一天又一天，总是这样。」

这就是无家者中间日复一日上演的事。走到身子酸痛、头脑半麻木。落魄的人为了夜里有个遮蔽而干出绝望的事，还奇怪吗？这个女人，我想，并不是妓女——除了自保逼着她不择手段弄钱的时候。我猜她从前做过店员，或者自己开过寄宿旅舍。你几乎能描出她一步步的滑落：从一间屋挪到另一间屋，房租越来越便宜，每次总落下点什么，直到最后，全部家当穿在身上，赤贫迎面而来。

不久，两套衣裳里的一套就得去了。得把它卖掉换饭吃、换住处。然后她的靴子会开始绽线，剩下那套衣裳会越来越脏，内衣换了又换不成，末了，也许她会被发现倒在街头，不省人事。十有八九，她会在警局被起诉「无可见生计来源」。运气好，送济贫院；运气差，进监狱。无论如何，这段间歇不会长；她还会走进天光里，继续那走啊走啊——那没完没了、单调磨人的走啊走啊。

## V The Price of a Bed / 五　一张床的价钱

===Original===
The young, pretty prostitute of the humbler walks of harlotry is a growing problem. The older women, who have long graduated in the profession, are of a different category; with these I did not come into contact, except in one or two instances where circumstances had pushed them from comparative prosperity into destitution. The type of girl I encountered in the public lodging houses is, as a rule, fresh, amusing and very friendly.

How do they find themselves members of this calling? The reasons are various; but sheer vice is not one of them. Viciousness is generally accompanied by a peculiarly cold commercial sense, which very speedily increases a woman’s earning capacity in this particular walk of life. The majority of girls are victims of circumstance. I do not mean that they have had illegitimate children, but that accident has pushed them into their position. A number of them have been domestic servants who have stayed out late on their evening off, and been too apprehensive to return after the appointed hour. In some cases these girls find refuge in a Salvation Army Shelter, but, very often, things turn out differently. It is not difficult for an attractive young thing to form an acquaintance, and in sheer high spirits and love of fun she will go to lengths she had never contemplated, and wake up the next morning in a man’s bed.

Once this has happened, it is very difficult for a girl to get back to routine work. To begin with, she will have no reference, for—and this is an important point—in the majority of instances she will not face her former mistress and give a tangible account of her absence, nor will she tell the truth. Most girls in such a case prefer to lose the wages due to them and forfeit their clothes—and the pressure of hard facts soon sends them on to the street.

===Chinese===
风尘行当里较低等路上的年轻漂亮妓女，是一个正在扩大的问题。那些早已「毕业」多年的年长女人属于另一类；我与她们没有接触，只有一两回例外——境遇把她们从比较宽裕的处境推入了赤贫。我在公共寄宿旅舍里遇到的那一类姑娘，通常鲜活、有趣、非常友善。

她们是怎么入了这一行的？原因各式各样；但纯粹的邪恶不在其中。邪恶通常伴着一种格外冰冷的生意头脑，而那会很快抬高一个女人在这一行里的挣钱本事。大多数姑娘是境遇的受害者。我不是说她们生了私生孩子，而是说意外把她们推到了这个位置。她们中有不少是家仆，歇班的晚上在外逗留过了钟点，惶恐得不敢按点回去。这类姑娘有的进了救世军收容所避难，但更多时候，事情走向了另一头。一个有吸引力的年轻生命不难结识个把人，凭着一腔高兴劲儿和爱玩的天性，她会走到自己从未设想过的地步，第二天早晨在男人的床上醒来。

这种事一旦发生，姑娘就很难回到按部就班的工作里去了。头一条，她没有了荐保——这是个要紧之处——多数情形下，她不肯回去面对从前的女主人、对缺席作一个像样的交代，也不肯说实话。处于这般境地的大多数姑娘，宁可不要欠下的工钱、舍弃寄存的衣裳——而铁一般的事实压迫下来，很快就把她们送上了街头。

===Original===
I have mentioned that many of these girls come from the North of England, particularly from Liverpool. The explanation for this is interesting. In Liverpool, as elsewhere, if a girl be convicted a number of times for solicitation, she is more or less marked down by the police, and the surveillance renders it difficult, if not impossible, for her to approach likely clients. For this reason she migrates to London with its larger area and its many avenues of escape from official espionage. As a rule, it is but rarely that this type of prostitute is arrested. Of necessity she plies her trade in out-of-the-way spots, not venturing into competition with her more opulent sisters of Shaftesbury Avenue and Piccadilly.

A very high standard of generosity obtains among them, and much devotion, even heroism. A Scotswoman whom I met in a North London lodging house told me that “her man” had lost both his legs in the war. He had come down from his native place to London where he had secured admission to an institution. They had hoped to get married, and when he was maimed from the war she was heartbroken. She could not live without him and followed him to London without a penny and without work. She took the way of prostitution to keep body and soul together. He did not know what she did, and every visiting day would welcome her with unfailing affection. He was a heavy smoker and she could never get him enough tobacco. She would go without food to buy him cigarettes; indeed, she only lived for those few hours twice weekly when she forgot everything but her love. She was not a showy-looking girl; she was built on peasant lines, and one felt she would make a splendid mother of sturdy sons. Her avocation had not dullled her mind or coarsened her manner. I do not think her soul, in any sense, was seared by what she did.

“What else can I do?” she asked me, her wide eyes staring.

===Chinese===
我提到过，这些姑娘许多来自英格兰北部，尤其是利物浦。这中间的缘故说来有趣。在利物浦，同别处一样，一个姑娘若因拉客被定罪过几回，警察就多多少少把她记了档，这监视使她接近可能的客源变得困难，甚至全无可能。因此她迁移到伦敦——地盘更大，躲开官方耳目的路子也多。这一类妓女照例极少被拘捕。出于必要，她在偏僻角落里做她的营生，不敢去同沙夫茨伯里大道和皮卡迪利那些更阔绰的姐妹们竞争。

她们中间盛行着极高标准的大方，还有许多的深情，甚至壮烈。我在北伦敦一家寄宿旅舍遇到的苏格兰女人告诉我，「她的男人」在战争中失去了双腿。他从家乡下来到伦敦，进了一家慈善机构。他们本指望成婚，战争把他弄成残废时，她心都碎了。她没有他活不下去，便身无分文、没有活计地跟到了伦敦。她走了这条路，卖身来维持灵魂与躯壳不散。他不知道她干的是什么，每个探视日都以不变的深情迎她。他烟瘾大，她永远凑不齐足够的烟。她宁可不吃饭也要给他买香烟；说真的，她只靠每星期那两回、几个钟头活着——那时候，除了爱，她什么都忘了。她不是个招摇的姑娘；她是农妇的骨架，你会觉得她本可以做个了不起的母亲，养几个壮实的儿子。她的营生没有磨钝她的头脑，也没有粗粝她的举止。我不认为她的灵魂，在任何意义上，被她所做的这一切灼伤了。

「我还能有什么法子？」她问我，一双大眼睛直直地望着我。

===Original===
And this is the question which must be faced in any discussion of social conditions. The usual alternative to the streets offered to the prostitute is work at a washtub. “Homes for Fallen Women”—the name emblazoned all over the building—lay great stress on the curative properties of a laundry. Clear starching, it would seem, cleanses all sin, and an expert ironer can cheerfully put her record behind her. It is thought, apparently, that residence in a place of this description, where femininity is herded together, devoid of that cold, brutal masculinity without which women in the herd cannot keep sane, will purge them of all desire for their old calling. Frankly, the majority of them have no “desire” for prostitution. If they could get their living any other way and, at the same time, retain their liberty of action, there would not be much hesitation. But it is not feasible to expect that a young woman should prefer the undiluted society of her own sex, varied by long arduous hours of physical toil, to the chances of a life of adventure, even though that life means frequently going without a bed.

I think the only avenue of hope lies in a different direction. In the first place a number of public lodging houses, properly sanitated and equipped, should be established. These could be run on the lines which at present obtain; the necessary payment being the only qualification for admission. There could be, however, an official attached to the house to whom the girls could go for advice, if they wanted any. There should not be the least hint of compulsion, but an unprejudiced woman of good manners and humanity, in such circumstances, might be of real assistance. If, for instance, a girl has a taste for dressmaking—and so many of them do wonders with their needle—the Registrar or Secretary, whatever her title might be, could take a note of the applicant’s qualifications and put her in touch with a firm in need of a hand. This is but one branch of commerce for which these young and quick-brained creatures would be eligible. There is plenty of capacity among them, and, as I have said, very little vice. What has happened to them is what happens to many. They have missed the train of life at a certain junction and have been left behind. The least little assistance—proffered frankly and uncoloured by preaching—would help them catch the train at the next stopping place. Stern, inflexible officialdom, the solution of the reformatory, institution, or “Fallen Home,” is worse than useless; they are all tarred with the same brush; they all postulate the same thing—that the prostitute is intrinsically evil and must be purged by fire. It is the entire absence of moral superiority that gives the Salvation Army such a great influence. Your social reformer wants always to reform; the Salvation Army, so far as I know it, wants only to help. There is a whole world of difference in the result of these two opposite ideals.

===Chinese===
而这，正是任何社会状况的讨论都必须直面的一个问题。提供给妓女的、替代街头的惯常出路，是洗衣桶边的活计。「堕落妇女之家」——这名字刷满整座楼——对洗衣房的疗效极为看重。浆洗澄澈，仿佛就能洗净一切罪愆；一个熨衣好手尽可以欢欢喜喜地把过去翻篇。看来人们的想法是：住进这么一个阴柔扎堆、又被剥夺了那种冷硬男性气的地方——没了这口气，扎堆的女人便保不住清醒——就能涤净她们对旧行当的一切欲念。坦白说，她们大多数对卖淫根本没什么「欲念」。倘若能换个法子谋生、同时又保住行动的自由，她们不会有半分犹豫。可是，指望一个年轻女子宁要纯而又纯的同性群处、加上经年累月的重体力苦役，也不要冒险生涯的各种际遇——哪怕这生涯常常意味着没有床睡——那是不切实际的。

我以为，希望的出路在另一个方向。首先，应当开办一批卫生达标、设备齐全的公共寄宿旅舍。它们可以照现行的方式经营；缴付必要的费用是入住的唯一资格。但可以给旅舍配一位官员，姑娘们想讨个主意时可以去找她。丝毫不得带强制的意味——一位心怀坦荡、有教养、有人情味的女人，在这样的位置上，或许真能帮上忙。比如一个姑娘喜好女红裁缝——她们中间有那么多人一根针玩得出神——登记员或秘书，不论名目叫什么，可以记下申请人的专长，替她联络一家正缺人手的铺子。这不过是这些头脑伶俐的年轻人可以胜任的商业行当之一。她们中间有的是能力，而且如我所说，极少邪恶。她们身上发生的事，也发生在许多人身上：在某个道岔口误了生活的列车，被撇在了站台上。一点点最微小的援手——给得坦白、不掺说教——就能帮她们在下一站赶上车。铁面无私的官僚作风，感化院、收容机构或「堕落妇女之家」那套解决方案，比无用还要糟；它们是一路货，全都预设了同一件事——妓女本质上是恶的，必须用火来炼净。救世军之所以有那样大的感召力，恰在于它全无道德上的居高临下。你那位社会改革家永远想着改造人；救世军，就我所知，只想帮助人。这两种相反的理想，结果上有天渊之别。

===Original===
An astonishingly high standard of self-respect is maintained among these girls. Directly they earn a shilling they will spend fourpence of it in a bath. They neglect no opportunity of washing their underwear, and their clothes are carefully brushed, indeed I have often wondered how they manage to appear so spick and span. I have been in the kitchen of a lodging house and seen a girl whom I met a few days before, enter almost dead with exhaustion. She has been two nights without a bed and has staggered into the kitchen for half-an-hour’s rest before she “walks” again. Her feet are swollen and bleeding, the cheap silk stockings all in holes, but she still maintains a meticulous neatness about her small hat and her cheap coat and frock. Powder, rouge and lipstick repair the ravages of fatigue, a borrowed needle and cotton deal with the stockings and she sets forth again looking like a rose refreshed. Their clothes are of the cheapest, generally bought from the little shops in Soho, or in the East End of London. The material is not high class, cotton plays a great part in its composition, but the cut is very good, and not always supplied by the tailor. Very often the girl will alter it herself, with a companion’s help and the aid of a few pins. So much comprehension, such amazing generosity, such swift compassion; all wasted in prostitution for the want of common sense, help and understanding.

===Chinese===
这些姑娘维持着一份惊人高涨的自尊。一挣到一先令，她们就肯花四便士洗一个澡。她们不错过任何洗内衣的机会，外衣刷得仔仔细细——我常常纳闷，她们是怎么做到那副干净齐整模样的。我曾在寄宿旅舍的厨房里，看见几天前认识的 一个姑娘走进来，累得几乎半死。她两夜没有床睡，踉跄着进厨房歇半个钟头，好接着再「走」。她双脚肿胀流血，廉价的丝袜全是洞，可她那顶小帽子、那件便宜的外衣和连衣裙，依旧收拾得一丝不苟。粉、胭脂和口红修补着疲惫的痕迹，借来的针线对付了丝袜，她再度出发时，活像一朵重新沐过露的玫瑰。她们的衣裳是最便宜的那种，多从苏活区（Soho）的小铺子或伦敦东区买来。料子不高档，棉布占大头，可剪裁很好，且不全是裁缝的手笔——常常是姑娘自己改的，一个同伴搭把手，再借几枚别针。这么多的善解人意，这般惊人的大方，这般敏捷的同情心——全都因为缺少常识、帮助和理解，而白白耗掷在卖淫里。

===Original===
Furthermore, at the risk of repetition, I must state again that there is little or no drinking among this type of prostitute, and that the root cause of their condition is an economic one. It will be argued, very rightly, that a little strength of mind, a small amount of courage, would have saved them from such a plight. The domestic who has outstayed her allotted hour; the girl who has slipped out of her home; the nursery governess who has taken French leave, all these could have retrieved their position had they faced the music. But unless you have been in direct contact with the type of which I am writing, you cannot understand how difficult, if not impossible, it is for them to approach a woman of a different social caste. Embedded in their psychology is the belief that their employer will, and must, not only censure, but punish their lapse from duty, and in the main their belief is right. The average mistress, faced by her domestic after a night’s absence, or even part of a night, would inevitably tell her to go without further notice. It is to avoid this unpleasantness that the defaulting absentee does not return. She prefers to join the ranks of the destitute.

The older women, not of the prostitute class, who frequent lodging houses are semi-permanents. These semi-permanents have regular, if poorly paid work. They are office cleaners, jobbing laundresses, daily cooks at cheap restaurants. The money they pay per week for their bed would rent a room in a poor locality, but even if such accommodation were available, and they could collect the necessary furniture, there is one overwhelming obstacle to such a mode of life. For the single woman well on to middle age, to live alone is to court a desolation of spirit that saps vitality. The loneliness of such an existence is intolerable. Few of these odd women have friends, or even acquaintances; they sustain their hold on life through the younger women whom they meet at the lodging house. They feed their emotions on the emotions of these others, gaining a spurious excitement from their tragedies and amusements.

===Chinese===
再者，冒着重复的风险，我必须再申明一次：这一类妓女几乎滴酒不沾，她们境况的根因是经济性的。有人会反驳——而且说得很有道理——说只要一点点意志力、一点点勇气，就能使她们免于这般田地。超了钟点的女仆；从家里溜出来的姑娘；不辞而别的家庭女教师——这些人不计前嫌回去面对，本都可以挽回自己的位置。可是，除非你同我笔下的这一类人有过直接的接触，否则你无法理解：让她们去接近另一个社会阶层的女人，有多难，难到近乎不可能。她们心里嵌着一个信条：雇主不仅会、而且必定会申斥并惩罚她们的失职——大体上，她们的信条是对的。寻常的女主人面对彻夜或半夜才归的女仆，必然叫她即刻走人。正是为了躲开这不痛快，误了钟点的人才不回去。她宁可加入赤贫者的队伍。

常来寄宿旅舍的年长女人中不属于妓女一类的，是半长期住户。这些半长期住户有固定的工作，虽然报酬微薄。她们是办公室清洁工、零活洗衣妇、廉价餐馆的日班厨娘。她们每周付的床钱，足够在穷地段租一间屋；可即便有这样的住处、也凑得齐必要的家具，这种生活方式面前还横着一道压倒性的障碍：对一个逼近中年的单身女人来说，独居等于自招一种掏空生命力的心灵荒凉。这种存在的孤寂是人所不堪的。这些零余的女人少有朋友，甚至少有相识；她们靠着旅舍里遇到的年轻女人维系对生活的把握。她们拿别人的情感喂养自己的情感，从她们的悲剧与消遣里讨来一份虚浮的兴奋。

===Original===
It is not only in the public lodging house that you find this particular phenomenon. You will find elderly spinsters and childless widows in all grades of society preying on the vitality of the young. Lack of occupation, in these latter cases, intensifies the desire for sensation and, whereas the office cleaner is content to observe her younger and attractive sisters, the middle class woman of certain, if small income, with nothing to do, actively oppresses her unfortunate friends and relations. The same morbid condition is noticeable in widows who have lost their husbands and sons and with them that sense of superiority which comes from male appreciation. In such cases it is the daughters who pay; for the mother, to sustain emotional contact with life, leaves very little opportunity for their privacy of feeling. But among outcasts you find a bond of fellowship almost as close and as elastic as the comradeship of man with man. They have that respect for liberty of action and privacy of thought which among women of ordered and leisured lives is rarely met with. They are void of that desire of possession which mars so many friendships. They desire to possess their own lives, they shrink from the responsibility of possessing their neighbours. So keen is this appreciation of spiritual aloofness, that it is hopeless for anybody to attempt to offer them material assistance, who does not share that same passion. Until this underlying fact is recognised, all attempts at what is known as rescue work will be unavailing.

The third class of destitute—the itinerant match-vendors—rarely come to the one and twopenny type of lodging house. They simply have not the means. They go to the unlicensed doss house, which still secretly flourishes in the backways of the city. They go also to a huge shelter in Whitechapel, concerning which I shall have much to say. But be they prostitute, office cleaner, or matchseller—whether they pay a few pence or a larger sum—they all suffer from the same crying and shameful injustice; the inadequacy of accommodation, the lack of proper bathrooms, the glaring inequality which supplies the outcast male with the decencies of life and denies them to women.

===Chinese===
这一特殊现象，并非公共寄宿旅舍所独有。在社会的各个阶层，你都能找到攫取年轻生命活力的老处女和无子的寡妇。在后面这些情形里，无所事事加剧了对刺激的渴求——办公室清洁工满足于旁观她年轻貌美的姐妹们，而那位有一点（哪怕不多）收入、又无所事事的中产女人，则主动地作践她那些不幸的朋友亲戚。同一种病态也见于失去了丈夫和儿子的寡妇——随之失去的，还有来自男性欣赏的那份优越感。这类情形里，付出代价的是女儿们：母亲为了维系与生活的情感接触，几乎不给她们的情感留一点私人的余地。但在落魄者中间，你会发现一种几乎同男人与男人之间的同志情谊一样紧密、一样有弹性的情谊。她们对行动自由与思想私域的尊重，在那些生活井然、优游的女人们中间是罕见的。她们没有那种败坏诸多友谊的占有欲。她们想占有的是自己的生活，却畏避占有邻人的责任。这种对精神独立的领会如此深切，以至于谁若不怀有同样的热忱，想向她们提供物质援助都是徒劳。这个底层的事实一日不被认识，一切所谓救援工作的尝试都将是白费。

第三类赤贫者——流动的卖火柴女——极少光顾一先令两便士这一档的寄宿旅舍。她们根本没有这个财力。她们去的是无照经营的宿夜所——这东西仍在城市的背巷里悄悄兴旺。她们也去白教堂一家巨大的收容所，关于它我有许多话要说。但不论她们是妓女、清洁工还是卖火柴的——不论付的是几个便士还是更大的数目——她们都承受着同样喧嚣而可耻的不公：住处的短缺，像样浴室的匮乏，还有那刺眼的双重标准——它把体面生活的设施供给落魄的男人，却拒绝给女人。

## VI The Black Plush Coat / 六 黑长毛绒大衣

===Original===
When I left the lodging house in Kennedy Court I had no money, and my sole stock-in-trade consisted of the two boxes of matches, which, soaked by the last night’s rain, were now dry. I got them off my hands at twopence each; it was too early in the day to get a better price﻿—men grow more generous as the evening approaches!

I craved for a cup of coffee, but I was stern with myself; I would not run the risk of spending half my capital. I therefore bought four more boxes of matches, and determined to effect a speedy sale. I was not content, however, to accept my failure to get employment as a cook as final. Good cooks are always in demand and, as I have said before﻿—l am an extremely good cook. I regard the production of a well-thought-out repast as a piece of creative work every bit as important as a chapter in a novel, let alone a triolet. Why, therefore, should I be debarred from practising my vocation? Surely this was an occasion when personality should be able to surmount the difficulties arising from the absence of what is known as a “character?”
===Chinese===
离开肯尼迪大院的寄宿旅舍时，我身无分文，全部家当就是那两盒火柴——昨夜被雨浸透，如今已经干了。我以每盒两便士的价钱脱了手；这一天还太早，卖不出更好的价钱——天色越晚，男人们越大方！

我渴想一杯咖啡，但我对自己毫不容情；我不肯冒险花掉一半本钱。于是我又买进四盒火柴，打算速速脱手。可我不甘心把当厨娘求职的失败当作定局。好厨子总是有人要的，而且如我从前所说——我是个顶好的厨子。在我看来，操办一席深思熟虑的饭菜，是一桩创造性的工作，同小说里的一章同样要紧，更不必说一首三节叠韵的小诗（triolet）了。那么，凭什么不许我操持本行？照理说，这该是个人风范足以压倒「品行证明书」（character）之缺所带来的困难的时刻了。

===Original===
I sold my matches well that day. I got fourpence for two and sixpence for two, and I bought myself a hunch of bread and cheese and a cup of coffee at a Lockhart’s. The food is very good in these establishments, much better and cheaper than at far more imposing places. It is a question, as Bernard Shaw said years ago, of whether you prefer nine-pennyworth of tablecloth and three-pennyworth of food for your shilling, or vice versa. The service is negligible at Lockhart’s, and the table implements are not at all refined, but your food and drink are both steaming hot, and the company has a rough and ready humour which gives salt to the poorest meal.

I had a conversation with the manageress as to the chances of engaging me as cook. There might, she thought, be a vacancy for a washer-up, but in the higher walks of domestic economy there was nothing doing; it was a matter of a reference once again.

It is a nice point﻿—this discrimination between the washing of dishes and their preparation. In the scullery, it would seem, absence of character does not matter: it is, I suppose, difficult to abstract a soup tureen or conceal a dish. But when it comes to the roasting of joints or the boiling of potatoes, custom steps in with a rigid hand. Alas! there was no admission for me into anybody’s kitchen, save as a hewer of wood or a drawer of water, and both these pursuits were too poorly paid for me to consider; I always went back to the match trade.
===Chinese===
那天我的火柴卖得不错。两盒得了四便士，另两盒得了六便士，我在洛克哈特（Lockhart's）给自己买了一块面包夹干酪、一杯咖啡。这类馆子的吃食很好，比许多排场大得多的地方又好又便宜。这正如萧伯纳多年前说过的：你出一先令，究竟是愿要九便士的桌布加三便士的饭食，还是反过来。洛克哈特谈不上什么伺候，餐具也毫不精细，可你的吃喝都是滚热的，同座的人有种粗率的幽默，给最寒酸的饭也添上滋味。

我同女经理谈过受雇为厨娘的机会。她想，兴许有个洗盘子的空缺；至于家政经济的高级行当，则毫无办法——又是品行证明书作梗。

这是个微妙的界线——洗碟子与做菜之间的这道分野。在洗碗间里，没有品行证明书似乎无妨：我想，一只汤盆怕是难以顺走，一只盘子也难以藏匿。可一旦轮到烤肉、煮土豆，惯例便伸出一只僵硬的手。唉！谁的厨房都容不下我，除非去做砍柴挑水的粗活，而这两样报酬都太薄，不值得我考虑；我总是又回到火柴这行当上来。

===Original===
I thought it probable that I might find less rigid requirements in the foreign quarters of Soho, where little restaurants jostle small cafés in every street. In the course of my pilgrimage I visited many kitchens where succulent repasts are prepared. Dark underground places, where the temperature is like that of a hothouse and the air vibrates with dramatic directions in the French and Italian tongues. Here again I could have got work as a vegetable maid at ninepence an hour, but the employment would have been irregular, and, as I early experienced, I could make more than that in the fine art of matchselling.

I had already discovered that in Soho you can buy matches by the dozen at reduced rates, but I decided to continue my own methods. I felt that if I laid in a larger stock-in-trade my market would slump, for whether I concealed them about my person or brandished them in the face of heaven, the fact that I had matches in reserve would affect the psychology of the buyer. As a matter of form I called at various Labour Exchanges and registered as a cook. It was a hopeless quest, but I wanted to make sure of my facts. I could, I suppose, have got a situation had I written Annie Turner a reference in my own name. But that would have been loading the dice, and I wanted to go through my adventures on the level. Therefore, I went around characterless, and found every door shut. It is a curious proof of the distrust of their own judgment that neither the officials nor the individuals to whom I applied could persuade themselves that I was not a thief. You will often hear the expression that character is written in the face, but it is only the very few who believe in the value of such testimony. For this reason I was slowly but surely being forced into the permanent calling of street vendor, from which, only by the merest chance, I found an escape.

With what was left after my meal I replenished my stock of matches, and once again made a good harvest. In possession of three shillings, I decided to try an experiment. I would see what could be done in the bar of a public house. In the majority of West End bars, street sellers are not allowed: the advent of a woman with flowers, bootlaces, or any other trifle, always causes a hubbub. The barmaids shout at her, the commissionaire hustles her, the poor thing might be a walking pestilence to judge by the disturbance. This is not a sex question, however; the male itinerant vendor is equally taboo. The objection does not come from the customers, but from the proprietary, and I have never been able to understand the psychological reaction.
===Chinese===
我心想，在苏活区（Soho）的外国人聚居地，要求兴许没那么死板——那里的每条街上，小饭馆与咖啡馆挤挤挨挨。一路寻访下来，我进过许多烹制可口饭菜的厨房：幽暗的地下室，温度如暖房，空气里震颤着法语意语的戏剧性吆喝。在这里我本可以当个洗菜女工，九便士一个钟头，可那活计时有时无；而且我早早领教过，卖火柴这门精艺挣得比它多。

我已经发现，在苏活区火柴可以按打贱价买进，但我决定仍用自己的法子。我觉得倘若囤上一大笔存货，行情就要跌——因为不论我把它们藏在身上，还是朝着老天爷挥舞，手里存着火柴这一事实，总会影响买主的心理。为走个形式，我也到过几家劳工介绍所登记为厨娘。那是徒劳的寻访，但我要把事实坐实。我原可以以自己的本名为安妮·特纳写一封推荐信，谋得一个差事。可那样等于给骰子灌铅，我要凭本分走完这趟历险。于是我便「无证一身轻」地四处碰壁，发现每扇门都关着。还有一个古怪的明证，可见人们何等不信任自己的判断：不论官家的还是私家的主事人，都无法说服自己相信我不是贼。「品行写在脸上」这话你常会听见，可真信这证词的没有几人。就这样，我被缓缓地、然而确凿地逼进了街头小贩这个长久的行当——幸而机缘凑巧，我才从中脱身。

用饭剩下的钱我又进了一批火柴，又做成了好几笔好买卖。手握三先令，我决定试一个实验：看看酒馆的吧台边能有什么作为。西区的酒馆吧台多半不许街头卖货：一个女人捧着花、鞋带或别的什么零碎一露面，总要引起一场骚动。酒吧女冲她嚷，门房推搡她，瞧那阵仗，这可怜人简直是一头行走的瘟疫。不过这倒不是性别问题；男性的流动贩子同样犯忌。阻拦不是来自客人，而是来自店家，这套心理反应我始终没能参透。

===Original===
There are, however, still a few Christian public houses off Shaftesbury Avenue where street vendors are admitted. I had spotted one of these, and about eight o’clock that evening I decided to stop active business, to buy myself a drink and to look round. The bar I chose was one of those cosy places where you sit on a high stool, close to the counter, which is flanked by a buffet, groaning with good things in the cheese and biscuit line, cold beef, ham and pickles, tomatoes and French mustard. I bought myself a glass of port, biscuit and cheese, borrowed an evening paper and waited events.

Presently a bunch of men came in, all in good spirits, after a day’s racing. Racing men are always kindly and most human, and I felt I was in for a good sale. I bided my time, and, when one of them started fumbling for a pipe, I intrigued the inevitable box towards him.

“Times are hard,” I said, with a sweet smile.

My victim, a tall, bearded creature with blue eyes, gave a sympathetic grin.
===Chinese===
不过，沙夫茨伯里大道（Shaftesbury Avenue）岔出去的街上，还有几家宅心仁厚的酒馆容得下街头小贩。我早已相中了其中一家。当晚八点来钟，我决定收了正经营生，给自己买杯酒，四下观望。我挑的这家是那种舒坦的铺子：你坐在高凳上，紧挨着柜台，旁边一列冷餐柜，摆满了干酪饼干类的好东西——冷牛肉、火腿、酸黄瓜、番茄和法式芥末。我给自己要了一杯波特酒、饼干和干酪，借了份晚报，坐等事态。

不多时进来一伙男人，赛马日刚散，个个兴致高昂。赛马人总是和气而最有人情味的，我自觉要做成一批好买卖。我按兵不动，待到其中一位摸索起烟斗，我便把那必不可少的盒子递了过去。

「世道艰难啊。」我甜甜一笑说。

我的猎物——一个高个子、蓝眼睛、蓄须的男人——报以体谅的咧嘴一笑。

===Original===
“That’s all right, my dear,” he said, and handed me a shilling.

Two of his friends followed suit to the same tune, and I was so pleased that I bought myself another port and some more biscuits.

“You’re doing well, miss,” said the barman. (There are no barmaids in this establishment). “Here, I’ll take a box. Will fourpence suit you?”

I gathered up the money with a rising pulse. For the first time I felt the joy of buying in the cheapest market and selling in the dearest. I began to think there was a great deal in finance. Presently, a man came in selling pocket glasses and powder-puffs. I was not in a position to buy anything, so he waited till a prosperous looking man arrived, and landed him for ninepence. Meanwhile, an elderly woman seated at the other end of the counter left her stool and came towards me.

She was dressed in one of those black, plush coats which seem to go on for ages. Nothing is able to destroy them; fifty years after they are made they still flourish in undiminished vigour, though time depresses their one time glossy look. She wore a battered straw hat with a droopy feather, her complexion was leaden, but her eyes, were large and intelligent.
===Chinese===
「不打紧，亲爱的。」他说着递给我一先令。

他的两个朋友照此办理，一样的价钱；我高兴之余，又给自己要了一杯波特酒和几块饼干。

「你生意不坏呀，小姐。」酒吧男侍说（这家铺子里没有酒吧女）。「来，我也拿一盒。四便士成不成？」

我收起钱来，脉搏直往上撞。头一回，我尝到了贱市买进、贵市卖出的快乐。我开始觉得金融这门学问大有讲究。不多会儿，一个卖袖珍眼镜和粉扑的男人进来。我无钱进货，他便等着，直到一位体面模样的先生驾到，九便士做成了一单。这时，坐在柜台另一头的一个上了年纪的女人下了高凳，朝我走来。

她穿着一件那种黑长毛绒大衣，这类衣服仿佛能穿到地老天荒。什么也毁不了它们；做成五十年后，它们依旧精力不衰地兴旺着，只是岁月磨去了当年那层光亮。她戴一顶瘪塌的草帽，插一根蔫羽毛，脸色灰败如铅，可那双眼睛又大又聪慧。

===Original===
“You’ve done rather brightly with your matches,” said she.

“Yes,” I answered, “much better than in the street.”

“You’re clever at selling,” she went on, and I could feel her studying me closely. “Will you have a drink?” she asked, and, nothing daunted, I accepted yet another port and further biscuits.

You never refuse anything when you are destitute. It is a mistake not to eat because you are not hungry. As a wise woman once observed to me, “You may want it later.”

“I’ve been watching you,” she went on, sipping her port, “and it occurs to me that we might do business together.”

Here indeed was an adventure! I felt myself on the threshold of a great discovery. What sort of business could the lady of the plush coat have with me?
===Chinese===
「你火柴卖得挺灵光。」她说。

「是啊，」我答，「比在街上强多了。」

「你挺会卖，」她接着说。我能觉出她在细细打量我。「喝一杯吗？」她问。我毫无惧色，又领了一杯波特酒和几块饼干。

落魄的时候，人家给什么你都别推辞。因为不饿就不吃，是个错误。正如一位有见识的女人从前对我说的：「回头你兴许用得着。」

「我一直在瞧着你，」她呷着波特酒往下说，「我忽然想到，咱们兴许可以合伙做点买卖。」

这可真是一桩奇遇！我自觉正站在一项重大发现的门槛上。这位长毛绒大衣的夫人，能同我做什么买卖呢？

===Original===
“You might sell on commission. I’d offer you quite good terms, and you’d find a ready market for the goods.”

She produced a brown paper parcel and showed me a collection of cigarette cases, matchboxes, pencil cases, all of them very neatly made in attractive-looking metal, brightly burnished. I discovered that this class of goods, made in Germany, can be bought wholesale at a very low figure.

“I’ll give you twenty-five percent commission on everything you sell according to my listed price, and fifty percent of all that you get over. You ought to get a good deal over,” she added, “as I say, you can sell well.”

I did a rapid mental calculation, and discovered that twenty-five percent meant threepence in the shilling. Most of the articles were marked at one and three to one and six, with sixpence for the pencil cases. With any degree of good fortune I ought to make more at this than at matches.
===Chinese===
「你可以做代销。我出的条件相当不错，货也现成有销路。」

她掏出一个牛皮纸包，给我看一批烟盒、火柴盒、铅笔盒，件件做工干净利落，金属胚子看着喜人，擦得锃亮。我这才知道，这类德国制造的货色，批发价贱得很。

「按我的标价卖出，统统给你两成五的佣金；超出标价的部分，对半分。你准能卖出不少超出价，」她补一句，「我说过，你是块卖货的料。」

我心里飞快一算：两成五就是每先令三便士。这些东西多标价一先令三便士到一先令六便士，铅笔盒六便士。只要运气不太坏，干这个该比卖火柴挣得多。

===Original===
“It won’t be any good for you to offer these in the street, my dear; you’ll have to try public houses, and I can’t give you any tips what to do; you seem to have got the tricks of the trade already. You haven’t been at the business long,” she added.

“Not very,” I answered, but I didn’t tell her anything more, and she didn’t ask me. “Meet me tomorrow, outside here at half-past one, and I’ll give you a consignment of goods. If I’m not outside, look for me in the bar.”

I stared at her, a little astonished. “Will you want any deposit?” I asked.

“Oh no,” she said. “I don’t do business that way. I chance my luck﻿—and it rarely fails me.”

Subsequently that woman handed over to my care a good few shillings worth of value. And she did not know me from Adam, and quite probably I might have come straight from gaol. But the homeless have their own fashion of determining who can and who can not be trusted. The lady of the plush coat had reckoned me up, and decided I was on the right side. Up to this point﻿—though her appearance was anything but prosperous﻿—I had decided that she was a person with a habitation, if only a top back room. The goods she carried about with her were worth ten shillings, and the fact that she was able to buy drinks without showing any anxiety, seemed to suggest she was used to, at any rate, small means. I still think my impression of her was correct; but it came out that she had no habitation, not even the poorest apology for a home. She turned to me quite casually and asked where I was going to sleep that night?
===Chinese===
「在街上兜售这些可不成，亲爱的；你得跑酒馆。该怎么做我就不指点你了——看样子，这行的门道你早摸着了。你干这行不久吧。」她添了一句。

「不久。」我答，别的话一句没多说，她也没问。「明天一点半，就在这门外碰头，我给你一批货。我要不在门外，就到吧台里找我。」

我盯着她，有几分讶异。「要押金吗？」我问。

「哦，不要，」她说，「我不兴那套做买卖的规矩。我赌我的运气——而运气很少辜负我。」

后来，那女人果真把价值好几先令的货交到了我手里。她与我素不相识，而我完全可能刚从牢里出来。但无家可归者自有他们的一套法子，断定谁可信、谁不可信。长毛绒大衣的夫人把我掂量过了，断定我站在好的一边。直到这时——尽管她的外表怎么也说不上宽裕——我一直认定她是有个住处的，哪怕只是顶楼一间后屋。她随身带的货值十先令，她买酒时又不见一点拮据之态，似乎说明她惯于至少有一点小小的进项。我至今认为我的印象不错；可后来才知道，她根本没有住处，连一个勉强算得上家的窝也没有。她忽然随口问我，那一夜打算睡哪儿？

===Original===
“I don’t know,” I said. “I have some idea of going up to Camden Town.”

“There’s no need to spend all that money,” she protested. “Come with me, and I’ll get you put up for a few pence. I know a place where they charge fourpence, but as you’re a friend of mine they will take you for half.”

It didn’t sound alluring, but I refused to be fainthearted. I was going to follow the stream and chance where it took me, even though it found its way into strange places. I told her I was much obliged, and would willingly go with her, and after a little more chat we left the bar and made our way across Piccadilly Circus, down the Embankment and across Waterloo Bridge.

She did not speak all the way. The habit of walking engenders silence; you get half doped with the unceasing exercise and have little energy for speech. I was frankly curious as to my companion’s history. But I never learned very much about her. She must have made enough to keep herself by selling her goods, but she belonged to no type of outcast in any settled place.

Some few are born like that﻿—the inheritance, perhaps, of some far off gypsy blood﻿—others acquire the fear of four walls and a roof, and no amount of suffering can dissipate it. My friend, I think, belonged to the latter category. I fancy that she must have spent some time in prison, for she had the habit of looking behind her with a sudden, furtive movement as though she expected the hand of the law to close upon her arm. There was also another reason for this apprehension. As I found later, she took drugs, and it may be that she added to her earnings by the sale of dope. It was a curious experience, that walk. I was sensitive to the atmosphere of mystery which surrounded her, and though we did not talk, we were not conscious of our silence.

She was obviously a woman of good mentality. Her speech was smooth, and her accent had a touch of distinction. I often look for my friend of the plush coat in or around Shaftesbury Avenue when I revisit the scenes of my adventures, but I have never caught a glimpse of her. Nor is this strange. Like moves to like, outcast gravitates to outcast, and until once more I strip myself of my comfortable home, and step across the boundary which divides security from starvation, I shall not look on her again.
===Chinese===
「不知道，」我说，「有点想上卡姆登镇去。」

「犯不着花那笔钱，」她拦住我，「跟我来，我让你几个便士就能安顿。我知道一个地方，收四便士；你是我的朋友，只收你一半。」

听着并不诱人，可我不肯气馁。我打算随波逐流，任它把我带去哪里，哪怕流进荒僻的去处。我说承她的情，愿意同去；又闲谈几句，我们便离了酒馆，穿过皮卡迪利广场，沿着河滨大道，过了滑铁卢桥。

一路上她一言不发。走路这习惯养得出沉默：你在不停的迈步里半入迷障，没有余力说话。我坦白说好奇我这位同伴的来历。可我对她始终知之甚少。她卖货想必挣得够自己糊口，但她不属于任何固定地盘上的落魄者类型。

有些人生来如此——也许是某种遥远吉普赛血统的遗传；另一些人是后天才染上对四壁一顶的恐惧，再多的苦难也驱不散它。我的朋友，我想，属于后一类。我揣想她坐过一阵牢，因为她有个习惯，会突然鬼鬼祟祟地回头张望，仿佛料着法律之手随时会扣上她的胳膊。这份惊惶还有另一个缘故。我后来发现她吸毒，兴许还兼做贩毒的生意贴补收入。那一路走得实在古怪。我对笼罩着她的神秘气氛很敏感；我们虽不交谈，却不觉得沉默的存在。

她显然是个心智健全的女人。谈吐流利，口音里带一点上流的意味。后来我重访旧地时，常在沙夫茨伯里大道一带寻找我这位长毛绒大衣的朋友，却再也没瞥见过她。这也不奇怪。同类聚同类，落魄者引力落魄者；除非我再脱下舒适的家，跨过那条分隔温饱与饥饿的界线，我是再见不着她了。

===Original===
We crossed Waterloo Bridge and turned towards the New Cut. I always feel a curious spiritual depression when I am on the south side of the river. Progress a little farther and the raucous vulgarity of Brixton, the muffled seriousness of Streatham, make their own atmosphere, but the immediate effect when you cross any of the bridges is as potent as anything you can experience in Bethnal Green or in Haggerston, that place of the distressed.

My spirits were not enlivened when we turned the corner of a narrow and evil-smelling alley. I kept close to my guide; for a moment my heart failed me, and I had a terrible temptation to run away. It was not so much the darkness, the feeling that the alley was ill-lit or even the sound of furtive feet that flitted about us; it was the close and heavy smell that emerged from the open doorways; that strong, sickly, acrid odour that emanates from humanity packed tightly close. It came with a big blast, that odour, when at last we stopped at an open door, and my guide knocked three times.

An old woman, with whom my companion exchanged a few words, shuffled along the passage. She bade me hand over twopence, and I followed her into a low-ceilinged room, dirty and dingy, and crowded with women, old, elderly and middle-aged, with one or two young things as the exception. A coke fire burnt in the grate, and the air was warm and horribly close. The kitchen at Kennedy Court was a palace compared to it; the benches were broken down, and many of the women sat on the floor, clasping evil-smelling bundles.

Here were no prostitutes, no office cleaners. The women were street vendors in the poorer parts; for in this walk of life, as in others, there are lights and shades among the ragged and forlorn, and few of the lodgers gathered together could have appeared at Piccadilly Circus or in Shaftesbury Avenue without being run in. There was one flower woman, young, among them. She did not belong to the noble army of flower girls with their admirable organisation and sturdy trade union principles. She had no recognised pitch, but made her pence by appeal to the charitable, who, judging by her hungry look, did not wholeheartedly respond. Like the rest, her feet were tragically ill-shod, in large and shapeless boots, originally meant for a man, held together by string instead of laces, with a hiatus between the upper and the sole, through which there showed a grimy foot. She wore one of those strange garments that go by the name of ulster; they appear to come into the world in a state of malnutrition and decay.
===Chinese===
过了滑铁卢桥，我们折向「新街市」（the New Cut）。一到了泰晤士河南岸，我总感到一种说不出的精神抑郁。再往前行，布里克斯顿的喧闹俗艳、斯特里汉姆的闷声严肃，各自酿成一种气氛；但你一过桥，那当面的感受，同你在贝思纳尔格林或哈格斯顿——那个困苦之乡——能领受的一样强烈。

拐进一条狭窄臭秽的小巷时，我的情绪更低了。我紧跟住领路人；有一刻我心一沉，受到逃跑的猛烈诱惑。倒不全是黑暗，不全是巷子灯光昏暗，甚至也不全是四周窸窣闪避的脚步声；而是那些敞开的门洞里涌出的又闷又重的气味——人紧紧挤作一团时散发出的那种浓烈、甜腻、刺鼻的臭气。最后我们在一扇敞开的门前停住，领路人敲了三下，那气味便劈头盖脸扑过来。

一个老妇人——我同伴同她交谈了几句——拖着脚步沿过道走去。她叫我交出两便士，我随她进了一间天花板低矮的屋子，又脏又暗，挤满了女人：老的、上年纪的、中年的，偶尔一两个年轻的例外。炉格里烧着焦炭火，空气暖得可怕地闷。肯尼迪大院的厨房比起它来简直是宫殿；长凳残破，许多女人就坐在地上，搂着一件件恶臭的包袱。

这里没有妓女，也没有办公室清洁工。这些女人是穷街陋巷里的街头贩子；因为在这一行里，也同别的行当一样，褴褛孤苦者中也有高低贵贱，聚在这里的房客，多数要是敢在皮卡迪利广场或沙夫茨伯里大道露面，准会被抓走。内中有一个卖花的年轻女人。她并不属于那支可敬的卖花姑娘大军——那有着出色的组织和结实行会原则的队伍。她没有固定的摊位，靠哀求善心人挣几个便士；看那饥饿的面色，善心人也响应得并不踊跃。同大伙一样，她脚上的鞋惨不忍睹：又大又走形的大靴子，原本是给男人做的，用绳子而不是鞋带束着，鞋帮鞋底之间裂着口子，露出一只乌黑的脚。她身上披着那种名唤「乌尔斯特」（ulster）的怪衣服；这类东西仿佛一降生到世上就是营养不良、未老先衰的模样。

===Original===
Some “lodgers” wore wisps of sad-looking fur﻿—“perfectly good mog,” as a cheery matchseller described it. I don’t know the origin of the name, but to me, it is an admirable synonym for an article of adornment highly cherished by the most destitute among my sex.

Apart from the flower-seller and a consumptive-looking girl who worked in a slop shop, i.e., a cheap tailor’s, my fellow lodgers were seasoned veterans. A few took off their hats, if you can so describe the battered objects perched on their matted hair, the rest retained them closely, fearful, I suppose, of losing a vital part of their possessions. Their faces were not clean, though I am sure before their mode of life shifted their perspective, they would have welcomed a wash.

There was very little chatter. Mostly they sat quiet, in an apathy of rest. Not that their faces expressed vacancy, but their minds were elsewhere. As I discovered, the eternal walking about erects a barrier between you and material things, and it is only the sharper and more primal needs of the body which arouse the active consciousness. My friend of the plush coat had disappeared, and presently I felt I would like to follow her. The lack of air, the smouldering smell of stale humanity affected me with a physical nausea, the like of which I had not before known.

I found the decrepit female in the passage outside﻿—I could write a whole chapter on the psychology of these aged doorkeepers﻿—and asked her for a bed. She pointed up a rickety flight of stairs lit by a faint gas-burner. The walls, originally painted brown, were black with age; the window on the landing, draped with torn curtains of Nottingham lace, had not been cleaned for generations. Up yet another flight, across a creaking landing, and into a large room filled with truckle beds.
===Chinese===
有些「房客」身上挂着几绺愁惨的毛皮——「顶好的猫皮」（mog），一位快活的卖火柴女这样说。这名称出处何在我不知道，但在我看来，它是我同性中最赤贫的人们珍爱的一种饰物的绝妙代名词。

除却卖花女和一个在血汗裁缝铺（slop shop，即廉价成衣铺）做工的痨病相姑娘，我的同宿者们都是沙场老将。有几个摘了帽子——若「帽子」二字当得起那顶扣在毡结头发上的破烂玩意的话——其余的都紧紧捂着不放，我想是怕失去这份命根子家当。她们的脸不干净，尽管我敢说，在生活方式扭转了她们的眼界之前，她们原是很乐意洗一洗的。

屋里很少有交谈。多半静坐着，处于一种休息的麻木里。倒不是脸上空无一物，而是心思在别处。照我发现的那样，无休止的步行在你与物质世界之间竖起一道屏障，只有身体更尖锐、更原始的需求才能唤起活跃的意识。我那位长毛绒大衣的朋友已不知去向，不多时我便想追随她而去。空气的稀薄、陈年人味闷烧的臭气，使我泛起一种生理上的恶心，此前从未领教过。

我在过道里找到那个老朽的女人——这些年老门房的心理，我足可写上一整章——向她要一张床。她指了指一道摇摇欲坠的楼梯，一盏昏暗的煤气灯照着。墙壁原是棕色的漆，年深日久已成黑色；楼梯平台上的窗，挂着诺丁汉花边的破窗帘，几辈子没擦过。再上一道楼梯，穿过吱呀作响的平台，进了一间塞满矮轮床的大屋。

===Original===
The air was impossible. I tried the next room on the same floor. It was smaller, and the window was open at the top. There was a vacant bed just underneath it, and, thankful at the chance of better atmosphere, I decided it should be mine.

I did not dare to look at the sheets, I felt somehow they were alive; indeed, it needed a very definite act of will before I could induce myself to take off my raincoat and my costume. I put on my nightdress over the rest of my clothes, hoping they would serve as protection against the invading army of insects which I was sure lurked in the sodden palliasses and unsavoury blankets. This, however, was an affliction from which I was spared. Generally speaking, your lodging house and doss house is free from lice. The lodgers, many of them, are fruitfully verminous, but the strays, which they leave behind them, are dealt according to those preparations discovered during the war, so that though the shakedowns in Waterloo Bridge Road are horribly dirty, they are free from vermin, being daily sprayed with strong chemicals.

This I did not know until the next morning, and apprehension of crawlers and an uncanny sense of spiritual discomfort kept me awake. Now the senses of the outcast, as I discovered, grow preternaturally acute, and though I had been on the streets only a few days, I had already learned to feel not only the approach of physical danger, but the proximity of evil. I felt evil was near me, moving towards me from the nearest bed. There was no light in the room, only the pale sinister grey of the South London sky, which seemed to distort the features of the face on the flock pillow next to mine into something almost inhuman.

I did not feel drowsy, but if slumber had approached me I must have fought it off. I had put my bag with my day’s things under the pillow, and I clasped it quickly as I lay, uncomfortable in mind, body and estate. Bad nights, however, were telling on me, and towards dawn I suppose I must have dropped off. I do not think I can have slept more than a few minutes, for I awoke with a start, my heart thumping, as it always does in moments of stress. The bed next to me was empty, and﻿—I knew it﻿—I felt it﻿—my bag had gone﻿ ﻿…
===Chinese===
屋里的空气没法待。我试了同层的隔壁一间。小些，窗子上头开着。窗下正好空着一张床，我为这口好空气的机会谢天谢地，决定就要它了。

我不敢看那些床单，总觉得它们是活物；实在要下一番狠决心，才说服自己脱下雨衣和套装。我把睡衣罩在其余衣裳外面穿，指望它们抵挡那支入侵的虫子大军——我确信它们就潜伏在湿透的草垫和气味可疑的毯子里。不过这场灾难我倒幸免了。一般说来，寄宿旅舍和宿夜所是没有虱子的。房客里有许多人虱子成群，但他们留下的散兵游勇，都用大战期间发明的药剂对付过了；所以滑铁卢桥路上的那些地铺虽脏得吓人，却没有虫，每天拿烈性药水喷洒。

这是我到第二天早晨才知道的；对爬虫的忧惧，加上一种说不出的精神不适，使我彻夜无眠。落魄者的感官，照我的发现，会变得超乎寻常地敏锐；我上街才几天，却已学会不仅觉察人身危险的临近，还觉察邪恶的贴近。我觉得邪恶就在近旁，从最近那张床上朝我挪来。屋里没有灯，只有伦敦南区天空那惨淡阴险的灰色，它似乎把邻枕上那张脸扭曲得近乎非人。

我不困，可就算睡意来了我也必得把它挡回去。我把装着全天家当的提包塞在枕头下，躺着的时候紧紧攥住它，心、身、境况三样都不安生。然而坏一夜接坏一夜，终究拖垮了我，将近黎明我想必是迷糊了过去。我想我统共睡了没有几分钟，因为我猛地惊醒，心咚咚直撞——每逢紧张它总这样。邻床空了，而且——我知道，我感到——我的提包没了……

===Original===
Now an unfamiliar sensation, something you have never quite experienced before is a psychological landmark. When I sat up and realized that the whole of my day’s takings, the money that I had so proudly earned by the sale of matches, plus the development of a special technique, had left me, I was gripped by actual terror. Not physical terror, which we must all of us have known, but a terror of the future, the material future that never before had touched me. I suppose the life I had been leading, one of hand-to-mouth destitution, had tangibly affected me, for I forget that behind all the hunger and anxiety my own home was waiting for me, and remembered only that I had to begin that day without a penny, and that before nightfall I must get a bed or walk the streets.

The terror slipped like a shadow when I got out of bed, and I became very angry. I could have forgiven the woman if she had taken half or nearly all the money; she might have left me the price of a cup of tea﻿—and she should not have taken my bag. I dressed myself with furious haste, and went in search of my friend of the night before. Emotion always changes one’s perspective, and whereas the previous evening I shrank from inspecting the large room next to where I had passed the night, that morning I marched in, indifferent to the smells, the suffocating reek which met me, and peered at every truckle bed in turn, until I found my lady embedded in her black plush coat.

She was past all rousing. Heavily drugged, she had gone over the border line to the world of the imagination, and the house might have been a fire, or murder committed straight in front of her, and she would not have known. I left her, and made my way back through the room, across the landing, down the flight of rickety stairs, and so out into the alley.

Some of the lodgers were already astir, searching among strange and unsavoury bundles in the dim light. Unlike Kennedy Court, most of the sojourners brought their bundles, which seemed to consist very largely of horrid looking skins﻿—“mog,” I suppose, in process of transfiguration to a higher plane. The skins of rabbit, cat and even dog, looked creepy, and I suspected insects and hurried past, with fearful feet.
===Chinese===
一种从未经历过的感觉，一件你从来不曾真正体验过的事，是心理上的一座界碑。当我坐起身，意识到一整天的进项、我靠卖火柴如此自豪地挣来的钱，连同那套独门技艺，全都没了的时候，一阵实实在在的恐怖攫住了我。不是肉体的恐怖——那种恐怖我们人人领教过——而是对未来的恐怖，对从前从未触及过我的那个物质前程的恐怖。我想，这种吃了上顿没下顿的赤贫生活已实实在在地影响了我：我竟忘了在一切饥愁的后面，我自己的家正等着我，只记得这一天我得一文不名地从头开始，天黑之前必须弄到一张床，否则就得走街。

下床时，那恐怖如影子般滑落，我随即怒不可遏。她若只拿走一半、或几乎全部的钱，我都可以原谅她；她本可以给我留一杯茶的价钱——她不该拿走我的提包。我火速穿好衣裳，去找昨夜那位朋友。情绪总会改变人的视角：前一晚我还畏缩着不敢细看隔夜屋隔壁那间大屋，那天早晨我却径直闯了进去，对气味、对扑面而来的窒息恶臭毫不在乎，逐张矮轮床端详过去，直到找到我那位夫人——裹在她的黑长毛绒大衣里。

她是万万叫不醒了。药下得重，她已越过界线去了想象世界；哪怕房子着了火，或者凶案就在她眼前发生，她也一无所知。我丢下她，穿出屋子，过了平台，下那道摇摇欲坠的楼梯，出到巷子里。

有些房客已经起身，在昏光里翻检各自古怪而气味可疑的包袱。同肯尼迪大院不同，这里的住客多半自带包袱，里面似乎装的大半是狰狞的皮子——想必是正在升华为高一等境界的「猫皮」吧。兔子皮、猫皮、甚至狗皮，看着瘆人；我疑心有虫，脚下发慌地赶紧走过。

===Original===
It was a relief to be back in the open air once more, though the alley was by no means fragrant, forlorn and dilapidated dustbins obtruded their unseemly presence on the path side. I walked off my anger along the Waterloo Bridge Road, and so over the bridge and along the Strand. By this time the business of the day was astir, and over the bridges were coming those fragile, pretty creatures that London breeds by hundreds, nay, thousands; delicate daughters of the suburbs on the way to those offices where, victims of a white slave traffic in the commercial sense, they tap typewriters for seven or eight hours a day.

I walked leisurely up to Holborn, and then, suddenly, just after nine o’clock, a rage of hunger fell upon me. I was gripped by the desire for food so fiercely that I felt I must scream in anger that I was baulked of a meal. Then I understood how it happened that starving women and men should suddenly break windows and throw stones. It is the result of an extreme want of nourishment, an active and impelling craving for hot drink, fresh bread and, if you are driven to the last ditch, roast meat. Think of the whole universe resolving itself into an anguished frenzy for a mutton chop, friend bacon and a poached egg! Think of wanting to eat so much that you could almost barter your most cherished recollections; your love of literature, the swing of those stately phrases which march through the mind like a triumphant army﻿—for food!

A curious state﻿—when the sheer force of an ill-fed body masters the mind and, like a savage, makes you run amuck. But it is something to have experienced such a moment. It is something to know why it is that women suddenly destroy the nearest thing to hand. I ask you to leave your comfortable beds, your well-spread tables, and live among the destitute, and you also will feel an overwhelming impulse to break windows or beat your hands against the stones.

I did not do either of these things, though I wanted to. But I determined, however I got it, I would have breakfast. I stood at the top of Southampton Row, and eyed the men on their way to their business. And then I selected a well-dressed individual about forty, with shrewd eyes and a certain humorous twist of the mouth. He wasn’t a literary man, I knew that, nor was he in commerce. I judged him to be a barrister, and I went straight up and opened my case.
===Chinese===
重回露天，尽管那巷子绝说不上芳香——破败的垃圾桶在路边摆出它们不体面的存在——总归是松了口气。我沿着滑铁卢桥路把怒气走散，过了桥，走上河滨大道。这时一天的事务已经苏醒，桥上走来那些伦敦成百上千地孕育出的纤细漂亮的人儿：郊区的娇弱女儿们，正赶往各自的写字楼——在商业意义上，她们是白奴贸易的牺牲品，一天七八个钟头敲打着打字机。

我缓步走到霍尔本，随后，九点刚过，一阵饥饿的狂怒兜头罩下。想吃东西的欲望攫住我，凶猛到使我恨不得愤怒地尖叫，怨一顿饭活活被夺。那一刻我懂了，饥饿的女人男人何以会突然砸窗掷石。那是营养极端匮乏的结果，是一种主动而不可遏制的渴望——要热饮，要新鲜面包，逼到绝境，还要烤肉。想想吧，整个宇宙化作对一块羊排、煎咸肉和一个荷包蛋的痛苦狂热！想想吧，想吃到几乎可以拿你最珍视的回忆去换——你对文学的热爱，那些如凯旋大军般列队行过脑海的堂皇句法——去换食物！

一种古怪的状态——营养不良的肉体凭蛮力制服了头脑，让你像野人一样狂性大发。但经历过这样一个时刻，总有所得。懂得女人何以会突然毁掉手边最近的东西，总有所得。我请你离开你舒适的床、你摆满的餐桌，到赤贫者中间住一住，你也会感到一股砸窗户、拿手掌拍石头的冲动，势不可挡。

这两样我都没有做，尽管我很想做。但我打定了主意：无论如何，我要吃上早饭。我站在南安普敦街（Southampton Row）口，打量着赶去办事的男人。随后我选中一个衣冠楚楚的四十来岁的人，眼睛精明，嘴角带一点幽默的弯。他不是文人，这个我知道；也不在商界。我断定他是个出庭律师，便径直走上前去，亮出了我的说辞。

===Original===
“You’ve got a lot of money,” I said, brightly.

He stopped, as I knew he would stop, curious to see what would happen.

“Well,” he said, “what about it, if I have?”

“I want some breakfast,” I said calmly.
===Chinese===
「您钱多得很呢。」我亮晶晶地说。

他站住了，我知道他会站住，好奇要看个究竟。

「哦，」他说，「就算我有，又怎么样？」

「我想吃顿早饭。」我平静地说。

===Original===
“Tell me why I should buy you any?”

“Because I have brains,” I said, desperately. The imaginary odour of frizzled bacon fired my will.

“If you have brains,” he answered, “use them to get some breakfast with.”

“I have,” I retorted, quickly. “I have chosen you.”

And at that he smiled, and then began to laugh, and I knew the day was won. For a man may resist tears, harden his heart to importunity, turn aside invective, but there is no son of Adam, who, can deny a woman when she makes him laugh.
===Chinese===
「说说看，凭什么要我给你买？」

「凭我有脑子。」我孤注一掷地说。煎咸肉的想象香味燃着了我的意志。

「你既然有脑子，」他答，「就用它给自己挣顿早饭去。」

「我正是这么干的，」我立刻回敬，「我选中了您。」

这话一出他莞尔一笑，随即大笑起来，我知道这一天赢了。男人可以抵挡眼泪，可以对纠缠硬起心肠，可以把咒骂撇在一边；可亚当的子孙里，没有一个能在逗笑他的女人面前说「不」。

===Original===
He surrendered at discretion, took me to the nearest tea shop and ordered bacon, eggs, toast and hot coffee. We talked over the meal, and I told him how hard I found it to get regular employment without a reference. He brushed that aside, but was quite interested when I explained the psychology of selling matches. He gave me a shilling as we parted, and I somehow felt it due to him that I should make another attempt to get engaged as a cook. I went to the Holborn Public Library and studied the advertisement column, and found that a cook, “good plain,” was wanted in Kensington. I spent some of my few pence on the fare, and called at a house near the High Street. There was nothing doing. The fatal lack of character stood like a flaming sword between me and security. I fought pretty hard, and offered to come for a day on trial, but the suggestion was received coldly. I had wanted to prove that a woman without a reference cannot get employment in a recognised vocation; and prove it I did. I went from that house resolved to waste no more time or effort in chasing a job, but to concentrate on the sale of cigarette cases, etc., on commission.

I felt obliged to treat myself to a wash, and by the time I had walked back to town it was just on the hour when I was to meet the lady of the black plush coat.
