Alas! she was not at the appointed place. I stood outside the friendly public house and waited for her wistfully. I opened the door and gazed hungrily inside. She was not there, and by closing time I had given up all hope of seeing her again. I spent my remaining coppers in matches, keeping a penny in reserve, but I had no luck. The afternoon is always bad for the match trade; it is the time when women do their shopping, and as I have said they are not profitable customers to street sellers. Moreover, I felt depressed, and despondency minimises your chances of a sale, so I did the only possible thing, I went and sat in the Park.

I did not often go to the Park in those hours when I had come to the end of my tether. You feel so acutely conscious of the gulf that cuts you off from social intercourse. I preferred to go to the British Museum and gaze at Rameses. The contemplation of that immeasurable aloofness has always given me rest and comfort. I have visited Rameses in many moments of poignant distress, and so it seemed natural to go to him when for the first time in my life I wanted food.

The material needs of life were becoming very much of an obsession. I realized with a start that I was no longer eager for the newspapers; I did not even trouble to look at the placards. Your vitality, unreplenished by comfortable food and sleep, instinctively fastens on those things that are essential to the maintenance of life. This is why your outcasts often appear stupid, stolid, almost mentally deficient. They know nothing of the affairs of the political or the literary world, they are unthrilled by the falling of dynasties, or the discovery of a planet. The avenues of interest open to the well-fed are closed to them, they are haunted always by the spectres, hunger and sleeplessness. For them so cruelly often there is only the street for a bed.
===Chinese===
他无条件投降了，带我到最近的一家茶馆，点了咸肉、鸡蛋、吐司和热咖啡。我们边吃边谈，我告诉他，没有品行证明书想找份固定工作有多难。他把这话轻轻拨开，但听我讲卖火柴的心理学时倒很有兴致。分手时他给了我一个先令；不知怎么，我觉得为了他，我该再去试一次受雇为厨娘。我去霍尔本公共图书馆研究广告栏，发现肯辛顿要一位「家常菜好手」厨娘。我花掉仅有的几枚便士当车钱，到高街附近一户人家登门。毫无结果。品行证明书这致命的缺憾，如一柄火焰剑，立在我与安稳之间。我苦苦力争，提出先试工一天，可这建议受了冷遇。我原想证明：一个没有推荐信的女人，在受承认的行当里找不到工作；我证明了。离开那户人家时我打定主意，不再浪费时间力气追逐工作，专心去做代销烟盒等等的买卖。

我觉得该犒劳自己洗把脸，等走回城里，正到了同黑长毛绒大衣夫人约会的钟点。唉！她不在约定的地方。我站在那家好心的酒馆门外，怅然等她。我推开门，眼巴巴往里望。她不在；到打烊时分，我已断了再见她的念头。我把剩下的铜子儿全买了火柴，留一便士备用，可是运气不济。下午向来是火柴买卖的淡季；那是女人们上街购物的时辰，而我说过，她们不是街头小贩的好主顾。何况我心头抑郁，而沮丧最折卖运。我便做了唯一可做的事——去公园里坐坐。

山穷水尽的那些时刻，我不常去公园。那条把你从社会交往中割开的鸿沟，你会感到锥心刻骨。我宁可去大英博物馆，去看拉美西斯。凝望那份不可丈量的超然，总能给我休息与安慰。多少回痛彻心扉的困顿里，我都去拜访拉美西斯；所以平生头一回想吃东西的时候，去投奔他似乎顺理成章。

生活的物质需求渐渐成了盘踞心头的执念。我悚然发觉，自己已不再渴慕报纸；连招贴都懒得看一眼。你的生命力得不到可口饭食与安眠的补给，便本能地死死抓住那些维持生存必需的东西。这就是为什么落魄者常显得愚蠢、呆滞、近乎智力欠缺。政界文界的事他们一概不知，王朝倾覆、行星发现都激不起他们的兴致。吃饱穿暖者敞开的种种兴趣大道，对他们统统关闭；他们心头永远游荡着两个幽灵——饥饿与失眠。而他们等着的，又那样常常残酷地只有——以街为床。

## VII Knocking at the Gate / 七 叩门

===Original===
It is confidently assumed that the doss house is extinct. The Public Lodging House for women, licensed by the London County Council, is supposed to have taken its place. This is not so. There are doss houses in all the poorer quarters of London, though they are more numerous on the south side of the river and in the East End than in other districts. The class of lodger who uses this kind of place for a night’s shelter is lower in the social scale than the women who frequent the lodging houses. There is, of course, the great economic difference between those who can pay one shilling and twopence, and those who can only rise to fourpence and, in some cases, threepence. As I explained, my first experience only cost me twopence, owing to the intervention of the lady in the plush coat. The general tariff is fourpence, and the accommodation is not so vastly inferior to those places which are supposed to be inspected by the L.C.C. and are permitted﻿—so far as the beds are concerned﻿—to flourish in undiminished dirt.

The doss houses are owned by individual proprietors, though I gather that there has arisen in this, as in other industries, a syndicate. The manageress, if she may be so called, is an employee at a small salary, and in certain instances, receives a commission on the takings. There is also frequently, a man on the premises in case of a disturbance. Usually, the outcasts who frequent the doss house are not quarrelsome﻿—they have not the energy; but cases have been known of a free fight, invariably terminated by the arrival of the man in charge who bundles the combatants out of the house. The floors are dirty, the bed clothes are of that uniform drab-grey which harbours dirt without exposing it. It is a dreadful colour, and always you feel that underneath the surface there must lurk thousands of germs, noxious bacilli and, very often, lice and bugs.
===Chinese===
人们满有把握地认定，宿夜所已经绝迹。伦敦郡议会（L.C.C.）发照管的女性公共寄宿旅舍，据说已取而代之。事实并非如此。伦敦所有较穷的街区都有宿夜所，只是河南岸和东区比别处更多。把这种地方当作一夜栖身之所的房客，其社会阶层低于常去寄宿旅舍的女人。当然，出得起一先令两便士的人，同只付得起四便士、有时三便士的人之间，有一道巨大的经济鸿沟。如我解释过的，我头一回的经验只花了两便士，多亏长毛绒大衣夫人从中说项。一般价目是四便士，而其设施比起那些据说受郡议会查验、并获准——就床铺而言——在毫不消减的污秽中照常兴旺的地方，也差不得太远。

宿夜所归私人业主所有，不过据我了解，同其他行业一样，这里也兴起了辛迪加。女管事——如果配得上这个称呼的话——是拿微薄薪水的雇员，某些情形下还从进项里抽佣金。场院内通常还养着一个男人，以防骚乱。常来宿夜所的落魄者一般并不好斗——他们没有那份力气；但也不乏混战的先例，总是以当值男人到场、把斗士们撵出门告终。地板肮脏，铺盖是那种千篇一律的暗灰色，藏得住脏，露不出脏。那是一种可怕的颜色，你总觉得那表层底下必定潜伏着成千上万的病菌、有毒的杆菌，而且常常还有虱子和臭虫。

===Original===
Bugs I met with, lice I did not encounter, generally speaking. This is, I think, a very definite alteration in the underworld of London. Not so many years ago, lice were rampant in many quarters, but, as I have said, the use of chemicals keeps them down, for which relief I give much thanks.

The people who use this kind of shelter are personally very dirty. They rarely have any opportunity of changing their clothes. They have lost that zest for personal daintiness so conspicuous at Kennedy Court, where the poorest little prostitute will wash her rags at every opportunity. Clean hands are not the rule in these particular sections, and many of the women are perennially verminous, so far as their hair is concerned. The strange thing is that, no matter how infected, they will not have it cut. It is not a question of shame; it is not a desire to escape criticism, for they could quite easily cut their hair themselves, nor is there any occasion to seek an official. But cut their hair they will not and masses of unkempt locks are wound round the head, literally alive with insects.

It is, I think, a feeling that long hair is the last touch of feminine attraction that life has left them. Possibly they feel that a cropped head would unsex them. They never express irritation at their uncomfortable condition﻿—they regard it, very largely, as an act of God, and day after day carry their load of dirt and misery without the faintest hope of any relief.

So keen is their resolve not to have their hair cut off that when, as sometimes happens, they are forced into the casual wards through sheer inability to walk, they will come out in the middle of the night rather than agree to have their heads attended to.
===Chinese===
臭虫我遇到过，虱子则一般说来未曾照面。我想，这是伦敦底层社会一个十分确切的变化。没有多少年以前，虱子还在许多街区横行；但如我所说，药剂的使用把它们压了下去——为此种种，我感激不尽。

使用这类栖身所的人们，个人卫生极差。他们难得有机会换洗衣裳。他们失去了对个人整洁的那份兴致——那在肯尼迪大院何等显眼：最穷的小妓女也会一有机会就洗她的破衣烂衫。在这些特殊的地段，干净的手不是常规，许多女人的头发常年生虱。奇怪的是，无论感染到什么地步，她们也不肯剪。这不是羞耻的问题；也不是想躲开批评——她们完全可以自己动手剪，无须求什么官家人。但她们就是不肯剪，一团团蓬乱的发绺缠在头上，当真爬满了虫子。

我想，这是一种感觉：长发是生活留给她们的最后一点女性魅力。或许她们觉得，剪了头就不再是女人了。她们从不为这不自在的处境发牢骚——很大程度上，她们把它当作天意，日复一日地驮着那份肮脏与悲苦，不带一丝得救的指望。

她们不肯剪发的决心如此坚决，以致有时——这是常有的事——实在走不动了被收进临时收容病房，她们也宁可半夜出走，不肯答应让人收拾自己的脑袋。

===Original===
Next to the question of hair the matter of feet is the most crucial. Boots are an insoluble problem, for they are always worn out. The most shapeless and terrible apologies for shoes are met with in the doss houses and the street, broken in the soles, bursting out at the sides, with huge cracks across the instep that chafe the skin and set up running sores. The cheapest pair of the most secondhand kind is beyond the means of this type of outcast to purchase, for, as the external condition deteriorates, so the earning capacity dwindles, and the danger of being arrested as a beggar increases. Many of them replenish their footwear from the scourings of dust bins. In the early morning you will often find a furtive figure turning over the refuse of the roadside. Crusts of bread are taken, and all kinds of garbage; but the treasure trove is a shoe, and if a pair is salved from the wreckage, physical contentment is assured.

The feet of the woman tramp, or street vendor﻿—it is the same thing﻿—are very pitiful to see. They are almost nonhuman in their shapelessness. Callosities, horny growths, bunions, destroy their contours, running sores are perennial and the efforts of Nature to escape the pain of contact with rough leather, result in distortion of the bone. Ingrowing nails are common; how should it be otherwise? The care of the feet calls for plentiful hot water and requisite toilet accessories; and these women, of whom I write, have not the means to wash their sores. There is, of course, due bathing accommodation in the casual ward of a workhouse, but as I shall show, the thing that survives longest and most fiercely among the destitute, is a passionate fear of restriction, the horror of detention within four walls, under a strange roof. For this reason before they will ask a night’s lodging of the Poor Law Guardians they will push endurance to an inhuman limit.
===Chinese===
头发之外，脚的问题最要命。靴子是一道无解的难题，因为总是穿坏。宿夜所和街头所见的最不成形、最可怕的鞋——鞋底断裂、侧面绽开、鞋面横着大口子，磨破皮肉、养出流脓的疮。最便宜的二手货也超出这类落魄者的财力，因为外在境况越糟，挣钱的本事越小，被当作乞丐抓起来的危险越大。许多人从垃圾桶的淘拣物里补充脚上的装备。清早你常会看到一个鬼祟的身影翻检路边的垃圾。面包皮被拿走，各样的垃圾也被拿走；而无主之宝是一只鞋——若能从残堆里凑成一双，身体的舒坦便有了保障。

流浪女或街头女贩——反正是一回事——的那双脚，看了叫人心酸。它们的走形几乎不类人足。胼胝、角质增生、拇囊炎毁掉了脚的轮廓，流脓的疮经年不愈，天性为了躲避粗皮磨痛所做的挣扎，造成了骨骼的扭曲。倒生长的趾甲是常事；又怎能不然呢？脚的护理要大量的热水和必需的盥洗用品；而我笔下的这些女人，连洗一洗疮口的资财都没有。济贫院的临时收容病房里当然有应份的沐浴设备；但如下文将表明的，赤贫者身上存续得最久、最烈的东西，是对约束的狂热恐惧，是对四面围墙、陌生屋顶之下被拘禁的恐怖。因此，她们宁可把忍耐推到非人的极限，也不肯向济贫法监护人讨一夜宿处。

===Original===
This is especially the case with the outcasts of the London streets. These women who have taken to the road and go out into the country have accustomed themselves to the casual ward, have assimilated every twist and turn of the law, and know to a nicety what they must do, and what the master has not the power to enforce.

There are some doss houses which are licensed by the L.C.C. Of these the Salvation Army Shelter in Hanbury Street, Whitechapel, is the largest. There for the sum of fivepence an outcast, however dilapidated, dirty, starving, or afflicted, can get a clean, warm bed. My night in Hanbury Street was one of the most poignant experiences of my adventures, and I shall deal with it at length later on. At the moment I am concerned to show how and where the outcasts sleep when they have sunk below the economic level of the licensed lodging house.

For those who have no money, not even a copper, there remains St. Crispin’s Dormitories. This Shelter, in the neighbourhood of Spitalfields, is run by charitable Catholics. The accommodation of necessity is frugal. A number of boxes run the length of the room, each box is provided with a mattress, pillow and blanket, and to obtain a box, women walk for miles and queue up early in the evening. Here there is considerable difficulty in keeping the place free from vermin, for very many of the lodgers have that long, matted hair which I have already described. St. Crispin’s is open from October to May. During the summer months it shuts down, for when the weather is warm a night on the Embankment or in one of the many open spaces of London is not insufferable. It is when the world is freezing and the wind cuts to your very soul that you cry out blindly for a bed. At such a time a dog kennel would seem hospitable: and yet all through the winter months hundreds of outcast women spend the night huddled in doorways, under arches, or keep themselves from freezing by that everlasting walking about.
===Chinese===
伦敦街头的落魄者尤其是这样。而那些上了路、走到乡下去的女人，已经同临时收容病房处惯了，把法律的每一处曲折都消化了，精确地知道哪些事她们必须做，哪些事主管人无权强加。

有些宿夜所是郡议会发照的。其中白教堂汉伯利街的救世军收容所最大。在那里，五个便士就能让一个落魄者——无论多么破败、肮脏、饥饿或病残——得到一张干净温暖的床。我在汉伯利街的那一夜，是我历险中最痛切的体验之一，稍后我将细谈。眼下我要说明的是，落魄者跌到持照寄宿旅舍的经济水平以下之后，如何睡、睡在哪里。

对那些身无分文、连一枚铜子儿也没有的人，还有圣克里斯平宿舍。这处栖身所位于斯皮塔佛德一带，由慈善的天主教徒经办。设施势必简朴。一排排木箱沿墙排开，每只箱子配一张草垫、一只枕头、一条毯子；为占得一只箱子，女人们要走好几英里路，傍晚早早地来排队。这地方要免于虫患颇为不易，因为房客里有非常多的人长着我前面描述的那种打结长发。圣克里斯平从十月开到五月。夏季月份它歇业，因为天气暖和时，在河滨大道或伦敦诸多露天场地上过一夜并非不可忍受。要到天寒地冻、风割进灵魂的时候，人才会盲然呼号着要一张床。那样的时节，一间狗窝也显得殷勤好客；然而整个冬季，数以百计的落魄女人夜夜蜷在门洞里、拱桥下，或者靠那永不停歇的走动使自己不至冻僵。

===Original===
The accommodation offered to women by the Metropolitan Board of Guardians is extremely limited. In the whole of London﻿—North, South, East and West﻿—there is but one casual ward where the destitute female can find a bed. The reasons for this limitation are interesting. Since the War, women’s casual wards have been handed over to the other sex. Paddington was a last female trench; now this has gone, and only Southwark remains. It follows, therefore, that to get a bed you must often﻿—indeed, most frequently﻿—traverse the length and breadth of London. For how shall it profit the outcasts at Highgate to know that on the other side of Lambeth Bridge a cubicle awaits them?

This male invasion of casual wards, intended for women, is an outcome of the fear of the authorities that an ex-service man should be discovered bedless and starving in the streets. This would arouse a very general indignation, and a steady fire of middle class criticism would be directed against the powers that be; it is the middle class, far more than the Socialist or Communist groups, that authority always fears. Were a man, who had fought in his country’s cause, found on the Embankment in the last stage of exhaustion, letters to the Press would rain down from all parts, the whole question of unemployment would be raised, and the old taunt of ingratitude flung in the teeth of the particular Cabinet responsible at the time.

There have been very few cases reported of ex-soldiers and sailors driven to the last gasp of endurance; and in order to prevent, so far as possible, such a contingency, the women’s wards of the workhouses have been taken from them. I want to make it perfectly plain that I, for one, would not take any beds from the men who fought for England. But why should the women, wives, mothers, sisters, sweethearts, of those same heroes be flung into the street in order to save the authorities from well-merited attack? The plea, that an old soldier must not starve, does not and cannot justify the callous indifference shown to a woman homeless and hungry.
===Chinese===
大都会监护人委员会提供给女性的设施极其有限。整个伦敦——东南西北——只剩下一处临时收容病房收容赤贫女性。这种限制的缘由颇堪玩味。大战以来，女性的临时收容病房陆续移交给了男性。帕丁顿是最后一处女性阵地；如今它也失守，只剩萨瑟克。于是，为求一张床，你往往——实在说，多半——要横穿整个伦敦。海格特的落魄者就算知道兰贝斯桥对面有一间小格铺等着她，于她又有何益？

男性这般侵占本属女性的临时收容病房，出自当局的一种恐惧：怕某个退伍军人被发现流落街头、饥寒交迫。那会激起普遍的公愤，中产阶级的批评将构成持续的火力，直射当权者；当局向来怕的中产阶级，远过于社会主义者或共产主义者。倘若一个曾为国征战的人，被人发现在河滨大道上气若游丝，致报社的信会从四面八方如雨而至，失业问题整个会被端出来，「忘恩负义」这句老话，会掷到当时内阁的脸上。

据报，退伍陆海军人被逼到忍无可忍的地步的案例寥寥无几；而为了尽可能防患于未然，济贫院的女性病房便从她们手里夺走了。我要说得明明白白：我个人绝不肯从为英格兰作战的男人们床上拿走一张。可是，为什么要为了让当局躲开一场罪有应得的攻击，就把这些英雄的妻子、母亲、姐妹、爱人抛到街上？「老战士不得挨饿」这条诉求，不能也不足以为对无家可归、饥肠辘辘的女人的冷酷漠视作辩。

===Original===
There is no question of charity involved in the matter of the casual ward. The workhouse is kept up out of the rates, and every citizen, male and female, has a right to claim the shelter thus provided. But because no one cares what happens to the woman who is down and out, because no one troubles to enquire if she has a place wherein to lay her head, she is deliberately and specifically thrown to the dogs, that Cabinet Ministers may escape a whipping.

This is not the only penalty exacted from my sex. The men in the casual ward have hot tea every morning; the women have the dregs of their teapots an hour later. This, at least, was the state of things at Southwark Workhouse where I spent a night in the casual ward. Following on my revelation of this cruel custom in a Sunday newspaper, the Board of Guardians gave instructions that Southwark should be provided with a gas stove whereon an urn could sit, in which the tea could be kept hot.

As well as the superior accommodation of male public lodging houses, and the unfair division of the casual wards, the authorities rightly afford opportunity for an out-of-work, or a destitute man to make good. The master of every workhouse is instructed particularly to note those male casuals who have been in the Army or the Navy; those with any trace of education; those who have average abilities. These men when they leave the ward are given an order of admission to a hostel in Holborn, where they stay, free of charge, for a week. The conditions of life there are quite human; they have good food, decent beds, rooms for recreation and free tobacco. I cannot speak too highly of the arrangements; they provide a man with a chance to regain the footing he has lost. During the week’s stay all efforts are made to find him a job, and he is allowed to come and go in his search for employment without let or hindrance. Wise provision, admirable organisation! For the sake of the men who have endured something of what I and my sisters have passed through, I am thankful beyond words that such a place exists.
===Chinese===
临时收容病房的事，谈不上慈善。济贫院靠地方税维持，每一个公民，无论男女，都有权要求它提供的栖身之处。可是，正因为没有人在乎一个穷途末路的女人会怎样，没有人费心问一问她可有搁脑袋的地方，她便被蓄意地、专门地抛去喂狗——好叫内阁大臣们免受鞭笞。

这不是我的性别缴纳的唯一罚款。临时收容病房里的男人每天早晨有热茶；女人们晚一个钟头，得到的是茶壶里的残汤。至少，我在萨瑟克济贫院临时收容病房过夜的那晚，情形如此。自打我在一家星期日报上揭露了这残忍的惯例，监护人委员会便下指示，给萨瑟克添置一只煤气灶，好搁一把烧水壶，让茶保温。

除了男性公共寄宿旅舍的设施更优、临时收容病房分配不公之外，当局也确实为失业或赤贫的男子提供了东山再起的门路。每座济贫院的主管人都奉命特别留意那些曾服役陆海军的男性临时收容者；那些带一点教育痕迹的；那些智力中平的。这些人离院时，会领到一张霍尔本一家旅舍的入院单，免费住上一周。那里的生活条件相当人道：饭食良好，床铺体面，有娱乐室，还有免费的烟草。这安排我怎么夸也不过分；它给了一个男人夺回失地的机会。住这一周里，各方竭力替他找活儿，他也来去自由，求职不受阻拦。明智的设置，出色的组织！为了那些同我和我的姐妹们一样吃过苦头的男人们，有这样的地方存在，我感激得无以言表。

===Original===
But why, because an outcast is a woman, should she be debarred from opportunity to make a living? Any one of the women with whom I came in contact﻿—I do not include the little prostitutes﻿—provided with a week’s respite, in decent conditions, afforded the opportunity to wash their rags, to mend their clothes and regain something of the human attributes of their beginnings, would emerge a different creature. But no! it does not matter what happens to the woman derelict; the policy seems to be that the sooner she dies of starvation and exposure the better for society.

There is no need, human or economic, to salve her. She is of no account. But save the man! Use the casual wards. Inspect the lodging houses. Throw open the kindly doors of a comfortably equipped hostel, and the Government shall escape the castigation they so merit. Apart from the Salvation Army, and one or two other bodies, the woman outcast in the London streets today is as derelict as the woman of Hood’s great lines.

Apart from political considerations there is, I think, a psychological explanation that the spectacle of a man out of work, feeling the humiliation which is the inevitable accompaniment of dependence, arouses not only commiseration, but indignation in the minds of the majority of women. Something, they feel, must be wrong with society, otherwise why should a decent, good-looking, well-spoken fellow be obliged, if not to beg, to do something very like it? But let a vagrant of the female sex come to the door, and, generally speaking, she creates a feeling of distrust, if not hostility. Dirt, in a man, not infrequently suggests romance﻿—in a woman it implies degradation, neglect and an obstinate refusal to undertake the obligations of her sex.
===Chinese===
可是为什么，就因为落魄者是个女人，就该剥夺她谋生的机会？我接触过的任何一个女人——小妓女们不计在内——只要给一周的喘息、体面的条件，有机会洗洗破衣、缝缝衣裳、找回一点生而为人的本色，出来时就会是另一个人。可是不！女落魄者的死活无关紧要；政策似乎是：她越早死于饥寒，对社会越好。

无论从人道还是从经济上，都没有必要拯救她。她无足轻重。可是男人要救！临时收容病房要给他用。寄宿旅舍要查。设备舒适的好旅舍要向他敞开温厚的大门——于是政府便逃脱了它罪有应得的笞刑。除去救世军和一两个别的团体，今日伦敦街头的落魄女人，正如胡德名句里那个女人一样孤苦无告。

政治考量之外，我想还有一种心理的解释：一个男人失业的景象——他忍受着依附于人必然伴随的屈辱——唤起的不仅是多数女人的怜悯，还有义愤。她们觉得，社会一定出了什么毛病，否则，一个正派、体面、谈吐斯文的小伙子，何至于不得不行乞、或做极近于行乞的事？可是让一个女性流浪者上门来，一般说来，她激起的是不信任，甚至敌意。男人身上的脏，常常暗示着罗曼史；女人身上的脏，则意味着堕落、疏忽，和一种拒不承担女性义务的顽固。

===Original===
No housewife of the well-kept home feels comfortable with an unkempt creature in the vicinity. “Get rid of her” is the usual instruction and irritable desire, generally coupled, I admit, with an instruction to hand out a piece of bread﻿—never, I swear, with that additional butter which makes it fit for human consumption. Oh! the difference between bread and bread-and-butter! If it were only possible for those people who never have to worry about their next meal to know the bitter taste of dry bread. Margarine, that substitute for generosity, beloved of the meagre, raises false hopes. How eagerly you take the first bite, with what satisfaction you proceed to masticate, and then﻿—that sickly, salty, rancid flavour overcomes you and in a violent physical revulsion you spit it out. There are brands of margarine which pass muster on the palate; but these are not for the delectation of the outcast. Poverty is their crime, and the punishment is unremitting. I have broken into this dissertation, because I am tired of hearing good and comfortable women complain of the wicked waste of good victuals bestowed at their back door. Tales have been told me of hungry beggars who cast slices of the best household in the gutter just outside. Judicious enquiry has generally uncovered the fact that the bread was very stale, and to make a meal on stale bread, unmoistened, is a physical impossibility, as I myself can testify. It is quite useless to say to the good and virtuous “Give money.” The answer is “They will spend it in drink”﻿—a wise provision of nature mercifully ordained for the comfort of the stomach. For when your energy is gone and you are vitiated and trembling, your digestion will not assimilate cold bread, but will respond immediately to the tonic of a glass of beer, or a cup of hot coffee. Wherefore, so say all outcasts, blessed be she who dispenses coppers instead of broken meat. Though a nicely cut sandwich, such as you would eat yourself, does not come amiss.
===Chinese===
没有一个家宅整洁的主妇，乐见一个邋遢的女人待在近旁。「把她打发走」是惯常的吩咐和恼怒的心愿，通常——我承认——还附带一句吩咐：递给她一块面包；但从不——我可以起誓——附带那点能使面包堪以下咽的黄油。唉！光面包和黄油面包之间的天壤之别！那些从不为下一顿饭发愁的人，若能尝一尝干面包的苦味就好了。人造黄油，那慷慨的代用品、吝啬鬼的心爱，唤起的是虚假的希望。你多么急切地咬下第一口，多么满意地咀嚼下去，随即——那甜腻、咸涩、哈喇的味道压倒了你，一阵剧烈的生理反胃，你把它吐了出来。有些人造黄油的牌子入口还过得去；但那不是给落魄者享用的。贫穷是她们的罪，惩罚无休无止。我之所以插进这段议论，是因为我听腻了那些善良舒适的女人抱怨：后门施舍的好饭菜被糟蹋了。有人给我讲过饥饿的乞丐把上好的家制面包片扔进门边阴沟的故事。一经细问，十有八九查明：那面包已经馊硬。靠不泡软的馊面包吃一顿饭，体力上绝无可能——我自己可以作证。对善良贞淑的人们说「给钱吧」，全然无用。回答是：「她们要拿去买酒喝」——这倒是造化慈悲的明智安排，好叫肠胃舒服些。因为当你精力耗尽、浑身虚颤时，你的胃消化不了冷面包，却会立刻响应一杯啤酒或一杯热咖啡的滋补。因此，一切落魄者都说：愿施舍铜子儿而非残羹的女人蒙福。虽然一份切得漂漂亮亮、你自己也肯吃的那种三明治，也并不讨嫌。

===Original===
But such is not the luck of woman. Too often she is regarded as a perambulatory dust bin, and packets of bread the worse for wear, mouldy potatoes, cheese rinds, are thrust upon her, thus clearing the pantry and poulticing the faint sense of reproach that sometimes attacks the amply nurtured. But make no mistake; those cruelly deceptive packages, unacceptable to man or beast, only serve to dishearten. I can imagine no greater nor more cruel disappointment, than awaits the poor woman who undoes the brown paper and white string so thoughtfully provided by the villa resident, only to discover the spring cleanings of the larder.

It is not for food alone that the outcast comes to the back door. There is always the lingering hope that a pair of boots may come her way. But since the war, such gifts are very rare and very precious, and indeed as a fellow outcast, some fifteen years upon the road, assured me, it is but seldom nowadays that you get so much as an old skirt.
===Chinese===
女人却没有这样的运气。她太常被当作一只行走的垃圾桶：磨损的面包包、发霉的土豆、干酪硬皮，一股脑塞给她，既清了食品柜，又敷一敷那份偶尔袭扰营养充足者的良心微痛。可是别弄错：那些男人畜生都不要的、残酷骗人的纸包，只会叫人心灰。我想象不出比这更巨大、更残忍的失望了：可怜的女人解开别墅住户体贴备好的牛皮纸和白细绳，却发现里面是食品柜大扫除的出货。

落魄者到后门口来，不单是为吃的。她心里总残存着一线希望：兴许能碰上一双靴子。但自大战以来，这类馈赠又稀罕又贵重；诚如一位同道的落魄者——在路上走了约莫十五年——向我担保的，如今连一条旧裙子都难得讨到了。

===Original===
Country houses are far more responsive in this respect than London. Probably the tradition of a large hearted hospitality still lingers, and the old commandment of the Middle Ages that none should be sent empty away may yet hold good. At any rate, I know that some of my outcast friends possess not only boots, but comfortable waterproofs and well-worn tweeds, and, as they have told me, there are places where they make a call each month or six weeks, and always there is a pile of garments waiting for them. It is interesting to note that when an outcast does obtain a decent garment she does not, as is popularly supposed, hurry to the pawnshop to raise the price of a pint of beer. For, I say again, beer plays a very small part in the life of the down-and-out, and once having got a garment able to resist the wind and weather, she will cling to the same until its final dissolution or her extreme need.

There is a nice legal point in relation to this collecting of clothes. He or she who goes to a house and asks for food or raiment is held guilty of begging and may be given in charge. But, so says the law, you may call at any house and offer to sell matches, or pins, or hairpins, or some other unimportant trifle, and there is nothing against the suggestion that the wares may be exchanged for the cast-offs of the wardrobe. Thus are the police placated and the liberty of the individual secured. Only in one case may proffered sale be omitted. You may call at any religious institution and ask for food without fear of the courts, even under the very shadow of a constable.
===Chinese===
乡间宅邸在这方面的回应远比伦敦慷慨。也许宽厚好客的传统犹存，中世纪那条「不叫人空手而去」的古训或许依然有效。至少我知道，我的一些落魄朋友不但有靴子，还有像样的雨衣和穿旧的花呢衣；照她们说，有些地方她们每月或每六周去登一次门，总有一堆衣裳等着她们。有趣的是，落魄女人真得到一件像样的衣裳时，并不像世人臆想的那样，急着进当铺换一品脱啤酒钱。因为我再说一遍，啤酒在穷途末路者的生活里只占很小的位置；一旦到手一件挡风遮雨的衣裳，她会死死抓住它，直到它彻底散架、或她山穷水尽。

关于这种收衣服的门道，有一个微妙的法律要点。上门求食求衣者，构成行乞罪，可被送官。但法律又说：你可以到任何人家上门兜售火柴、别针、发夹或别的什么无关紧要的小玩意，而且不妨提议以货易衣橱里的旧衣。警察由此安抚，个人的自由由此保全。只有一种情形可免于兜售之仪：你可以到任何宗教机构求食，不必怕见官府，哪怕就站在警察的影子底下。

===Original===
A dear old tramp of my acquaintance indulges in this exercise as a form of sport and greatly delights to ring the bell of a certain Nazareth House with the appraising eye of a policeman upon her. Women do not, as a rule, offer plants in exchange for clothes. That is a branch of industry reserved for men. Nor is this to be wondered at. The enthusiastic young wife who joyfully surrenders her husband’s favourite jacket for an aspidistra, at the persuasion of a man, would indignantly refuse a similar proposal from a member of her own sex. She would, indeed, immediately credit the woman with sinister designs. What should she do with a jacket? While the bare notion that any of her pretty garments should go to the clothing of the vagrant would be so distasteful as to induce the abrupt closing of the door. It may be that better luck attends the outcast at the country house because there we still find the male retainer. Even in moderate mansions or dwellings of still lesser calibre, something like a butler, a footman or a tweeny man, is yet to be found, and the immutable law of sex decrees that the most battered specimen of femininity will find a readier sympathy in the bosom of a man than from a more blooming specimen of womanhood.

This then, seems to me, an additional reason why the case of the outcast woman should be dealt with justly. Most of these street wanderers have been wives, many of them have been mothers, and for this reason alone their cry should appeal to the heart of man.
===Chinese===
我认识的一位亲爱的老流浪妇，把这门功课当运动来练，特别乐于在警察估量的目光下按响某家拿撒勒修女院的门铃。女人照例不拿花草去换衣裳。那是留给男人的行业分支。这也不足为怪。那位热心的年轻太太，在男人劝说下欢欢喜喜地舍出丈夫的心爱上衣，换一盆蜘蛛抱蛋；若同样的提议出自同性之口，她定会愤然回绝。真的，她会立刻断定那女人居心叵测。她要一件男人上衣做什么？至于她自己任何一件漂亮衣裳要穿到流浪女身上去——这光秃秃的念头本身就够倒胃口，足以叫她砰然关门。乡间宅邸那边落魄者的运气较好，或许因为那里还留着男仆。即便中等的宅邸、乃至更次一等的人家，也还找得到一个近似管家、男仆或候补小厮的男人；而性别的铁律裁定：再残破的女性标本，在男人胸中寻得的同情，也比在最娇艳的女性标本那里来得现成。

在我看来，这便是落魄女人的处境必须得到公道对待的又一条理由。这些街头流浪者大都做过妻子，许多做过母亲；单凭这一点，她们的呼号就应当打动人心。

## VIII The Awful Business of the Door-Handle / 八 门把手的可怕勾当

===Original===
I first went to the casual ward under very melancholy conditions. I was dispirited to find that my entry into commerce had been postponed. I had high hopes as to what I should achieve in the sale of cigarette cases, etc., on commission. But my friend of the plush coat did not turn up. I went to the public-house again that evening, but she was not there. My low spirits were reflected in my trade—I could not get my matches off, and no one would give me more than twopence. I watched the outside of that public house with a sinking heart. I had two coppers on me after a purchase of a cup of tea and a miserable bun, and there was no means by which I could raise any more. My only chance of escaping an unpleasant experience was to go home. But that would have been to confess failure, and when it comes to the fundamentals of life I always develop a certain sticking power. I decided to see this experience through.

The wind had fallen, and a peculiarly unpleasant sleet was coming down, stinging your face and creeping down between the collar of your coat and your neck. I felt as if I had been an outcast for years and I had to take myself in hand pretty thoroughly before I found courage to speak to a policeman.

The outward and visible signs of my destitution were very plain that evening. Unconsciously I had acquired the slinking gait which comes of glancing furtively behind to see if you are being followed by some prosperous citizen with an eye to complaint. It was difficult to hold up my head, my shoulders drooped wearily. I wished very much that I was coward enough to go back to my bed. But I knew that such a course would cut me off from the knowledge I was so desperately eager to gain. To understand how an outcast feels, you yourself must be outcast.

===Chinese===
我头一回去临时收容病房，正赶上极消沉的境况。发觉我的商业进场被推迟了，我很沮丧。对于代销香烟盒之类抽取佣金能有何等成就，我原抱有很大希望。可是我那位长毛绒大衣夫人没有露面。当晚我又去了那家酒馆，她不在。情绪低落反映在了我的生意上——火柴卖不动，谁也不肯多给两便士以外的东西。我看着那家酒馆的外面，心一直往下沉。买了一杯茶、一个寒酸的小圆面包之后，我身上只剩两个铜子儿，再没有办法多弄到一文。要逃脱一场不愉快的经历，我唯一的机会是回家。可那就等于承认失败；而一到人生的根本关头，我总会生出一股黏劲。我决心把这场经历看到底。

风停了，一场特别叫人难受的雨夹雪正下着，扎得脸生疼，顺着衣领往脖子里钻。我觉得自己仿佛已经落魄了好多年，不得不把自己狠狠收拾了一通，才鼓起勇气去跟一个警察搭话。

那天傍晚，我穷困的外在可见标志十分明白。不知不觉间，我已经染上了那种鬼祟溜边的步态——那是老要回头偷看、生怕被哪个存心投诉的体面市民跟上的人特有的。头不容易抬起来，肩膀疲惫地耷拉着。我巴不得自己够胆小，能回自己床上去。可我知道，那样一条路会把我同我拼命渴望求得的知识隔断。要懂得落魄者是什么滋味，你自己就得落魄。

===Original===
“Where is the nearest casual ward, please?” I asked a young policeman.

“There’s only one for women, and that’s in Great Guildford Street, Southwark. You’d best go over Westminster Bridge and then enquire your way.”

===Chinese===
「请问最近的临时收容病房在哪儿？」我问一个年轻警察。

「只有一家收女人的，在大吉尔福德街，萨瑟克。你最好过威斯敏斯特桥，再一路问过去。」

===Original===
I followed his directions and, much buffeted by cold and rain, found myself in the Kennington Road. I was directed to a workhouse near Lambeth Walk, where I interviewed the porter. He explained that his establishment was for men only, but that he would give me an order which would admit me to Southwark. Furthermore, he handed me a red counter, marked with the mystic figure One.

“If you give this to the tram conductor, he’ll give you a ticket,” he explained.

“What shall I have to do at the Workhouse?” I asked, rather frightened.

“Oh, nothing very terrible, my dear,” was the cheery reply. “They’ll take your money away till the morning, that is, if you have any. You can’t go in with more than a shilling, you know.”

My resources, I explained, were twopence, and I got into the tram, feeling as though the eyes of all the world were on me.

===Chinese===
我照他的指点走，被寒冷和雨水一路抽打，到了肯宁顿路。有人指给我兰贝斯步道附近的一座济贫院，我在那里见了门房。他解释说，他这家只收男人，但他可以给我一张准入萨瑟克的单子。此外，他递给我一枚红色筹码，上面印着神秘的数字一。

「把这个交给电车售票员，他会给你一张票。」他解释道。

「我进了济贫院得做什么？」我问，心里相当害怕。

「哦，没什么可怕的，亲爱的，」回答得很爽朗。「他们会把你的钱收走，到早晨再还——也就是说，你要是有钱的话。你知道，带超过一先令是进不去的。」

我说明了我的全部家当是两便士，随后上了电车，觉得全世界的眼睛都在盯着我。

===Original===
“Fares, please,” said the conductor, and then I surprised myself.

Something inside me asserted itself, and I leaned forward in my best social manner, in marked contrast to my dilapidated clothes, and handed the conductor the counter.

“Stop outside the casual ward, if you please,” I said.

The woman next to me edged farther along the seat, but none of the others took any notice. The conductor beamed at me genially, answered “Certainly, Miss,” and gave me a ticket.

I was awfully glad he called me “Miss,” because it seemed to show that those other outcasts who had handed him their counters had been treated just as nicely.

===Chinese===
「请买票。」售票员说。接下来我把自己都吓了一跳。

我体内有什么东西自行站了出来，我以我最上流的社交姿态向前欠身——同我那身破烂衣裳形成鲜明对照——把筹码递给售票员。

「请在临时收容病房门口停一下，劳驾。」我说。

我旁边那个女人往座位另一头挪了挪，别的谁也没理会。售票员和蔼地朝我满脸堆笑，答一声「好的，小姐」，给了我一张票。

他称我「小姐」，我高兴极了，因为这似乎表明，那些同样交过筹码的落魄姐妹们，也受过一样客气的对待。

===Original===
He stopped at the nearest point to the House and wished me good luck very cheerily. I found the entrance to the casual ward with difficulty—the walls seemed very high and the night was dark. When, at last, I unearthed the bell, the long clanging peal was ominous. Automatically the door opened, and the porter told me to come inside.

He was a nice looking young man with bright eyes and a great sense of humour.

“What’s your name?” he asked.

The inevitable “Annie Turner” duly came forth.

“Where were you born?”

The question seemed to me ridiculous.

“I don’t know,” I answered.

“Now, that’s a pity,” said the porter. “Where did you go to school?”

“Manchester,” I said, lying boldly.

===Chinese===
他在离济贫院最近的一站停了车，痛痛快快地祝我好运。我费了好大劲才找到临时收容病房的入口——墙显得很高，夜又黑。等我终于把门铃摸出来，那一长串刺耳的铃声听着不吉利。门自动开了，门房叫我进去。

他是个模样周正的年轻人，眼睛明亮，极有幽默感。

「你叫什么名字？」他问。

少不了的那声「安妮·特纳」照例应声而出。

「你在哪儿出生的？」

这问题在我看来荒唐得很。

「不知道。」我答。

「唉，那可遗憾了，」门房说。「你在哪儿上的学？」

「曼彻斯特。」我说，撒谎撒得脸不改色。

===Original===
Age came next, colour of eyes and hair, and then—“What’s your height?”

“Now why on earth,” said I, “do you want to know that for?”

The porter bent towards me confidentially. “The Minister of Health’s very interested in your height,” said he. Whereat I determined that the Minister of Health should be fully satisfied.

Now I have always wished to be a tall woman and Fate having brought me the opportunity I gave myself five foot nine inches, which the porter, highly amused, duly entered up.

“Now you go on upstairs,” said he, “and the female attendants will look after you.”

It has been said that this particular porter is cross-tempered—some of my fellow casuals have whispered it with bated breath. For this reason, I should like to say, he is one of the nicest people I have ever met and I hope in a future life he will be attendant to St. Peter.

===Chinese===
接着是年龄、眼睛和头发的颜色，然后——「你身高多少？」

「我说，你们到底，」我道，「打听这个做什么？」

门房神秘兮兮地朝我俯过身来。「卫生大臣对你的身高很感兴趣。」他说。于是我打定主意，一定要让卫生大臣称心满意。

我一直盼着自己是个高个子女人，造化既把机会送到手上，我便给自己报了五英尺九英寸，门房觉得有趣极了，照例登记在册。

「现在你上楼去吧，」他说，「女管理员会照应你。」

有人说这位门房脾气坏——我的一些落魄姐妹曾屏着气这样咬耳朵。为此我要说句公道话：他是我平生所见最和善的人之一，但愿来生他能去给圣彼得当门房。

===Original===
I was received by a young woman seated at a table, who put me through the identical catechism framed by the porter. These preliminaries over, she explained, quite nicely, the position of the woman admitted to the casual ward.

“This place,” she said, “as you know, is run by rules decided by the authorities. They are not made by us, but we are responsible that they are carried through. I hope you will see your way to conform to them.”

This reception, I admit, was a great surprise. I had thought to find Bumble rampant. I had expected to be treated with ignominy, if not with derision. I found nothing of either. The attitude of all the officials with whom I came in contact was quite human. There was nothing in their manner that could possibly affront that fundamental dignity born in the meanest of mankind. I was destitute, but that did not impair my inalienable right to human treatment. Throughout my stay I was never once made to feel I was a pauper.

===Chinese===
接待我的是一位坐在桌旁的年轻女子，她把门房拟定的那套问答原样又考了我一遍。这些开场手续过后，她客客气气地讲解了获准进入临时收容病房的女人的处境。

「这地方，」她说，「你是知道的，按当局定下的规矩办事。规矩不是我们定的，但规矩得到执行，责任在我们。希望你能够遵照办理。」

我承认，这样的接待大大出乎意料。我本以为会撞上一个耀武扬威的班布尔。我料想自己会受到羞辱，即便不受嘲弄。结果两者都毫无踪影。我接触到的所有官员，态度都相当富有人味。他们举止里没有任何东西可能冒犯那种连最早微的人与生俱来的基本尊严。我一文不名，但这并不损害我不可剥夺的、受人对待的权利。整个逗留期间，没有一次让我觉得自己是个贫民。

===Original===
“Have you any money?” asked the young woman.

I handed her my twopence and asked if I might keep my nightdress.

“We supply one,” she answered. “I am sorry, but you must not take anything with you into the ward.”

I solemnly handed over the contents of my packet, together with a dilapidated powder puff, the one thing that had not been taken from me in the doss house.

“If you have any private papers, or letters I will seal them in a packet and keep them for you until the morning. We have no desire to pry into your affairs; you understand that?”

I had not any papers, and I explained that I had spent the previous night in a doss house and should enjoy a hot bath immensely. She led the way into the bath room, and I had a hot tub, with decent soap and a good sized bath towel. The attendant watched me fairly closely.

===Chinese===
「你身上有钱吗？」年轻女子问。

我把两便士交给她，又问睡衣可不可以留着。

「我们发一件。」她答。「对不起，进病房什么也不许带。」

我郑重其事地交出了小包里的全部家当，外加一个破旧的粉扑——那是宿夜所里唯一没被人拿走的东西。

「你要是带有私人文件或信件，我会封成一个纸包，替你保管到早晨。我们无意窥探你的私事；你明白吗？」

我没有什么文件，便说明我头天夜里是在宿夜所过的，很想来一个热水澡。她在头前带路进了浴室，我洗了个热水澡，有像样的肥皂和一条够大的浴巾。管理员相当仔细地瞧着我洗。

===Original===
“Why you’re quite clean,” she said, and seemed remarkably astonished. “It’s such a relief,” she continued, “to find someone who isn’t dirty. By the by, is your head all right?”

I bowed for her inspection, and my hair passed muster.

“You’ve no idea what some of them are like, you know. We have women in here who are fairly alive.”

“They can’t help it, really, can they?” I said protestingly. “It’s difficult to find the money for a wash, let alone a bath.”

“But it’s unfair to the others, all the same,” said the young woman, “and the worst of it is some of them won’t let you touch their heads.”

===Chinese===
「咦，你挺干净的嘛。」她说，显得异常惊讶。「碰到一个不脏的，」她接着说，「真是松了口气。顺便问一句，你的头没事吧？」

我低下头给她验看，我的头发过了关。

「你根本想不到有些人是什么样子。我们这儿进来的女人，有的简直浑身是活的。」

「那也由不得她们，真的，不是吗？」我抗议道。「凑个洗脸的钱都难，更别说洗澡了。」

「可对别人还是不公平，一样地不公平，」年轻女子说，「最糟的是，有些人死活不让碰她们的脑袋。」

===Original===
It was not any good to argue the point, and her attitude is quite explicable. It is not a nice job to have to “cleanse” heads riddled with lice. As I have said a number of outcasts decline to let their hair be interfered with. A small percentage, however, yield to necessity and submit to treatment.

The particular official who is on duty has to tackle the proposition with results unpleasing to a sensitive stomach. Generally speaking, when the offending head is shaved, and excavations conducted under the scalp—for lice burrow deep—the patient is exhausted. For it is a strange thing that, deprived of this mass of obscene life, the body seems to grow weak, and frequently a day or two in bed in the infirmary is necessary to restore normal conditions. It may be that the unceasing irritation which would drive most people almost mad, serves as a kind of counterblast to the mental stress of seeking food and lodging. In other words, the ceaseless activity of the lice may prove an anodyne to the pangs of starvation. Remove the artificial stimulus and collapse sets in.

===Chinese===
这一点争也无益，她的态度完全可以解释。「清扫」满是虱子的脑袋本来就不是件美差。如我所说，许多落魄女人不肯让人动她们的头发。但也有很小一部分人屈从于必要，接受处置。

值班的那位官员得对付这道难题，其结果叫肠胃敏感的人看了难受。一般说来，等作孽的脑袋剃光了、头皮底下又开了掘——因为虱子钻得深——受刑的人也就精疲力竭了。怪就怪在：一旦失去这一团龌龊的生命，身体似乎反而虚弱下去，往往要在医务所卧床一两天才能复原。也许，那种能把大多数人几乎逼疯的无休止的瘙痒，对四处谋求食宿的精神苦楚反倒起了一种抵消作用。换句话说，虱子无休无止的折腾，或许正是饥饿绞痛的一剂镇痛药。撤掉这人为的刺激，虚脱便随之而来。

===Original===
I followed the bright-faced young woman along a bleak corridor, dressed in the workhouse nightgown, a striped garment, fastening at the back, with long enveloping sleeves.

“Leave your clothes on the floor outside the door,” she said, “they’ll be inspected, and, if necessary—baked.”

This was a polite way of telling me that should my garments prove to be verminous they would be dealt with. There is always a hot chamber working in the House. Sometimes the clothes suffer as well as the insects, and the unfortunate casual gets back a singed skirt, or a scorched petticoat. I was put wise as to this by a wonderful woman, by name Kitty Grimshaw, one of the finest characters on the road.

===Chinese===
我穿着济贫院的睡袍跟在面包鲜亮的年轻女子身后，走过一条阴冷的走廊——那是一件条纹衣裳，背后系扣，袖子又长又裹身。

「把衣服搁在门外地上，」她说，「有人来查验，必要时——烘烤。」

这是一种客气的说法，告诉我倘若我的衣裳查出虱子，就要照章处置。济贫院里总有一间热烘房日夜开着。有时衣裳同虫子一道遭殃，倒霉的收容者领回一条烤焦了边的裙子，或一件燎糊了的衬裙。这件事是一位了不起的女人给我点破的，她叫基蒂·格里姆肖，路上数得着的好人物之一。

===Original===
“If they burns your clothes, me dear,” she said, “they’re bound to make ’em good, the law says so, and I stands by the law.”

There is nothing indeed about the law as it applies to the Workhouse that Kitty does not know. She is like one of those old soldiers, who have mastered King’s Regulations so completely that they can trip up a superior officer at any minute.

“Law is law,” Kitty always says, “and don’t you forget to let the Master know you know it.”

===Chinese===
「他们要是烧了你的衣裳，亲爱的，」她说，「就得照价赔，法律这么说的，我就认法律。」

关于济贫院适用的一切法律，实在没有基蒂不知道的。她像那些老兵，把《国王条例》背得烂熟，随时能把一个上级军官绊个人仰马翻。

「法律就是法律，」基蒂口头常说，「别忘了让院长知道你懂法律。」

===Original===
I was given a mattress, a pillow and a pair of blankets, and told to take them into my “cell”—word of ill omen. It sent a shudder through my body. I do not understand why the word “cell” is employed, unless it be that it is so exact a replica of the prison variety that even the official sense of humour would boggle at another name. Still, cubicle might be tried; it would not have so ominous a suggestion.

I was left in the darkness very much alone. I had been oppressed with the sense of sleeping humanity the previous night; I had been surrounded by an atmosphere that would not let me rest; but in the terrible isolation of my cell, my soul ached for the company of the women with their unspeakable bundles, their rabbit skins, their “mog.”

High up in the wall was a tiny, round window, like a porthole, far beyond my power to reach. There was a little observation shutter in the door; now and again it was lifted and the light from the corridor outside peeped in.

The mattress was not too hard, the blankets soft and warm, but the pillow was as stiff as a log of wood. It is as though the Guardians feel the casual must not have comfort everywhere. This policy is part of the determination to prevent a return visit. Thus, you may stretch your limbs, hug your arms under soft fleecy wool, but your head shall find no ease. To and fro—to and fro, you turn on that torturous pillow.

===Chinese===
他们发给我一床褥子、一只枕头、两条毯子，叫我自己搬进「囚室」——不祥的字眼。它叫我浑身一颤。我不明白为什么要用「囚室」这个词，除非它同监狱里那种一模一样到如此地步，连官方的幽默感也不好意思另起名目。不过，「小隔间」未尝不可一试；它不至于带这么凶的兆头。

我独自被撇在黑暗里，形单影只得很。头天夜里，我是被一片沉睡的众生压迫着；我被一种不许人安息的气氛包围着；可如今，在我这间囚室可怕的孤绝里，我的灵魂竟渴念起那些女人做伴——连同她们那些说不清的包袱、兔皮、她们的「猫皮」。

墙上高处开着一扇小小的圆窗，像一扇舷窗，远非我伸手够得着。门上有一个小小的观察孔活板；时不时给人掀开，外面走廊的灯光往里窥一眼。

褥子不算太硬，毯子柔软暖和，枕头却硬得像根木头。监护人委员会的意思似乎是：收容者不可处处得着安逸。这一政策是防止人再来的决心的一部分。于是，你可以伸展四肢，把胳膊拢在软绒绒的毛毯底下，脑袋却休想安生。翻过去——翻过来，你在那只受刑的枕头上辗转。

===Original===
The “cell,” slightly funnel-shape, is like a coffin, as it suddenly occurred to me. I felt myself entombed in an instant, cut off forever from the light of day. I wanted to scream—a sob choked my throat—I was getting hysterical—and I knew it. And over and above all this quaking of the flesh, and shrinking of the spirit, was another and more dreadful piece of knowledge. I knew that if I tried the handle of the door it would not open. *No handle was there.* I could not escape from my funnel-shaped coffin, I might beat my hands upon the wall, but I could not get free.

===Chinese===
那间「囚室」略呈漏斗形，像一口棺材——这个念头突然攫住了我。刹那间我觉得自己已被活埋，永远隔绝了天日。我想尖叫——一声呜咽堵在喉咙里——我要歇斯底里了——我自己知道。而在这肉体发抖、灵魂畏缩之上，还有一层更可怕的知识。我知道，倘若去试门上的把手，它绝不会开。*门上根本没有把手。*我逃不出我这口漏斗形的棺材，我可以双手捶墙，但我出不去。

===Original===
I felt that if I went to the door and ratified my instinctive suspicion I should be unable to control myself. So I kept quiet and would not go to see what I feared was true. But in the grey dawning when I was quieter—though I had not slept—I crept out and went towards the door. And it was even as I had thought.

===Chinese===
我觉得，若是走到门边、证实了我本能的疑惧，我就会管不住自己。于是我安安静静待着，不肯去看我担心是事实的那个事实。但到了灰蒙蒙的黎明，人平静了些——虽说一夜没睡——我爬起来，挪到门边。果然同我想的一模一样。

===Original===
Some months ago there was a case in the police court, where it was alleged an inmate of a casual ward was “locked” in her cell. The superintendent stated upon oath that this was not so. “There are,” he said, “no keys.”

I know of no better example of the letter of the truth—and the violation of the spirit. There are no keys, but, as I have said, when the casual is duly in bed, the handle of the door is withdrawn.

===Chinese===
几个月前治安法院有一桩案子，有人指称一名临时收容病房的在押者被「锁」在她的囚室里。主管人当庭起誓说并非如此。「这里，」他说，「没有钥匙。」

我想不出更好的例子来说明何为字面上的真实——与精神上的违背。钥匙确实没有，可是如我所说，收容者按部就班躺上床后，门上的把手便被卸走了。

===Original===
The official explanation of this device would appear quite reasonable. It is said that if people could open their doors and come out of their cells, they would visit each other all night. One or two convivial spirits might, perhaps, drift into the corridor, but for the most part the casual is so dog-tired that any such spirit of enterprise is knocked out. But even were every casual to emerge, an attendant on night duty could shoo them back with the warning that if they came out again they would be shut in. It is as I have said, the Guardians do not desire to extend hospitality too often. Therefore, they inflict slight penalties upon the body and the soul, which, in the aggregate, make up a sum sufficiently imposing to make a night in the ward a thing most strenuously to be avoided.

===Chinese===
这一装置的官方解释听上去相当合理。据说，倘若人们能自己开门出屋，她们会整夜互相串门。也许有一两个爱热闹的会飘到走廊里来，但大体而言，收容者累得跟狗似的，这点进取精神早被打掉了。退一步说，就算个个都出来，值夜的管理员也能把她们轰回去，警告说再出来就把她们关起来。正如我说过的，监护人委员会不愿意把好客之情施舍得过于频繁。因此，他们对肉体和灵魂课以些微的罚金，积少成多，聚成一笔足够可观的数目，叫人在病房过一夜成为避之唯恐不及之事。

===Original===
I had always known that the Guardians deliberately adopt this regime, that their chief intent is to keep casuals “out,” not to welcome them in. But to know a thing and to experience it is widely different. I wish some of the guardians could be “destitute” and try their own wards.

===Chinese===
我一向知道，监护人委员会是蓄意奉行这套体制的，他们的主要意图是把收容者拒之「门外」，而不是迎进门来。可是，知道一件事同经历一件事，相去不可以道里计。但愿哪位监护人也能「落魄」一回，尝尝他们自家的病房。

===Original===
We were aroused the next morning about half-past five. The cell door was open. I found my clothes outside the door and put them on in the dim light. I could see other figures also putting on their clothes up and down the corridor. It was a queer experience. Things always seem foreshortened in the half-light before the night is utterly faded away and the morning come. When we were all dressed we folded up our blankets and carried them with pillow and mattress to the end of the corridor from whence they were despatched to be fumigated. Outside each door the number is painted in bold figures—a discovery which somehow made me feel more than ever like a convict. We were then shepherded into the day room, a mournful place with bare boards, whitewashed walls and a long trestled table. There was no fire in the grate. Large tin mugs full of what was supposed to be tea were placed on the table together with slices of bread spread with a particularly distasteful brand of “marge.”

===Chinese===
第二天早晨五点半左右，我们被唤醒。囚室的门开着。我在门外找到自己的衣裳，就着昏暗穿上了。走廊前后，我看得见别的人影也正在穿衣。这是一段古怪的经历。在夜色未尽、清晨未至的微明里，万物总显得透视变形。穿戴齐全后，我们把毯子叠好，连同枕头褥子搬到走廊尽头，从那里送去熏蒸消毒。每扇门外都用粗大的字体漆着编号——这一发现不知怎么使我比先前更像一名囚犯了。随后我们被领进日间活动室，一处凄凉的所在：裸木地板，刷白的墙，一张长支架桌。炉膛里没有火。桌上摆着一只只大锡缸，盛着据说是茶的东西，还有几片面包，抹着一个味道特别败胃的牌子的人造黄油。

===Original===
After a bad night every woman aches for a cup of tea, and I do not think I ever felt more disappointed than when I found the liquid in the tin mugs to be loathsomely lukewarm. I could have cried at this uncalled-for rebuff. I was prepared for weak or unsweetened, but not cold tea. This piece of foolish unkindness, as I have said, is now remedied. The tea made for the men is poured off into an urn which sings merrily on a gas ring until required.

===Chinese===
熬过一夜苦楚，哪个女人不渴一杯热茶；而发现锡缸里的液体温吞得令人作呕时，我想我从未那样失望过。这份无端遭遇的抢白，几乎叫我哭出来。茶淡、没糖，我都预备好了忍受，唯独没预备是凉的。如我所说，这桩愚蠢的刻薄如今已经改正。为男人们沏的茶倒进一把大水壶，在煤气炉圈上快活地唱着，随用随取。

===Original===
I tried to munch a piece of bread, but the marge was more than I could stomach, and feeling rather light in the head and unhappy about the heart, I sat on a form and observed my fellows.

===Chinese===
我试着嚼一片面包，可那人造黄油实在咽不下去；脑袋发飘、心里发苦，我便在长凳上坐下，打量起我的同伴们。

===Original===
The hour for leaving the House from the casual ward is somewhere about half-past seven. A good few were going out that morning and I listened with interest to their plans. One woman, young and good looking, was anxiously asking how she could get from Southwark to Dulwich, because she had no knowledge of London and possessed but a few pence. She was an ironer by trade and should have had little difficulty in getting work. It was Kitty Grimshaw who told her what to do.

===Chinese===
从临时收容病房离开济贫院的钟点在七点半上下。那天早晨有不少人要出去，我饶有兴味地听她们盘算行止。有一个年轻貌美的女人正着急打听怎么从萨瑟克去达利奇，她不认得伦敦的路，身上只有几个便士。她本行是熨衣工，照说不难找到活儿。告诉她该怎么办的，正是基蒂·格里姆肖。

===Original===
“Get the other side of Westminster Bridge, me girl,” said she, “and ask a likely looking chap the nearest way. As like as not he’ll give you a copper.”

The young woman departed, and her place was taken by a gaunt female who came to the same fountainhead for information.

She wanted to know the best casual wards on the road to Tunbridge Wells, and once more the fine old Irishwoman came to the rescue. Kitty has a strong, handsome face. She is over sixty, but quite upright, with a wealth of greyish hair and quick, humorous eyes. She is a most efficient woman and, as she told me, can plant a field of potatoes with any man, and is first on the list for a number of fruit pickers. She is well-known at Southwark, where she returns every month or six weeks. She has been on the road for some eight years, driven there by that economic pressure which has dehoused so many women. She lived for some time in a room in Kennington, supporting herself by daily housework, with occasional incursions into a laundry.

“And then, me dear, they wanted me room, so they could get more rent—and somehowes they managed it and I’ve been on the road ever since. A fine, healthy life it is, and many’s the helping hand I get, though—” she glanced at her feet, “boots is me trouble. You’re new to the House, me dear,” she added, “but don’t you worry, I’ll put you right, so that you’ll know what to do when you come again.”

===Chinese===
「过了威斯敏斯特桥再说，姑娘，」她说，「挑个体面模样的小伙子问最近的路。十有八九他会给你一个铜子儿。」

年轻女人走了，她的位置换上一个瘦骨伶仃的女人，也到这同一口活水源头来讨教。

她想知道去坦布里奇韦尔斯一路最好的临时收容病房，那位出色的爱尔兰老妇人又一次出手搭救。基蒂有一张坚毅漂亮的脸。她年过六十，腰板却挺，一头浓密的灰白头发，一双快捷风趣的眼睛。她是个极能干的女人——照她自己说——种起一田土豆来不输任何男人，还排在好几个摘果工名单的头一名。她在萨瑟克是熟面孔，每月或每六周回来一趟。她上路已有八年光景，是被那种把无数女人赶出家门的经济压力逼上路的。她先前在肯宁顿赁了一间房住，靠按日帮佣糊口，偶尔进洗衣房打一阵短工。

「后来呀，亲爱的，他们想要我这间房，好多收房租——也不知怎么一来就叫他们弄成了，打那以后我就一直在路上。这日子好，养人，帮我的人多着呢，只是——」她瞟了一眼自己的脚，「靴子是我的难题。你是头回进这家院，亲爱的，」她添了一句，「可别愁，我会教你上路，教你下回来了知道该怎么做。」

===Original===
It was at this moment that a short, stugger little woman with a red face claimed attention. She was very agitated and most aggrieved. It seems that her vest, which had been fumigated with the rest of her clothes, was missing from her bundle, and leave the House without that vest she would not. The attendant on duty, a kindly young woman, of great humanity and understanding, had hunted for it up and down the corridor, but nowhere could it be found. Various women were interrogated but not a shadow of a vest was forthcoming. The red faced lady would not budge; the vest was hers, and somehow or other it must be produced.

===Chinese===
正在这时，一个红脸膛、矮墩墩的小个子女人嚷嚷起来了。她又激动又委屈得不行。原来她的背心——同其余衣裳一道熏蒸过——在包袱里不见了，而没有那件背心她死也不出济贫院。值班的管理员是个厚道体谅的年轻女子，为它在走廊里上上下下找了个遍，哪儿也没有。好几个女人都被盘问了，可连背心的影子也没有一片。红脸膛太太寸步不让；背心是她的，说什么也得给找出来。

===Original===
“And right you are,” said Kitty, “and isn’t it the law? They take your clothes and if they lose your clothes you’ve got to have the value, and haven’t I had many a fight over the same thing and never have I been beaten.”

She tossed her head with its ample thatch, due, she assured me, to the constant use of vaseline—“twopence the box and well worth the price.”

Under Kitty’s behest a further, furious search was instigated, and at last the attendant, a little weary, brought back in triumph, the most begrimed thing in vests ever to be seen.

===Chinese===
「你对着呢，」基蒂说，「这不就是法律？他们收了你的衣裳，弄丢了就得照价赔，这一样事我斗过多少回，可从没输过。」

她甩了甩那头浓密的头发——她向我担保，这全亏常年抹凡士林——「两便士一盒，值当得很。」

在基蒂的号令下，又发起了一轮更加凶猛的搜查，末了，那位有点儿疲乏的管理员凯旋而归，捧回一件背心，其脏污程度堪称背心中的旷世奇观。

===Original===
“Here it is, Martha, and of all places in the world I found it in the men’s room.” She looked at the red-faced woman. “Have you been trying to get off?” she asked.

The sally was received with shouts of laughter. Such a little thing relieves the monotony in a casual ward! Everyone is so pathetically eager to break through the cold officialdom, which, for all the kindness of the attendants—and I found them very kind—is always present.

Martha, being comforted, went on her way, and I found myself left with Kitty and a big battered woman of about fifty, and two others.

===Chinese===
「在这儿呢，玛莎，天底下的地方不挑，偏在男病房里找着的。」她看着那红脸膛女人。「你是一直想溜出去会人吧？」她问。

这句俏皮话博得一片哄笑。临时收容病房里，这么一件小事就解了沉闷！人人都那样可怜巴巴地急着凿穿那层冷冰冰的官府气——虽说管理员们个个和气——我也确实觉得她们和气——那层官府气却总在。

玛莎得了安慰，上路去了；我发觉自己同基蒂、一个约莫五十岁饱经风霜的大个子女人，还有另外两个人留在了一起。

===Original===
Like Sterne’s starling, I was beginning to wish to get out. The walls seemed to be closing in on me. I got a little panic-stricken. Supposing this machine with which I had placed myself in contact should hold me against my will? Suppose they said that I must stay. Guardians have such plenary powers to use against the poor. I saw myself sentenced to remain permanently in an institution, I remembered with quick alarm the “tests” by which they measure your intelligence. They might easily find me mentally deficient!

===Chinese===
像斯特恩笔下那只椋鸟一样，我开始想出去了。四壁仿佛正朝我合拢。我有点慌了神。万一我把自己送进的这部机器要违反我的意愿扣住我呢？万一他们说非留下不可呢。监护人委员会对穷人有的是这样的全权。我看见自己被判终身滞留在一所收容机构里，我又惊又怕地想起他们用来测量智力的那些「测验」。他们满可以判定我智力欠缺！

===Original===
I went to the attendant and asked if I could go. It was then that the jaws of the trap began to close.

“You can’t go until tomorrow morning,” she said, “unless the superintendent gives you permission. According to law, you’ve got to give a day’s work for your lodging. You are due to go out on Sunday.”

“But—but I’ve got a chance of work. I may lose it if I’m kept here.” Already I could detect it in my own voice that rising note that speaks a nervous excitation. I realised that if I did not keep cool I must arouse official antagonism. Emotional display is terribly contagious in any form of institutional life, and at the first sign the official mind takes fright and closes down on the unfortunate pleader.

“The superintendent will be up about ten,” she said, “meanwhile you get on with some work.”

===Chinese===
我走到管理员跟前，问能不能走。就在这时，陷阱的颚开始合拢了。

「明天早晨之前走不了，」她说，「除非主管人准你。按法律，你得拿一天劳役换一宿床位。你该星期天出去。」

「可是——可是我有个工作的机会。要是扣在这儿，兴许就黄了。」我已经能从自己的嗓音里听出那种越拔越高的、说明神经亢奋的调门。我明白，若不保持冷静，必定激起官府的敌意。在一切收容机构的生活里，情绪发作传染得可怕，风声一起，官府的心思便受了惊，朝那倒霉的恳求者当头压下。

「主管人十点钟上下楼，」她说，「这会儿你先干点活儿。」

===Original===
She motioned me towards Kitty, and obediently I went and asked what I was to do. Kitty at that moment was cleaning the grate, heaving up great handfuls of ashes—they are not lavish with implements in the House—to a running commentary on life in general and her own adventures in particular.

“There’s nothing particular you can do,” she answered, “just you *look* busy, that’s what matters.”

I found this to be the case. The tables had already been scrubbed; the corridor washed; there were only the brass knobs on the doors that awaited attention. I polished and repolished with assiduity. But time hung heavy on my hands. A kindly attendant gave me the tip that the superintendent would not like to find me idle, so I traversed the corridor over and over again, loathing each separate handle, to which I applied Brasso with a new and instinctive dislike.

===Chinese===
她朝基蒂那边示意，我便乖乖过去问该做什么。基蒂那时正在收拾炉膛，一把一把掏着炉灰——济贫院里家什是不肯多给的——嘴里对世道人心乃至她自己的历险作着连珠点评。

「没什么特别可做的，」她答，「你只消*装出*忙的样子，要紧的就是这个。」

我发现果真如此。桌子早擦过了；走廊也洗过了；只剩门上的黄铜球形把手还等着人拾掇。我勤勤恳恳擦了又擦。可时间在手上沉甸甸地坠着。一位好心的管理员给我透了风：主管人见我闲着要不高兴的。于是我在走廊里走了一趟又一趟，憎恶着那一个个把手，往上面抹擦铜水（Brasso）时，怀着一种新生的、本能的嫌恶。

===Original===
I think I must have looked not very well, for presently the battered woman—her name was Ellen—beckoned me mysteriously.

“Kitty’s got some hot tea made for you, dear. I put it in your cell; drink it up, I’ll see that nobody catches you.”

To this hour I do not know what special pains and penalties were risked by Kitty and the kindly Ellen in the doing of this act of mercy. But whatever punishment they might have incurred they did not worry, and out of the largeness of their hearts, without a thought of themselves, they got me what I wanted that moment most in all the world.

The tea was hot and sweetened, and as I drank, vitality swept back into my blood. Kitty, it seemed, had a small store of groceries concealed somewhere on her person, or on the premises, from which she drew when occasion required. With the genius of her race she had already enlisted supporters all over the building, and Ellen, transmitting her desire, had induced a man on the next floor to supply hot water—tact did all the rest.

===Chinese===
我想我气色一定很不好，因为不多会儿，那位饱经风霜的女人——她叫埃伦——神神秘秘地朝我招手。

「基蒂给你弄了点热茶，亲爱的。我搁你囚室里了；快喝了，我盯着，不叫人逮着你。」

直到今天我也不知道，基蒂和好心的埃伦行这一桩善举，冒的是什么样的罪名与责罚。但无论会招来什么惩处，她们全不在意，凭着心胸的宽大，把自身安危置之度外，替我弄来了那一刻全世界我最想要的东西。

茶是热的，加了糖，喝下去，生机轰然涌回血液。看样子，基蒂身上或这院里什么地方藏着一小批杂货，需用时便取出来。凭着她那个种族的天才，她早已在全楼纠集起一批支持者；埃伦传递她的心愿，说动了上一层楼的一个男人供应热水——其余的靠圆通办妥。

===Original===
I had a chat with Kitty after this refreshment and she warned me very solemnly to avoid certain casual wards.

“Some are good, some are bad, but I manage to get on with them all, except Tonbridge. I can’t never go to Tonbridge—never again.”

It sounded strangely ominous. I was intrigued as to the fate that presided at this place of doom.

“What happened, Kitty?” I asked.

“It’s the law, me dear,” she said, “that the Master mustn’t put you to work on an empty belly; neither must you be put out of the House on an empty belly, and who should know this better than meself. There’s another law, me dear, the Master shan’t put you to do any of his private work, unless he pays for the same. An’ there was a certain woman that came in with me, an’ she told the master that she was by trade a laundress. ‘Then you’ll suit me,’ he says, ‘for I’ve a sight of washing that wants doing.’ ‘An’ I’m not doing your washing,’ says the woman, ‘an’ me wanting food.’ The Master shouted at her, but she was obstinate, an’ I’m not blaming her.”

“ ‘An’ ye’ll do me washing,’ says he, ‘or ye’ll go to gaol.’ ‘An’ it’s to gaol I’ll go,’ says she, an’ for all I told her what to say she sat there an’ she wouldn’t speak. She was given in charge, brought before the Bench an’ as I’m a living sinner they gave her fourteen days. The Master never said a word it was his own washing, an’ the poor creature never had the wit to speak up for herself.”

===Chinese===
用过这顿接济，我同基蒂聊了一阵，她极其郑重地警告我避开某几处临时收容病房。

「有的好，有的坏，可我同哪家都处得来，只有汤布里奇不行。汤布里奇我再也不能去了——再也不能了。」

这话听着出奇地不祥。主宰着那处绝命之地的是什么劫数，我起了强烈的好奇。

「出什么事了，基蒂？」我问。

「是这么条法律，亲爱的，」她说，「院长不许叫人空着肚子干活；也不许空着肚子把人撵出院门，这条谁该比我更清楚。还有一条法律，亲爱的，院长不许派你做他自己的私活，除非他照价给钱。跟我一块儿进来的是个女人，她告诉院长她本行是洗衣妇。『那你正合我用，』他说，『我有一大堆衣裳等着洗。』『你的洗衣我不洗，』那女人说，『我还等着饭吃呢。』院长冲她嚷，可她倔得很，我也不怪她。」

「『你就得给我洗衣裳，』他说，『不然就进监狱。』『进监狱就进监狱，』她说，我把该说的话都教了她，她还是坐在那儿，就是不开口。她被送了官，带上法官席，我是个活着的大罪人，他们真判了她十四天。院长半个字没提那是他自己的衣裳，那可怜人也蠢，不会替自己声辩。」

===Original===
“And how did you come into the story, Kitty?”

“You wait an’ listen, me dear. When he was through with giving the poor thing to the police, he comes to me an’ wants to start me washing the floor. I didn’t argue, I just said, quite quiet, ‘But, Master, that’s not the law, an’ none should know it better than you—an’ me belly is empty an’ on an empty belly the law says you mustn’t put me to work. An’ I’ll be pleased an’ proud to tell the Bench the same.’ ”

“He scowled at me, but I had me bit of cheese before I scrubbed. I wasn’t bearing him any ill-will, but he got a spite against me, an’ when it was time for me to leave I had a cup of cold gruel, though something hot in the way of tea is the right of ev’ryone before they leave the House. He wouldn’t give me any, so I took the gruel an’ hides it in the garden, an’ then I walks out an’ I asks if there’s anyone can send me to a committee lady, an’ don’t you forget, me dear, that’s what you’ve got to do. Always go to a committee lady. I found one right enough, an’ I asked her very civil if she’d be kind enough to step up to the House an’ see me breakfast. An’ she came, God bless her, an’ I showed her me gruel. She put the Master in his place an’ stood by while I had me cup of tea, an’ she wished me a pleasant good morning an’ gave me sixpence. But—”—Kitty spat on her blacklead brush—“I can’t never go to Tonbridge again.”

===Chinese===
「那你自己是怎么搅进这故事里的，基蒂？」

「你别忙，听着，亲爱的。他把那可怜人交给警察，完事了，就过来要支使我洗地板。我不争论，只慢条斯理地说：『可是院长，法律不是这样的，这您该比谁都清楚——我这肚子还空着，法律规定空肚子不许派活。这条话我乐意得很、也体面得很，照样讲给法官席听。』」

「他朝我瞪眼，可我擦地之前先吃了自己那一小口奶酪。我对他并无恶意，他却记恨上了我；到我该走的时候，只给我一杯冷稀糊，虽说按规矩，临出院前每人该得一口热乎的茶。他不给我，我就端了那杯稀糊，把它藏在园子里，然后走出门去，问有没有人能领我去见一位女委员——你可记牢了，亲爱的，你该做的就是这一条。有事先找女委员。我找是找着了一位，客客气气求她行个好，上院里来亲眼看我吃早饭。她真来了，上帝保佑她，我把那杯稀糊给她看。她把院长劈头一顿好训，站着看我喝完那杯茶，和和气气祝我早安，还给了我六便士。可是——」——基蒂朝她的黑铅刷上啐了一口——「汤布里奇我再也不能去了。」

===Original===
But for Kitty’s entertainment time would have been a heavy burden. There is nothing so enervating to the spirit as a repetition of totally useless work, and the only alternative to the re-scrubbing of tables already scrubbed, the re-polishing of handles already shining, is to pick oakum. This is of all tasks the most cruel. It tears the finger nails and soils the soul; it has no value, social or economic. Oakum is employed, it is true, as hospital swabs, but, like all mechanical, impersonal labour, it is untouched with any satisfaction. Oakum-picking is part of the system deliberately designed to deter people from claiming a night’s lodging to which, as members of a community heavily rated, they are entitled.

===Chinese===
若没有基蒂的消遣，时间会是一副沉重的担子。再没有比重复全然无用的劳作更耗损精神的了；而除了把擦过的桌子再擦一遍、把锃亮的把手再擦得锃亮之外，唯一的替代便是拣麻絮（oakum）。这是一切苦役中最残忍的一种。它撕烂指甲，玷污灵魂；它没有任何社会或经济价值。麻絮确实可用作医院的纱布卷，但正如一切机械的、非人的劳动一样，它不带丝毫满足感。拣麻絮是那套蓄意设计的制度的一部分，目的是吓退人们去索取一晚的床位——那床位，作为重税社区的一员，他们本是应得的。

===Original===
The same system compels the compulsory detention of any casual until the morning of the second day. There could be no ethical or material objection to any woman doing two or three hours’ useful work. This, however, is not the object, which is to undermine all feelings of self-respect, and implant in the mind the belief that poverty is a crime which must be heavily punished. Not by any active or deliberate cruelty, but by the imposition of futile yet degrading denials. This denial of liberty, this abnegation of freedom, is so insistent that only in the last resource will a London outcast go into the House. The case of women on the road is different and will be subsequently dealt with.

===Chinese===
同一套制度还强制扣押每一名收容者到第二天早晨。让一个女人做两三个钟头有用的活计，无论在道义上还是实利上都无可指摘。然而这不是目的所在；目的是铲除一切自尊，并在心里植下这样的信念：贫穷是一桩罪，必须严惩。不是用任何主动的、蓄意的残忍，而是用强加的、徒劳而屈辱的克扣。这种对自由的褫夺、对自由的弃绝，如此咄咄逼人，以致伦敦的落魄者不到山穷水尽，绝不肯进济贫院。路上女流浪者的情形则不同，容后再叙。

===Original===
Following on the publication in a Sunday newspaper of my article dealing with the Casual Ward, a revision of the rules has taken place. Oakum picking has been abolished for both male and female casuals, and the latter are now permitted to spend two or three hours daily in washing and mending their clothes and attending to their persons. These alterations, with the provision of hot tea at breakfast, already referred to, stand to the credit of the Metropolitan Asylums Board, and though there is still much which awaits reform, these alterations afford me and my fellow casuals cause for thankfulness.

===Chinese===
自一家星期日报刊登了我那篇谈临时收容病房的文章之后，规章做了一次修订。男女收容者的拣麻絮之役一概废除，后者如今获准每天花两三个钟头洗补衣裳、拾掇自己。这些改动，连同前面说过的早餐热茶的供应，记在大都会收容事务局的功劳簿上；尽管尚有许多有待改革之处，这些改动已使我同我的落魄姐妹们心怀感激。

===Original===
The arrival of the superintendent, a pretty woman, with bobbed hair, aroused my jaded energies. I seized on a duster and rubbed at a door handle, breathing heavily on the brass. My request was explained to her by an attendant official.

“Have you any hope of work?” she asked.

“At Ealing,” I answered, “I am sure of a day’s charing.” Ealing was the first place I could think of.

===Chinese===
主管人到了——一位标致的女子，剪着齐耳短发——唤起了我疲沓的精力。我抓起一块抹布擦拭一个门把手，朝铜面上重重呵着气。一位值勤官员向她转达了我的请求。

「你有找到工作的指望吗？」她问。

「伊灵，」我答，「那儿有一天打杂帮佣的活，是稳的。」伊灵是我当时想得起来的头一个地名。

===Original===
“Have you been here before?” she asked.

“It’s the first time I have ever slept in a casual ward.”

“In that case I’ll let you go. But you must understand if you come back here within a month you will have to stay for *three* days; if you return next month, you will have to stay the usual time—a day and a night. On no account will you be let off again.”

===Chinese===
「你从前来过这儿吗？」她问。

「这是我这辈子头一回在临时收容病房过夜。」

「既然这样，我放你走。但你必须明白，一个月内你再回来，就得留住*三*天；下个月再回来，就得照常留宿——一昼一夜。无论如何不会再放你了。」

===Original===
I wanted to blaze out my sense of the injustice of this rule, but I remembered in time that she was but the instrument and not the law, and I realised that the Superintendent, like the other officials, was far more humane than the institution which they served. Besides, I was beginning to have a wholesome fear of the State; I did not want to be thrust back into that awful cell. The thought of a second night in that funnel-shaped coffin appalled me. I hurriedly thanked her, and turned towards the office to get my hat and coat, powder puff and pence.

===Chinese===
我真想把这规矩的不公痛痛快快嚷出来，可我及时想起，她不过是器具，不是法律；我也明白，这位主管人同其他官员一样，远比他们所供职的机构更有人性。何况，我开始对国家生出一股有益的畏惧；我不想被推回那间可怕的囚室。在那口漏斗形棺材里再过一夜的念头吓坏了我。我匆匆谢过她，转身去办公室取我的帽子、外衣、粉扑和铜子儿。

===Original===
But I was not to leave the house without further proof of loving kindness. The battered Ellen came after me and slipped into my hand a thing most valued in the outcast life—*a piece of soap*.

===Chinese===
然而我离开这院子之前，还要再领受一份爱心的凭证。饱经风霜的埃伦追上来，往我手里塞进一样落魄生活里最贵重的东西——*一块肥皂*。

===Original===
I followed the attendant down the stone stairs, a very nice young woman, whom I shall always remember with gratitude. She advised me to go to the Labour Exchange and said that daily chars were in demand and she wished me good luck with a bright smile.

The door clanged behind me and I went out into the bleak, raw day, feeling as if I had escaped from the grave. I reeled almost with the sense of liberty, a liberty that my hunger, weariness and great distress could not embitter. I understood then why it is that humanity dreads what is known as organised relief. I contrasted the ghastly regulations of the Workhouse with the warm, unfettered welcome of the Salvation Army, and I knew that if I found myself again in such a plight rather than go to the casual ward I would spend the whole night walking the streets.

===Chinese===
我跟着那位管理员走下石阶——一个很好的年轻女子，我会永远怀着感激记起她。她劝我去劳工介绍所，说按日打杂的女工正缺人，然后笑容明亮地祝我好运。

门在我身后哐当一声，我走进阴冷生硬的白昼，只觉得自己像从坟墓里逃了出来。我几乎被自由的感觉冲得脚步踉跄——一种我的饥饿、疲惫和巨大愁苦都败坏不了的自由。我那时才懂得，人类为什么惧怕所谓有组织的救济。我把济贫院那套森然的规条，同救世军温暖无拘的欢迎放在一处对照，便知道：倘若再落到这般田地，我宁可整夜在街上走，也不进临时收容病房。

===Original===
And if I felt this in an institution characterised by the humanity of the officials, how intolerable must be the bitterness of a House ruled by insentient force? I thought of Kitty standing up before the master at Tonbridge and fighting the Law, and in my first flush of recovered independence I saluted that fine old warrior battling, not for her belly alone, but for the bellies of others.

===Chinese===
倘若在一个人性尚存于官员的机构里我尚且有此感受，那么一座由无情暴力统治的济贫院，其苦涩又当如何难堪？我想起基蒂在汤布里奇的院长面前挺身而立、为法律而战的身形；在重获独立的头一阵热流里，我向那位出色的老战士致敬——她战斗，不只为自己的肚皮，也为别人的肚皮。

===Original===
There is, I understand, a Union of Poor Law Officials, who, apart from their work of obtaining decent wages and conditions for the members, are steadily striving to alter the regulations governing casuals. In this they are helped by individual guardians. But on the whole Boards have developed little consciousness since the days of Bumble. They have no souls to save nor bodies to be kicked and, while in London, at any rate, superintendents, male and female, have lost that sense of brutal superiority condemned by Dickens, their superiors have remained untouched. In the process of economic evolution, the soul of Bumble has ascended to a higher social plane.

===Chinese===
据我所知，有一个济贫法官员联合会，除了为会员争取像样的工资与条件之外，也在扎扎实实地谋求修改管辖收容者的规章。这件事上，他们得到个别监护人的襄助。但总的说来，各委员会自班布尔的时代以来并没有长出多少良心。他们没有灵魂可救，也没有皮肉可踢；伦敦的主管人——无论男女——固然已经丢掉了狄更斯痛斥的那种粗暴的优越感，他们的上司却依然故我。在经济演化的进程中，班布尔的灵魂已然升入了一个更高的社会层面。

===Original===
I left before the midday meal, which consists of potatoes, bread, and a little cheese. The evening meal is skilly. The casuals have no tea, except in the morning. I rejoice to think that on this morning on which I write the women at Great Guildford Street have their tea hot; a small thing—but to them a great feat to have accomplished.

===Chinese===
我在午饭前离开了，那顿饭是土豆、面包和一点奶酪。晚饭是稀糊（skilly）。收容者们除早晨外没有茶。想到我落笔的这个早晨，大吉尔福德街的妇女们喝上了热茶，我很欢喜；一件小事——但对她们来说，是一场了不起的胜利。

## IX Kitty and the Widow Who Drugged / 九 基蒂与服药的寡妇

===Original===
What is it that drives women to the Casual Ward? The unthinking will answer at a venture, drink, depravity, or in exceptional cases, persistent misfortune. The class of women which seeks the shelter of the House in Great Guildford Street is drawn from London and the country. The former, in many cases, are casual in every sense of the word. Sometimes an entire family is landed miles away from their home, having lost the excursion train. In such case the police send them to the Metropolitan Asylums Board Office, under the arches of Hungerford Bridge, on the Victoria Embankment. They are given tickets of admission to the workhouse, and the mother and children are put together, while the husband goes to the men’s ward.

===Chinese===
是什么把女人们赶进临时收容病房？不动脑子的人会信口答：酗酒、堕落，个别情况是接二连三的厄运。到大吉尔福德街那座济贫院寻求庇护的这一类女人，伦敦和外乡的都有。前者有很多是字面意义上的「临时」——一家老小有时误了游览专车，被撂在离家好几里地的地方。这种情形下，警察会把他们送到维多利亚堤岸亨格福德桥桥洞下的大都会收容事务局办事处。他们领到济贫院的准入票，母亲和孩子安置在一处，丈夫则进男病房。

===Original===
Sometimes a woman suspected of professional begging gets an order for the Casual Ward with her baby, in which case the officials communicate with the Royal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children. The Society has no jurisdiction inside the House, but an inspector will lurk outside, and, on the appearance of the suspected with her children, he will march her off to the police court, where he will apply for a summons against her for neglect. In the majority of instances she will go to prison for a month.

This seems to me to be one of the greatest abuses of the system. It is not only the professional beggar who may be seized on, towards the poor woman who comes in with her baby in a verminous or dirty condition, the same procedure is enacted; having enjoyed the respite of a night’s shelter, the unfortunate creature is seized on as she emerges with her child.

===Chinese===
有时，一个被疑为职业乞讨的女人会领着婴儿拿到一张进临时收容病房的单子；这种情形下，官员们会知会皇家防止虐待儿童协会。协会在济贫院内部没有管辖权，但一名督察会埋伏在外，等被疑者领着孩子一露面，便把她押去治安法院，申请一张玩忽照料罪的传票。多数情形下，她会进监狱蹲一个月。

在我看来，这是这套制度最大的弊病之一。会遭此毒手的还不止职业乞丐；对一个抱着婴儿、衣虱肮脏进门的可怜女人，同一套程序照样上演；享受过一夜栖身的喘息，那不幸的人带着孩子一出门，便被拿下。

===Original===
In some few cases professional beggars may be neglectful or unkind to their children. Personally, I have many times tried to get a definition of the term “professional.” So far as I can gather the authorities lay it down that a “professional” beggar is one who gets his living by the getting of alms. An itinerant street seller who occasionally begs does not come under the same heading. It must be remembered, however, that if a woman with young children stands in the street selling matches, or some other article, she runs the risk of being charged with exposing her children, and once again prison is the conclusion of the whole matter.

===Chinese===
在少数案例里，职业乞丐也许确有疏于照料或虐待子女的。就我个人而言，「职业」一词的定义我讨过许多回。就我所能打听的，当局定下的界线是：「职业」乞丐者，以乞讨所得为生计之人。偶尔行乞的流动街头小贩不归此类。然而须记住：一个女人带着幼童站在街头卖火柴或别的什么货色，就担着被控「置子女于险境」的风险，而到头来，结局又是监狱。

===Original===
What, then, is the “professional” beggar to do? The root cause of this attempt to gather a livelihood is the housing shortage. It is not possible to pay the rent demanded for even the poorest room unless one member of the family is in receipt of a steady wage. Many of these beggars have lost their husbands, and have no trade by which they can support their family. Even if they had a trade they would have no place in which to ply it. For once a family has been dispossessed of a roof tree, it is unimaginably difficult for them to find another resting place. Wherefore these professional beggars alternate between the streets, the cheaper doss houses, the casual ward and prison. Generally speaking, during one of these terms in gaol, the accused’s children are compulsorily adopted. A law exists which empowers the Guardians to take over children without the consent or even knowledge of the parents, who have no right to see them, or even to know their whereabouts until they reach the age of fourteen. Some very pitiful cases have come to my knowledge where a woman, forced by circumstances out of her home, has been charged with neglecting her children, has been sent to prison, and has come out to find that she is childless. Her babies have been taken away from her, and she will see them no more.

===Chinese===
那么，「职业」乞丐该怎么办呢？这种谋生尝试的根由是房荒。除非家里有一人挣着稳定的工资，否则连最破的房间要的房租也付不起。这些乞丐许多死了丈夫，又没有一行手艺养家。即便有手艺，也没有地方施展。因为一家人一旦被夺去了栖身之屋，再寻一个落脚处便难于登天。于是这些职业乞丐便轮转于街头、廉价宿夜所、临时收容病房和监狱之间。一般说来，就在其中一次服刑期间，被告的子女被强制收养。有一条法律授权监护人委员会无须父母同意、甚至无须父母知情即可接管孩子；父母无权探视，甚至无权知道下落，直到孩子满十四岁。有几桩极其凄惨的案例是我亲知的：一个女人被境遇逼出家门，被控疏于照料子女，下了狱，出来时发觉自己已无儿无女。她的婴孩被人从她身边夺走了，她再也见不着了。

===Original===
There are a certain number of casuals who belong to a very different section of society. Women of position, who from time to time find themselves without resource, prefer a lodging in the House to a night in the streets. A woman of education and character visits Great Guildford Street every two or three months, and remains her allotted time of two nights and a day. She is the widow of a Government official and in receipt of a good pension. She has acquired the drug habit, however, and to satisfy the craving runs through her money long before the next instalment is due. On receipt of her cheque, she will get her jewellery and clothes out of pawn, make some additions to her wardrobe, and take a decent lodging. And then, gradually, her possessions find their way to the pawnbroker, she is turned out of her room, her shillings dwindle to pence, and she finds herself utterly destitute. When she has completely exhausted the patience of all her friends and relations, she comes to the House, generally at the last lap of the quarter.

===Chinese===
也有一小部分收容者，来自迥然不同的社会阶层。有身份的女人，不时发觉自己山穷水尽，宁可到济贫院投宿，也不在街头过夜。有一位受过教育、品行端方的妇人，每两三个月来一趟大吉尔福德街，住满分配给她的两夜一天。她是一位政府官员的遗孀，领着一份优厚的抚恤金。然而她染上了药瘾，为了满足瘾头，下一期款项远未到期，钱便花了个精光。一拿到支票，她就把首饰和衣裳从当铺赎出来，再添置几件，赁一处体面的住所。然后，渐渐地，她的家当一件件进了当铺，她被房东扫地出门，先令缩水成便士，她发觉自己一贫如洗。等把亲友的耐心耗了个干净，她便进济贫院来——通常是在季度将尽的关头。

===Original===
I met her once at the Relieving Office under Hungerford Bridge, and she told me in her quiet, well-bred manner she was going to the Casual Ward that night, as she felt she must have a hot bath. The effects of the dope were wearing off, and her pleasant, cultured voice had its inevitable effect.

Everybody likes her at the House, and she is as popular with the casuals as with the officials. I saw her again one day in the West End, beautifully dressed and evidently in the receipt of her pension. She greeted me most charmingly, and asked me to tea. A few days after I knew she would reappear in rags, and later on go in as a casual.

===Chinese===
我在亨格福德桥下的救济发放处见过她一回。她以她那安静而有教养的方式告诉我，当晚她要进临时收容病房，因为她觉得非洗个热水澡不可。药劲正在消退，她那悦耳斯文的嗓音照例起了它应有的效果。

济贫院里人人都喜欢她，收容者中间她人缘好，官员中间也一样。后来有一天我在西区又见到了她，衣着鲜丽，显然正领着抚恤金。她极其亲切地招呼我，请我去喝茶。我知道，几天之后她又会衣衫褴褛地露面，再往后，便又作为一名收容者进院来。

===Original===
Then there is a little woman who has had a lawsuit pending for years. She will produce masses of letters and documents—and, like Miss Flite, thrust them into your hand with impassioned vituperation of certain nefarious solicitors and perjured witnesses. She is an embroidress by day, but works only by fits and starts, lured by that mirage of wealth that has led many a poor soul to shipwreck. It may be that one day she will come into her own, or it is equally on the cards that she may throw herself into the river, and end her misfortunes. She is possessed by this one idea, and when she goes into the House, can speak of nothing but her case.

===Chinese===
还有一个小个子女人，一场官司缠身多年。她会搬出一大堆信件文书——像弗莱特小姐（Miss Flite）那样，把它们塞进你手里，痛骂某些奸诈的讼师和作伪证的证人。她白天做绣花活，却三天打鱼两天晒网，被那座财富的海市蜃楼诱着——那蜃景曾把多少可怜人引向覆灭。也许有朝一日她会得偿所愿，也同样有可能，她一头栽进河里，给自己的厄运画上句号。她被这一个念头攫住，进了济贫院，嘴里也只剩她的案子。

===Original===
Sometimes an elderly actress, long since out of work, comes to Great Guildford Street. She does not complain, but retails the story of her sufferings and her triumphs without comment. Now and again she gets a job in a “fit-up,” and tours through the country from village to village, playing at the local halls. Then again, there is a little woman, the wife, probably, of a professional man, who has outbreaks of dipsomania. On such occasions she will leave her home, sell what belongings she can carry, and steadily and blindly drink until the fit passes and she finds herself either in a doss house or derelict upon the pavement. It is at this point that, like a homing pigeon, she comes to Southwark, where her clothes are baked and generally tidied, and the rest restores her to something like her normal self. What happens when she goes back nobody knows, but, apparently, her husband always receives her—for every time she come to the casual ward she is wearing clothes which were once expensive and of a new and fashionable cut.

===Chinese===
有时，一位久已失业的老女演员也会来到大吉尔福德街。她不诉苦，只是不加评论地细数自己受过的苦与得意的往事。偶尔她在「简易班底」（fit-up，自带行头下乡巡演的剧团）里找到一份活，便一个村一个村地巡演下去，在当地的会堂登台。再者，还有一个小个子女人，多半是哪位专业人士的妻子，患间发性酒狂。每逢发作，她便离家出走，变卖随身带得动的衣物，没日没夜地盲饮，直到发作过去，发觉自己不是躺在宿夜所，就是瘫在人行道上。每到这一步，她便像信鸽归巢似的来到萨瑟克，衣裳经烘烤收拾，一番安歇使她大致恢复原样。回去之后情形如何，无人知晓；不过显然，她丈夫回回都收留她——因为她每回来进临时收容病房，身上穿的都是些曾经昂贵、新式时髦剪裁的衣裳。

===Original===
This strange and fitful company flit in and out of the great doors. But for them the House has not the terrors that it holds for the perennially destitute—those who have no abiding place, and for whom life has no foothold. I do not suppose the horror of the coffin-shaped cell ever affects the repose of the widow who drugs, the one who drinks, or those other transitory visitors who take the workhouse in their stride. But for the homeless it remains a thing of menace—a trap from which, once caught, they cannot escape.

===Chinese===
这一班古怪忽隐忽现的过客，在那两扇大门里翩然进出。对她们来说，济贫院并不具有它对常年赤贫者——那些没有立锥之地、生命无处落脚的人——所持有的恐怖。我想，棺材形囚室的骇人，从未搅扰过服药寡妇的安眠、酗酒妇人的清梦，以及其余那些把济贫院视若等闲的匆匆过客。可对无家可归者，它始终是一件威胁之物——一张陷阱，一旦落入，便脱身不得。

===Original===
Then we come to the women on the road—sturdy, fine specimens, who have become tramps from economic necessity. Such an one is Kitty Grimshaw, who was forced from her lodging in circumstances common to many. Kitty does a regular round, returning to Southwark as the central point. She has still friends among her former employers, and as she told me, could earn a decent living, if only she had a room. Physically fit and astonishingly witty, she is as clean a living woman as could be found.

===Chinese===
接下来再说路上的女人们——筋骨结实、品貌端正的人，纯粹出于经济所迫才做了流浪者。基蒂·格里姆肖便是其中之一，她是在一种许多人共同的境遇里被从住处逼出来的。基蒂走一条固定的巡回线，以萨瑟克为中心折返。她旧日的雇主中还有朋友；照她跟我说的话，只要有一间房，她就能挣一份体面的生活。身子骨硬朗，谈吐机智得惊人，走到哪儿找得出第二个生活得这样干净的女人。

===Original===
“Many’s the opportunity I get of seeing life,” she told me. “A man will tell me in the road, ‘Come on, Kitty, an’ give me a love, an’ there’s a sixpence for you.’ But I says to them, ‘keep your sixpence, I’ve a man of me own in the Navy, and, please God, I’ll be married next Christmas.”

===Chinese===
「我看人生百态的机会多着呢，」她告诉我。「路上会有男人跟我说：『来吧，基蒂，亲我一个，给你六便士。』可我对他们说：『留着你的六便士吧，我自己有个男人在海军里，托上帝的福，明年圣诞节我就要结婚了。』」

===Original===
She told me a great deal, about her “man.” He is a bo’sun’s mate, and her great anxiety is to keep from him that she is a tramp. He writes to her every few weeks and always sends a little money.
