“How do you get his letters?” I asked. It did not seem possible that the workhouse should act as a clearing station for correspondence.

“I pays an old lady sixpence a week to take his letters in, me dear. She lives at Peckham, an’ I call there every time I come to town.”

===Chinese===
关于她那个「男人」，她给我讲了许多。他是一名水手长副手（bo’sun’s mate），她最大的心事，就是瞒住他自己正在流浪这一节。他每隔几周给她写信，总还附一点钱。

「他的信你怎么收呢？」我问。济贫院居然兼营信件转运站，这似乎不太可能。

「我每礼拜付一位老太太六便士，替我收他的信，亲爱的。她住在佩卡姆，我每回进城都上那儿去。」

===Original===
I often think of Kitty with her big, bright eyes and unconquerable spirit. It’s a great thing to know that, though life has dealt her such heavy buffets and left her without a home—forcing her to tramp the roads with her small stock of matches, hairpins, etc.—she still preserves her capacity for happiness, her belief in the good things to come. What can a woman who, at sixty years of age, believes confidently that her man is coming home to marry her? I am not a sentimentalist, but I share Kitty’s faith in her sailor. I hope he will come back to her; my belief in humanity will receive a blow if he does not return.

===Chinese===
我时常想起基蒂，想起她那双又大又亮的眼睛和不屈的精神。知道这一点是极好的：生活虽给了她那么沉重的打击，害得她无家可归——逼着她揣着火柴、发夹那点小货色在路上奔波——她却仍保有感受幸福的能力，保有对将来的好日子的信念。一个六十岁的女人还笃信她的男人会回家来娶她，你还能对她多要求什么呢？我不是个滥情的人，可在她那位水手身上，我同基蒂一样抱着信心。我盼望他回到她身边；他若不回来，我对人类的信念要挨一记闷棍。

===Original===
It was Kitty who put me wise as to the advantages and disadvantages of certain local workhouses. But whatever their varying degrees of discomfort, one and all prescribe the same penalty for poverty. You have to pay for your night’s bed by a day and night’s detention. It is argued that were this punishment abolished, the Houses would be flooded with the destitute. This I deny—for however the externals may be softened, the interior fact remains the same. You are in an institution, the machinery of which, if you are not wary, may catch you up—and once caught in the toils you may abandon hope.

===Chinese===
各处本地济贫院的利弊好坏，也是基蒂给我指点明白的。但不论它们的难熬程度如何参差，对贫穷开的罚金却家家一致：一宿床位，须拿一昼一夜的拘禁来偿付。有人说，废了这重惩罚，济贫院就要被赤贫者挤破门槛。这话我不认——因为无论外表如何软化，内里的事实始终如一。你身在一家机构之中，它的机器，你若不提防，便可能把你卷进去——一旦陷入罗网，你便可以死了心了。

===Original===
A second argument is that were the casual put to do economic work, it would be in defiance of trade union rules and regulations. I admit the validity of the contention within limits. It would obviously be improper to institute any kind of workshop or factory from which goods could be marketed. The same objection would hold good were the goods simply for local consumption. But there can be nothing to prevent the women being set to do jobs which would materially affect the well being of all casuals. They could wash the nightgowns, etc., used by the inmates or make new ones. They could hem tea cloths and dusters; they could make up garments from the rolls of material sent by the charitable. They could even knit shoes and frocks for the babies born in the Infirmary. Those with an aptitude for cooking, could work in the kitchen. Two or three hours constructive labour would amply pay for their bed, and at the same time conserve that human dignity to which we are all entitled. The imposition of useless, meaningless tasks has a humiliating effect; they are the outcome of deliberate design.

===Chinese===
第二种论调说，倘若叫收容者做有经济产出的活，便是违犯工会的规章条令。这主张在限度之内，我承认其成立。开设任何可以把货品推向市场作坊工厂，显然不合适。即便货品仅供本地消费，同样的反对也站得住。但派女人们做些切实关系全体收容者福祉的活计，则无可指摘。她们可以洗收容者们穿的睡袍等等，也可以缝制新的。她们可以给茶巾和抹布缝边；可以用善心人送来的一卷卷布料裁制衣裳。她们甚至可以给医务所里出世的婴儿织鞋织裙。有烹调天分的，可以进厨房。两三个钟头建设性的劳动，足偿床资而有余，同时保全那份我们人人有份的人的尊严。强加无谓、无意义的苦役自有其羞辱之效；那是蓄意设计的产物。

===Original===
These and similar reforms could be brought about without any derangement of the civil machinery. If the women who sit as Poor Law Guardians would only rid themselves of the belief that destitution is the outcome of moral delinquency, instead of the result of economic pressure, much could be accomplished. But the majority of women who hold official positions on public bodies are convinced that the casual is an economic, rather than a human problem, and should be dealt with at the cheapest possible rate. It seems to have passed the consciousness of the majority of citizens that any man or woman has the right to claim a bed, inasmuch as they have contributed to the rates. For rights are the last thing that the destitute are held to possess, and the pauper, casual or residential, is only regarded as a source of national trouble and expense.

===Chinese===
这些以及类似的改革，无须惊动行政机器分毫便可办到。只要那些出任济贫法监护人的女人们肯丢掉一个信念——赤贫是道德败坏的产物，而非经济压力的结果——便可大有作为。然而身居公共机构官职的大多数女人坚信：收容者是一个经济问题，而非人的问题，处理起来越便宜越好。任何男女既然缴过地方税，便有权讨一张床——这一点似乎越出了大多数公民的意识之外。因为权利是人们认定赤贫者最不配有的东西；而贫民，无论临时的还是长住的，只被看作国家麻烦与开销的一个源头。

===Original===
One last word I have to say regarding the Casual Ward. When the women are not engaged in the repetition of meaningless tasks, there is nothing for them to do in the way of recreation. Books are not provided—the day room did not boast even a single paper or magazine when I was there. With only bare walls on which to gaze, and nothing but weary hours to look forward to, it is to me a miracle that the mind of the woman who migrates from House to House does not become an utter blank.

===Chinese===
关于临时收容病房，我还有最后一句要说。女人们不重复那些无意义的苦役时，消遣上便一无可为。图书是没有的——我在的那阵子，日间活动室连一份报纸、一本杂志都夸耀不出。四壁光秃可供凝视，前途唯有倦怠的时辰，一个从这所济贫院迁往那所济贫院的女人，脑子竟没有变成一片彻底的空白——在我看来，这已是一个奇迹。

## X A Very Gallant Gentleman / 十 一位非常豪侠的君子

===Original===
To spend a whole night in the streets is an experience that has a permanent effect on the psychology.

My first adventure of this kind found me unprepared. I was compelled to walk about, not from lack of money to pay for a bed, but because I could not find any place that would take me in.

===Chinese===
在街头熬一整夜，是一种对心理留下永久烙印的经历。

我头一次遭遇这种事，全无准备。我被迫四处走动，不是因为付不起床位钱，而是因为找不到一个肯收留我的地方。

===Original===
It was on a Saturday night, and I had had a good day. I had got into touch with my friend of the plush coat, and had secured from her a number of cigarette cases which I had sold at profit to myself. After I had bought myself a meal and a glass of port, I was the proud possessor of three shillings and sixpence, and I felt entitled to a luxurious time. I had learned from my fellow outcasts that the beds at the Church Army Shelter in Great Peter Street were very cosy, and I anticipated an agreeable lodging. Sunday, I knew, there would not be much trade astir, so I could stay in bed as long as they would let me, buy myself a bit of lunch, and resume my commercial activities in the evening, reinvigorated and refreshed.

===Chinese===
那是一个星期六的晚上，我那天过得不错。我同长毛绒大衣夫人接上了头，从她手里弄到一批香烟盒，转手卖了，赚了一笔。给自己买了一顿饭、一杯波尔图酒之后，我骄傲地拥有三先令六便士，自觉有资格享受一段奢侈时光。我从落魄姐妹们那里听说，大彼得街教会军收容所的床铺非常舒服，我盼着一夜安适的投宿。我知道，礼拜天没什么生意可做，所以尽可以赖床赖到人家不许为止，再买一点午饭，养精蓄锐，晚上重新开张我的商业营生。

===Original===
Such was the programme I mapped out—and I recall to this moment the pleasurable feeling with which I sauntered down Whitehall. My open-air life, in spite of the biting weather, had given me an added strength, of which hunger and lack of sleep could not deprive me. After all, I thought, there is a lot to be said for the physical existence which consists of a continual struggle to provide the body with essentials and leaves untouched the field of the mind. This may sound fantastic, but it holds much validity. Later on, when you have led the life of the homeless at a continuous stretch, your imagination comes into play, and the old appetite for ideas returns, invigorated. But at the outset the material blocks all other avenues of approach; you want to feed your belly, as Kitty would say, and rest your limbs, and your body is clamorous until these things are accomplished.

===Chinese===
这便是我拟定的日程——直到此刻，我还记得自己悠悠然走下白厅时那份愉快的心情。我的露天生活，尽管天寒刺骨，却给了我一股额外的力气，饥饿和缺觉都夺不走。毕竟，我想，这种肉身的存在自有其一番道理：它 consists of（全副心思）用在源源不断地为身体挣得必需品上，心智的田地反倒无人惊动。这话听着玄虚，却大有道理。到后来，你一连许多时日过惯了无家可归的生活，想象力便开始运转，对思想的老胃口又回来了，而且更加健旺。可在起初，物质堵死了一切旁的门径；用基蒂的话说，你要喂饱肚皮，要让四肢歇息，这些事没办妥之前，你的身体吵闹不休。

===Original===
It was just after nine when I arrived at the Church Army Shelter. The house has an appearance of prosperity that a little chilled me. Through the wide open door I caught a glimpse of white paint and shining brass, which made me very conscious of my bedraggled skirts. But, after all, I had nothing to fear. I could pay for my bed, and I had experienced so much humanity, such ready kindliness in my travels, that I had forgotten the other side of the picture. A woman directed me to the Sister. I went along a passage, and turning to the right, found myself on a small landing, from which a flight of stairs led down into a very bright and pleasant little kitchen. A young woman, in official cap and apron, was frying sausages which sent forth a most appetising smell.

===Chinese===
我到教会军收容所时刚过九点。那房子一副殷实气派，叫我微微心冷。透过敞开的大门，我瞥见白漆和锃亮的黄铜，不由得万分意识到自己拖泥带水的裙子。可是毕竟，我没什么可怕的。床位钱我付得起；一路上我受了那么多人的善意、那么多现成的和气，竟把这幅图画的另一面忘了。一个女人指点我去找那位姊妹。我沿走廊走去，右一拐，来到一方小平台上，一道楼梯从那儿通下去，直通一间明亮宜人的小厨房。一个年轻女子，戴着制帽、系着围裙，正在煎香肠，香味诱人极了。

===Original===
I made to go down the stairs, but was stopped by a sudden gesture from the Sister. She waved a frying fork at me.

“Stop where you are,” she said. “Don’t come down here.”

As she spoke I felt that I was destitute in every sense of the word.

“Good evening,” I said, politely. “May I have a bed?”

“Stay where you are,” she repeated, and I went back to the little pen at the top of the stairs, surprised and hurt. I realised for the first time since I began my journey, how demoralising it is to be sized up on your external appearance, and the tears were very near my eyes when I asked again for a bed, and announced that I could pay for it.

===Chinese===
我正要下楼梯，姊妹一个突然的手势止住了我。她朝我挥着一把煎肉叉。

「站在那儿别动，」她说。「别下来。」

她说话的当儿，我感到自己在字面意义的每一点上都一文不名。

「晚上好，」我客气地说。「能给我一个床位吗？」

「站在那儿，」她重复道。我退回楼梯口那方小围栏里，又惊又痛。自上路以来我头一次认识到：单凭外表被人掂量，是怎样一件瓦解人心的事。当我再次讨一个床位、并声明我付得起钱时，眼泪几乎要夺眶而出。

===Original===
The statement did not impress the Sister, and under her eyes my shabbiness increased. Draggled coat and sodden hat grew visibly more and more demoralised.

“Please stay there.” The frying fork marked out the frontier. Then there followed a searching cross-examination, which I bore patiently and politely, under the belief that when the interrogatory was finished I should be allowed a bed. As I discovered later, however, my impression was wrong.

===Chinese===
这句话没能打动姊妹，在她的目光下，我的褴褛升级了。泥污的外衣和湿透的帽子眼见得越来越狼狈。

「请待在那儿。」煎肉叉划定了国境线。接着是一场刨根问底的盘问，我耐心而客气地受着，心里以为问完之后总会给我一个床位。然而我后来发现，这想法错了。

===Original===
“What is your name and occupation?”

“I am out of work,” I said.

“When were you in a regular job?”

“Some time since,” I answered, and felt almost criminal.

“Your address?”

“I haven’t one.”

“No permanent address?”

In face of what I had told her the question seemed irrelevant.

“Well then,” she faced me with frank hostility, “where did you spend last night?”

===Chinese===
「你叫什么，做什么营生？」

「我失业了。」我说。

「上一份正经工作是什么时候？」

「有些日子了。」我答，觉得自己几乎像个罪犯。

「你的地址？」

「没有。」

「没有固定地址？」

方才我都告诉过她了，这问题显得文不对题。

「那么，」她带着毫不掩饰的敌意正对我，「你昨天夜里睡在哪儿？」

===Original===
I had to tell her the truth. I could not frame a suitable lie, besides this was a case when, it seemed to me, fiction would not help matters. The Church Army Shelter existed, I had been told, to supply beds at tenpence a night for destitute women. Why, then, should I shirk the admission of a palpable fact? And yet I felt somehow that my answer would do me out of the comfortable bed I needed so badly.

She seemed shocked at the mention of the casual ward, and positively bristled when I confessed to a common lodging house.

“Oh no,” she said, “I can’t give *you* a bed,” and I knew that the words were the spontaneous expression of her feeling. She did not want me and my bedraggled clothes in her bright, clean kitchen. She had no use for a woman who quite recently had lodged in Kennedy Court, and she hurled at me the condemnation of a whole world when she turned me down.

===Chinese===
我只好说实话。我编不出一个合适的谎话，何况在我看来，这种情形下虚构也于事无补。人家告诉我，教会军收容所的存在，就是为了给穷困的女人提供每晚十便士的床位。那么，一个明摆着的事实，我何必讳言？可不知怎么，我预感到我的回答会叫我失去那张我急需的舒服床铺。

一听临时收容病房三个字，她像是吓了一跳；等我招认住过公共寄宿旅舍，她简直毛发倒竖。

「哦，不行，」她说，「我不能给*你*床位。」我知道这话是她心意的自发现。她不要我、不要我这一身泥污衣裳进她明亮干净的厨房。一个不久前还落脚在肯尼迪大院的女人，她用不着；她回绝我的时候，把整个世界的谴责劈头掷向了我。

===Original===
I have been assured that the Church Army affords help to many destitute women. Facts and figures have been poured upon me. Statistics have set forth how many hundreds and thousands of homeless creatures have been fed and housed. This may be so, but there is an interior sympathy which expresses itself not only in statistics, and when I went to Great Peter Street that night I felt that spirit was not there.

And here I must say a word as to the moral effect of this particular treatment. I discovered that since I had left my home I had acquired a new psychology. I not only looked, but I felt destitute, and the Sister’s refusal struck me like a blow in the face. Why should she turn me down? I was clean, I was honest, I had the money to pay—and I needed a comfortable bed.

I suddenly comprehended with a dreadful clearness what destitution does to the soul. It destroys the sense of human dignity, and as your rags are, so you become. I was bedraggled, I looked miserable and I had not slept—anywhere, and the Sister in the pretty kitchen cooking supper did not want anything like *me* among the clean, white paint and shining brass fittings.

===Chinese===
有人向我保证，教会军帮助过许多穷困的女人。事实和数字向我倾泻过不少。统计表摆出过成千上万无家可归者得到食宿的数目。也许如此，可有一种发自内心的同情，不是单靠统计数字就能表达的；那晚我到大彼得街去，我觉得那种精神不在那里。

这里我必须说一句这种对待在道德上的效果。我发觉，离家之后我得了一种新的心理。我不仅是看上去穷，我是感觉上穷了；姊妹的回绝像一记耳光打在我脸上。她凭什么回绝我？我干净，我诚实，我付得出钱——而且我需要一张舒服的床。

我忽然以一种可怕的清晰懂得了穷困对灵魂做了什么。它摧毁人的尊严感；你的破衣烂衫是什么样，你就成了什么样。我泥污满身，一脸苦相，而且哪儿都没睡过——那位在漂亮厨房里做晚饭的姊妹，在她干净的白漆和锃亮的黄铜器件中间，不要任何像*我*这样的东西。

===Original===
I once heard a prosperous-looking man say something that expressed what the Sister at that moment must have felt. Someone was speaking with anger as to the terrible condition of the slums which breed deterioration of mind and body.

“That’s all very well,” said the prosperous one, “but after all they are dogs—let them live in their kennels!”

Well, I hadn’t got even a kennel, though at that moment I wanted to slink off and find one. But I wasn’t through yet. I did not want unjustly to condemn the Sister, and it was necessary that I should discover the type of lodger that was acceptable.

===Chinese===
有一次我听一个体面模样的男人说了句话，那话道出了姊妹那一刻必有的心思。当时有人正愤然谈起贫民窟的可怕境况——它滋生身心的溃败。

「道理都对，」那位体面人说，「可他们到底是狗——就让它们住狗窝好了！」

唉，我连个狗窝都没有，虽然那一刻我真想溜出去找一个。可我还没完。我不愿不公道地断罪那位姊妹，我得弄明白什么样的房客才算合格。

===Original===
I lurked in the passage near the entrance—lurking is one of the fine arts I learned in my wanderings—and watched the door to the kitchen like a lynx. Presently a nice-looking young woman carrying a despatch case walked briskly up the street and into the house. I judged her to be a shop assistant, who wanted to get away from living-in for a night. I watched her enter the little lobby and descend the stairs—no frying fork barred her way, and I listened to her conversation with the Sister.

She answered all enquiries satisfactorily and was fixed up for the night.

I left that house with a burning sense of injury.

===Chinese===
我在入口附近的走廊里潜伏下来——潜伏是我在流浪途中学会的绝技之一——像山猫一样盯住厨房的门。不多时，一个模样周正的年轻女子拎着公文包，脚步轻快地沿街走进屋来。我断定她是个店铺店员，想从包住的店里出来躲一晚。我看着她进了小门厅，走下楼梯——没有煎肉叉拦她的路——我听着她同姊妹的对答。

她有问必答，事事令人满意，当夜的铺位妥了。

我离开那座房子，心里烧着一团冤屈。

===Original===
It was not that I was penniless. I had been through that stage and had not scrupled to ask for help. When I was hungry I had begged. But now it seemed to me I was in an even more desperate condition. I had the money for a decent bed and I could not get one!

Had the Sister merely told me at the first that she had no accommodation I should not have been so hurt. But it seemed to me deliberate cruelty to interrogate me and then to tum me down. The fact that she did this effectively disposes of any suggestion that she knew from the first that there was no bed available, and that this was not so I subsequently learned.

By this time it was close on ten, and I did not relish the walk back to Holborn. Besides, I could not accept the possibility that only in Kennedy Court could I be taken in. Surely there was some other shelter?

===Chinese===
倒不是我身无分文。那个阶段我经历过，求援并不犹豫。饿的时候我讨过饭。可眼下这种境况在我看来更加绝望：体面床位的钱我有，床位却讨不到一个！

倘若姊妹一开口就告诉我没有铺位，我也不至于这样痛心。可她盘问了我一场，再把我回绝——在我看来，这是蓄意的残忍。她既这样做了，便有效地驳倒了「她从一开始就知道无床可供」的猜疑；而且我后来得知，事实也并非如此。

这时已近十点，我不情愿再走回霍尔本去。再说，我不能接受世上只有肯尼迪大院肯收我这一可能。总还有别的收容所吧？

===Original===
The stars were cruelly bright that evening, and the glory of the sky made my position more unhappy. I think I could have borne it better had it rained. I watched the electric signs on the Embankment dazzling in their shifting brilliance. Where was I to go? What on earth was I to do?

I went to the inevitable policeman and asked if he could tell me of a lodging house.

“There’s one in Belvedere Road, over the Bridge and turn to the right,” he told me, and I set off on my tramp.

===Chinese===
那晚的星亮得残忍，天空的壮丽愈发衬得我处境凄凉。我想，若是下雨，我倒还能好受些。我望着堤岸上的电光招牌在变幻的光华里耀眼明灭。我去哪儿呢？我究竟怎么办呢？

我去找那位免不了要找的警察，问他可知道有什么寄宿旅舍。

「贝尔维迪尔路有一家，过了桥往右拐。」他告诉我。我便上了路。

===Original===
There is always something to discover in the land of the homeless. You learn quite a lot about streets which invariably escape your notice in ordinary life. Belvedere Road seemed to me one of the longest in London; it is full of many and curious depositories. I passed the office of the State of India tucked in between a beer-house and something to do with deep sea fisheries. Large and imposing brass plates recorded the names of obscure Government departments, hoary with age and decrepitude.

There were not many people in the road that night, and those that I passed were respectably dressed and seemed to be hurrying home to a nice supper. When I had almost given up hope of its discovery I chanced on the lodging house.

===Chinese===
在无家可归者的国度里，永远有新东西可发现。你对街道的了解会大长——寻常日子里它们从不出现在你眼下。贝尔维迪尔路在我看是伦敦最长的一条；一路上满是各种古怪的库房堆栈。我路过印度事务部的办事处，它夹在一家 啤酒店和一个同远洋渔业有关的什么机构中间。大而气派的铜牌记录着一些名不见经传的政府部门的名字，老朽得须发皆白。

那晚路上人不多，擦肩而过的几个衣履体面，像是都赶着回家吃一顿好晚饭。就在我几乎断了找到它的念头时，寄宿旅舍撞到了眼前。

===Original===
A large, austere-looking building, the exterior did not discourage me—I was prepared to scale any physical barriers to get a bed. It was the chill of the spirit that held me back. The lodging house was under the auspices of the Church Army, and this paralysed my will. I ought, of course, to have applied for admission, when I might have chanced on a more kindly reception. It was, I admit, a piece of moral cowardice to turn away from a door, but my state of mind was very raw, my emotions had been badly twisted. Something told me I should be refused for the second time, and it is too much to ask of even destitute humanity to be twice crucified on the same cross.

===Chinese===
一座高大冷峻的房子，外表并没有吓退我——为了一张床，任何物理的障碍我都预备翻越。叫我却步的是心头的寒意。这寄宿旅舍也归教会军主办，这一下瘫痪了我的意志。按说我本该进去申请投宿，说不定还能碰上一副比较和善的面孔。我承认，从一扇门前掉头走开，是一桩道义上的怯懦；可我的心境正一片生疼，情绪被狠狠拧过一道。有什么告诉我，我将第二次遭到回绝——而要求一个落魄的人在同一副十字架上钉两回，未免太过分了。

===Original===
So I went off again on the tramp, making instinctively for a policeman.

“There’s a place where you may get put up somewhere near Southwark, in the neighbourhood of Union Street, I think.”

It was a cross-country journey, which you could not break by omnibus or tram; but I did not mind, for by this time the fascination of the streets had got me. There are two or three processes which you go through when you are homeless. At first when you have walked about for two or three hours you get very tired. Your head aches and your limbs are like lead; the fact that you have no fixed objective is like a pall on your spirits—you urge yourself forward on mere will. But if you keep on for another hour, or two, or three, your pains vanish—in some strange way you forget cold, hunger and thirst. Your brain is light, your feet move on air. The noises of the street form a monotonous accompaniment which gradually merges into silence; you see little, hear less, feel not at all. Trouble and regret fall from you—it is as though you were doped. You have no sense of distance or time, and gradually your movements become automatic. Instinctively you adopt the slouch of the tramp; you feel yourself one with the streets; you have lost your entity.

===Chinese===
于是我重新上路，本能地又奔一个警察去。

「有个地方兴许能安置你，在萨瑟克附近，好像是在联合街那一带。」

那是一段乡野式的跋涉，公共马车和电车都插不上手；可我不在乎，因为这时候街道的魅力已经把我抓住了。无家可归的人要经历两三个阶段。起初，走了两三个钟头，你疲惫不堪。头疼，四肢灌了铅；没有固定目的地这一事实像一方棺罩压在心上——你全凭意志把自己往前赶。可要是再走一个钟头、两个、三个，疼痛便消失了——你会以一种奇怪的方式忘掉寒冷、饥饿和干渴。脑子变轻了，脚踩着空气走。街上的嘈杂成了一支单调的伴奏，渐渐融进寂静里；你视而不见，听而不闻，浑然无觉。烦恼与悔恨从你身上剥落——仿佛服了麻醉剂。你没有距离感，没有时间感，动作渐渐变成自动的。你本能地操起流浪汉那副懒散步态；你觉得自己同街道合为一体；你已经失去了你的自身。

===Original===
I walked through many ages, as it seemed to me, until I chanced upon the place described by the policeman. By this time it was very late, and as it was Saturday, lodgings for the night were difficult to get. If you keep your eyes open on a Sunday morning you will find there are not many homeless creatures in the streets. Everyone, however desolate, makes a push to get put up, somehow or other, for the seventh day. For this reason the lodging house was full, but the management was very kind. The superintendent saw that I was done, and asked me if I would like to rest by the fire in the kitchen—the same communal kitchen which sheltered me in many parts of London.

===Chinese===
我仿佛走过了许多个时代，才撞上警察说的那个地方。这时夜已很深，又是星期六，当夜的铺位难讨。星期天早晨你若留心，会发现街上无家可归的人不多。人再孤绝，也要千方百计为第七天谋一个安身之处。因此这寄宿旅舍住满了，但管理人非常和善。主管人看出我垮了，问我愿不愿在厨房炉火边歇歇——正是那种在伦敦许多地方收留过我的公共大厨房。

===Original===
I was too tired to observe very closely, and with the understanding and good manners you always find among the destitute, I was not pestered with talk. I just sat by the fireside, feeling sorry for myself and all the rest. (This house is run by the *Christian Herald* Mission, and I found them very Christian. I visited the place later, and in due course shall have something to say on this count. But this story of my first night in the streets must not be broken in its continuity.) Very soon I went on the tramp again.

I could, I am sure, have sat there longer, but if you rest for more than a few minutes you get stiff, not only in body, but in mind, and the “doping” process I have mentioned, has to be gone through all over again. I explored many of the broad streets and fine roads of the district and gradually worked back to Blackfriars. It was past midnight, but near the Ring there was still a flaring whelk-stall round which some night birds were eating. There were no women among them. Women are not welcome at any kind of stall; indeed many coffee stall keepers will not serve you if you are alone, and even when you can persuade the proprietor to let you buy a cup of tea his manner is uncivil, even abusive. The most abject specimen of man is quite welcome if he has the pence to pay for his refreshment. But in the case of women there is the rooted belief that they must be bad lots or they would have a home; if they are not thieves they are prostitutes, and either way, even a commercial connection with them might cause trouble with the police.

===Chinese===
我累得没法细看；落魄者之间那种你总能遇到的体谅与礼数，也使我免于攀谈的搅扰。我就坐在炉边，为自己、也为其余一切人难过。（这房子由《基督教先驱报》布道团经办，我发现他们极有基督教精神。这地方我后来重访过，届时自有一番话可说。但我头一夜流落街头的故事，不可中断它的连贯。）不多时，我又上路了。

我确信本可以在那儿多坐一会儿，可你只要歇上几分钟以上，就会发僵——不光身体，连心智也僵，我说过的那套「麻醉」过程，就得从头再走一遍。我把这个区的许多宽街大路都探了一遍，渐渐折回黑衣修士。已过半夜，斗拳场（the Ring）附近还有一个灯火通明的螺肉摊，几只夜鸟围坐着吃。她们中间一个女人也没有。任何一种摊子都不欢迎女人；说真的，许多咖啡摊主见你独身一个女人，根本不卖给你；即便你劝得动掌柜的让你买杯茶，他的态度也无礼，甚至恶声恶气。男人里最卑贱的标本，只要付得出几便士的茶钱，都大受欢迎。可一到女人身上，就有那条根深蒂固的信条：她们必定不是好货，否则怎么没有家；不是贼就是娼，无论哪一种，哪怕同她们做一笔买卖，都可能招来警察的麻烦。

===Original===
When I hear eloquent and educated women declaim on the platforms as to the wrong done to our sex by some inequality of the law that gives the father priority of control over the child, or makes the husband responsible for his wife’s debts, my mind goes spinning back to those real disabilities which press on woman. Feminism might never have blared its trumpet for all the good it has done the derelict. Women have won the vote, it is true, but they have not won the right to decent lodging house accommodation, they have not won the right to purchase at a coffee stall a cup of tea or coffee or a penny bun. Their only hope of getting such refreshment, with civility, is to accompany a man. Woman qua woman among the derelict bears all the disabilities of her sex—and no one cares a pin about it.

===Chinese===
每当我听见口若悬河、受过教育的女人们在讲坛上慷慨陈词，说法律如何偏待我们性别——把对子女的优先监护权给父亲，或叫丈夫为妻子的债务负责——我的心便 spinning（飞转）回那些实实在在压在女人身上的障碍。女权运动就是从没吹响过它的大喇叭，对落魄女人又损失了什么呢。女人是赢得了选票，这不假；可她们没有赢得体面寄宿的权利，没有赢得在咖啡摊上买一杯茶、一杯咖啡或一个便士小面包的权利。她们想有礼貌地得到这样一点饮食，唯一的指望是身边带一个男人。落魄者当中的女人，作为女人，承受着她性别的全部障碍——而没有一个人为此操一个铜子儿的心。

===Original===
I have never been able to understand why because a woman is a prostitute—often because she is tired of being without a bed—Society should be held blameless in withholding from her the rights and privileges of an ordinary citizen. Rightly and properly, public indignation is felt and expressed if a man in any walk of life is refused a glass of beer in a saloon bar, on account of his shabbiness. But no one seems to think it unjust or even strange that a coffee stall keeper should “shoo” off a woman who wants to buy food or drink in the watches of the night. I have tested this particular attitude. I have been out late in my ordinary attire and have asked for a cup of coffee, and I have never been refused. Indeed, the same stall keeper who told me gruffly he did not serve women when I was destitute, was more than affable when I appeared decently dressed. He supposed I was having a look round for curiosity, and was quite ready to give me any number of legends as to his observations of life.

It was a curious experience and taught me very much. Once more I was conscious of the hideous standard which schedules you solely by externals, and cannot see beyond a woman’s bedraggled hat and sodden coat.

===Chinese===
我一直不能理解：为什么一个女人做了妓女——常常不过因为她睡不上床睡烦了——社会扣住一个普通公民的权利与特权，就可以免受责难。一个男人，不管哪一行的，因为穿得寒酸而被酒吧拒卖一杯啤酒，公愤便理所当然地生出、喊出。可一个深夜想在摊子上买点吃喝的女人，被咖啡摊主「嘘」地轰走，似乎没有人觉得不公，甚至没有人觉得奇怪。这种态度我亲身试过。我穿着平常的衣裳深夜出门要过咖啡，从没被拒绝过。真的，同一个摊主，在我落魄时曾粗声粗气地说他不招待女人，等我穿得体面了再去，和气得不得了。他当我出来好奇地逛逛，巴不得把他阅世的心得讲上三天三夜。

那是一次古怪的经历，教了我许多。我再一次意识到那套可怖的标准——它单凭外表给你定档，看不见一个女人泥污的帽子、湿透的外衣背后还有别的。

===Original===
The streets by this time were very empty. There was none of that lingering night life that you find in the West or the East End of London. It was as though the pavements had been swept of humanity, and in the frosty air my footsteps rang out sharply. I was not making for any particular spot—I just followed the way that fancy led me, going up one alley and down another. Past an open doorway, inside which a sleeping figure huddled, cowering deep down among its clothes. Sometimes other figures could be seen in a passageway and on the staircase, all motionless in the drugged sleep of intense fatigue.

As I emerged from one of the courts that intersect the main road at the foot of Blackfriars Bridge I was conscious of being followed. As I walked a shuffling, hesitant tread came behind me. When I stopped the unknown creature stopped as well. It was an eerie feeling, and it broke through the somnolence of nerve and muscle, awaking a sense of actual fear. Stirred to watchfulness, I could not bring myself to look round, but hurried on through a tangle of courts and narrow streets. And then I knew that the more I hurried, the more frightened I should get. Already the pursuer was invested with devilish attributes; I felt as though I were going to scream. But I got a grip of myself and stood quite still, waiting for the enemy to come up to me.

===Chinese===
这时街上已非常空了。没有伦敦西区东区那种流连不去的夜生活。人行道仿佛被人一扫而空，严寒的空气里，我的脚步声格外脆响。我并不奔哪个特定的地方去——只随兴之所至，钻进一条小巷，又钻出另一条。经过一个敞开的门洞，里面一个睡着的人影蜷成一团，深深缩在衣裳里。有时过道里、楼梯上也看得见一些人影，都在极度疲惫的酣睡中一动不动，睡得像服了麻醉药。

我从黑衣修士桥桥堍横穿大路的那几条小院里走出来时，发觉身后有人跟随着。我走着，背后响起一阵趔趄迟疑的脚步。我停，那陌生的家伙也停。那感觉鬼气森森，它凿穿了神经和肌肉的麻木，唤醒一种真切的恐惧。我警觉起来，却怎么也下不了决心回头，只加快脚步，穿行在院落窄巷的乱阵里。随即我明白了：我越赶路，就越害怕。追随着我的那个东西已经披上了一身魔性；我觉得自己快要叫出来了。可我攥住了自己，一动不动站定，等着那敌人走上前来。

===Original===
Along the pavement shuffled a pitiable looking woman, with the worst pair of boots that I have ever seen. She was hugging a shabby looking bundle and shivered every now and then with the cold. Her face was ageless with suffering, her eyes seemed to have lost all memory of hope.

“Is there anything you want?” said I. “Can I do anything for you?”

“It’s lonely like,” she said. “I’ve been by myself all the evening. I felt I’d like to be near someone for a bit.”

The loneliness of the streets is something that comes upon you in great waves. You are not conscious of it when you are doped with the eternal walking about, but when consciousness stirs, you know you are alone, and very frightened. It is as if you were in a cold sea, where you kept afloat by ceaseless striving, knowing that if a billow breaks over you, it will sweep you away.

“Is there anywhere you could get a bed?” said I.

She knew a place, she said, where she could get a shakedown for a few pence.

“One gets tired,” she said. “A bed sometimes is past dreaming.”

I had given up all thought of a bed for myself that night, so I gave her the price of mine. She was so surprised that it made me ashamed. She stood with the coppers in her hand, half hesitating as to whether she should take them. I gave her a friendly word and she shuffled off at last, making for the doss house where she hoped to shelter.

===Chinese===
沿人行道蹭过来一个模样可怜的女人，脚上那双靴子是我平生所见最糟的一双。她抱着一个寒酸的包袱，冷得时不时一哆嗦。她的脸被苦难磨得没了年纪，眼睛似乎早把希望忘了个干净。

「你有什么要的吗？」我说。「我能帮你什么忙吗？」

「怪冷清的，」她说。「我一个人待了一晚上。想挨着谁待一会儿。」

街头的孤独是一阵一阵大浪似的扑到你身上的。当你被无休无止的行走麻醉着，你意识不到它；可意识一旦苏醒，你知道自己是孤身一人，而且怕得很。那感觉就像身处寒冷的海，靠无休止的挣扎浮着，心里明白只要一个巨浪砸下来，就会把你卷走。

「有地方能弄到床位吗？」我说。

她说她知道一个地方，花几个便士能弄个地铺。

「人会乏的，」她说。「一张床，有时候想都不敢想。」

我自己那晚已断了床的念头，便把我的床位钱给了她。她惊讶得叫我羞愧。她攥着那几个铜子儿站着，半晌拿不定主意该不该收。我对她说了句友好的话，她终于一步一蹭地走了，奔那家她指望栖身的宿夜所去。

===Original===
By this time I wanted to leave South London and get back to more familiar ground. I crossed Blackfriars Bridge and walked along the Embankment. In less inclement weather you will still find destitutes on the benches by the river, but on this bitter night no one could have spent a night in the open and remained alive.

I passed along in the ghostly silence, broken only by the lapping of the water on the piers. Presently I met another wanderer, a man this time. A genial down-and-out, with the desire for friendly intercourse.

“You’re in a hurry, aren’t you, mate?” said he.

“It’s cold,” I answered, “and I want to keep warm.”

“Are you making for over the bridges?”

“I’ve just come from there,” said I. “I shall go the other way.”

“Well, then, my way shall be yours, mate.”

I nodded acquiescence and we walked along in silence, broken occasionally by my attempts at conversation.

“I don’t know as I wants to talk, mate,” said my escort. “I just thought you might like my arm round your waist.”

===Chinese===
这时候我想离开南伦敦，回到熟悉些的地界。我过了黑衣修士桥，沿堤岸走。天气不那么酷烈的时候，河边长椅上还见得到落魄的人；可在这样刺骨的夜里，露天过一夜而能活命的人是没有的。

我在幽灵似的寂静里走着，只有桥墩上的水声偶尔一破。不多时又遇见一个夜行的人，这回是个男人。一个和气的穷途末路者，怀着友好交往的愿望。

「你赶路呢，伙计？」他说。

「冷，」我答，「我想走暖和点。」

「你是往桥那边去？」

「刚从那边来，」我说。「我往另一头走。」

「好啊，那我的路就是你的路，伙计。」

我点头应允，我们默默走着，偶尔被我搭话的尝试打破。

「我不觉得我要说话，伙计，」我的护卫说。「我只是想，你兴许乐意让我搂着你的腰。」

===Original===
There was a simplicity of approach about his method which took away all suggestion of any possible offence. After all, it was very natural to suppose that a homeless woman should like the attentions of a homeless man, and I could imagine that my friend was a very kindly creature, with a strong and comforting arm.

But custom and training, and some of that fastidiousness which even destitution cannot kill, made his request impossible. I thanked him very kindly, told him that I must hurry on, and left him, puzzled and protesting.

Throughout my many adventures I never chanced on a brutal or uncivil man. There is a gentle chivalry among those who from destitution, or by reason of their employment, are in the streets at night. There is no fear that a workman or a tramp will take you for what you are not, and the suggestion that I might like an arm round my waist is but the equivalent of an invitation to sit out for a dance. The only difference is that in the world of the homeless you just seize the moment as it flies; tonight you may be on Victoria Embankment, tomorrow may find you at Streatham Hill, or Kensal Green.

===Chinese===
他这路数带着一种质朴，把一切可能的冒犯之意都卸掉了。毕竟，设想一个无家可归的女人乐意领受一个无家可归的男人的殷勤，是非常自然的事；我也能想象，我这位朋友是个很厚道的家伙，有一条结实而令人心安的胳膊。

可是习俗与教养，还有那一点连赤贫也扼杀不掉的洁癖，使他的请求无从应允。我很客气地谢了他，说我必须赶路，丢下他满腹狐疑、连声抗议地走了。

在我诸多历险中，我从未撞见一个粗暴或无礼的男人。在那些因穷困、或因职业而深夜行街的人们中间，存在着一种温文的骑士精神。工人也好，流浪汉也好，绝不会把你错当成另一种人；那条「搂腰」的建议，不过是邀你共舞一曲的等价物罢了。唯一的分别是：在无家可归者的世界里，你只管捉住飞逝的当下；今夜你或许在维多利亚堤岸，明天也许就到了斯特里瑟姆山，或肯萨尔绿野。

===Original===
I have a very precious memory of one particular night. The bitter weather had broken, and it was mild, almost warm. I had come down the Embankment to go to the Metropolitan Asylums’ Board Office under Hungerford Bridge. There, as I have said, you may apply between the hours of 10 p.m. and 2 a.m. for a bed. The lucky ones are sent off to the Salvation Army Shelters, where they are housed free of charge, others are despatched to the Church Army, and the latest comers are sent to Southwark Casual Ward.

I was not lucky on this particular night. The shelters had taken their quota, and there only remained the workhouse. I did not want to repeat my experience; indeed, to tell the truth, I was a little frightened of venturing inside institutional doors again. So when they had given me my admission form and the red counters for my tram fare, I went back to the Embankment and sat down, waiting for something to turn up.

===Chinese===
我有一段格外珍贵的记忆，属于一个特别的夜晚。酷寒的天气已经松动，夜色温和，几乎算得上暖。我沿堤岸下来，到亨格福德桥下的大都会收容事务局办事处去。如我所说，晚上 10 点到凌晨 2 点，可以在那里申请床位。运气好的被送往救世军收容所，免费安顿；次一等的被派往教会军；来得最晚的，送萨瑟克临时收容病房。

那一晚我不走运。各收容所的配额已满，只剩下济贫院。我不想重温旧梦；说实在的，再贸然跨进收容机构的门，我心里有点发怵。于是领了准入单和付电车费的红色筹码之后，我折回堤岸，坐下，等着有什么转机出现。

===Original===
Now, as I always found in this strata of society, silence is a master card. You must leave it to others to speak to you. Two men came and sat beside me. They had been on night shift on some engineering works. They had also been chums in the war and recounted many experiences. I sat and listened with proper admiration, as becomes a woman, and I had my reward.

“George,” turned towards me.

“Aren’t you the young lady wot I saw come out of the Asylums Board, just now?”

I said that was the case.

“Did you get a bed, my gal?”

“They gave me an order for the workhouse, but I shan’t go there, I can’t stick it.”

“I should say not,” said George. “Did you hear that, Dick?” and he motioned to his friend.

“The workhouse ain’t any good,” said Dick. “Look here, my gal, you come on and have a cup o’ cawfee.”

===Chinese===
在这一阶层的社会里——我一向有所发现——沉默是一张王牌。你得让别人先开口。两个男人走过来，在我身边坐下。他们在某处工程上夜班。他们又是战场上的一对老伙计，讲了不少旧事。我坐着，以一个女 人相宜的恰到好处的钦敬听着，而我也得了报偿。

「乔治」朝我转过身来。

「你不是刚才我从收容事务局里看见走出来的那位年轻小姐吗？」

我说是的。

「弄到床位了吗，姑娘？」

「他们给了我一张去济贫院的单子，可我不去，我受不了那个。」

「要我说也别去，」乔治说。「听见没有，迪克？」他朝他朋友那边一摆头。

「济贫院顶不了用，」迪克说。「听着，姑娘，跟我来，去喝杯热乎的咖灰。」

===Original===
I went with him meekly to a coffee stall near Savoy Hill. He bought me a cup of steaming hot coffee of excellent flavour, and offered me a piece of cake.

“You’re kindly welcome,” he said, politely, and seemed quite hurt when I refused. He told me he went to work every afternoon at four o’clock and got off just before midnight, when he always went for an airing before going home.

“It’s a treat to see the dawn come up in St. James’ Park. I fair love to sit in the Mall, and the lights across the river ain’t so dusty … Ain’t you got any friends, my gal?”

I said I had some friends, but they were very far away—as indeed they were during the whole of my adventure.

“I live with my father,” said Dick. “He’s just on seventy-three.” He told me the details of his family life, with a simple continuity, almost wholly lacking in the more sophisticated members of society.

“He’s a good old man, though it’s a bit dull sometimes. I shan’t get married while he lives, you know, there’s nobody else to look after him.”

I commended his affection and deplored the lack of responsibility evinced by his other relations.

===Chinese===
我乖乖地跟他到萨沃伊山附近一个咖啡摊。他给我买了一杯滚热的咖啡，味道好极了，又请我吃一块蛋糕。

「不用客气，」他客气地说，我谢绝时他显得挺委屈。他告诉我，他每天下午四点去上工，午夜前一刻下班，下班后总要在外面透透气再回家。

「看圣詹姆斯公园的天亮，是一大享受。我就爱坐在马尔林荫道上，河对岸的灯火也不赖……你一个亲人也没有吗，姑娘？」

我说我有几个朋友，只是离得非常远——的确，在我整个历险期间，他们一直那么远。

「我跟我父亲住，」迪克说。「他快七十三了。」他把他的家事一桩桩讲给我听，那份朴素的连贯，是社会上更精致的人们几乎全然缺乏的。

「他是个好老头，就是有时候有点闷。他活着我就不结婚，你知道，没有别人照顾他了。」

我称赞了他的孝心，又叹惋他那些亲属责任心的匮乏。

===Original===
“What do you say to having a walk round St. Thomas’s Hospital, my gal. It’s pleasant tonight.”

But I had had enough of South London and St. Thomas’s seemed a long way off.

“I don’t think so,” I said. “Let’s go over to the Embankment and look at the river.”

I shall never forget the look in his eyes when I said this.

“None of that, my gal,” said he. “None of your looking at the river like that, you know.”

I did not realise what he meant, and leaned over the parapet, watching the lights and rather amused.

“Look here, my gal,” said he. “It ain’t a bit of good trying to throw yourself in, ’cause I shan’t let you. Things aren’t as bad with you as that, eh?”

There was a warm urgency in his voice, a real solicitude, utterly devoid of any artificiality. I was a woman in distress and the innate strength of the male was stirred in response to my need. He wasn’t going to let me hurt myself if he could help it.

“Oh, I’m not like that,” said I. “I shouldn’t throw myself in the river just because I was hard up.”

“Look here!” His kindly Cockney face peered into mine. “Let me give you your bed money. I can stand it all right.”

“It’s very kind of you.” It was a little difficult to speak, “but I don’t think I want a bed tonight. I’ll just go up to a lodging house I know and see if they’ll let me sit in the kitchen.”

“But you’re welcome, you know, you’re welcome!”

I knew that and told him so with considerable fervour.

“I’ve taken a liking to you, my gal,” said he. “I’m mostly here on the Embankment about twelve, and if you’re by tomorrow you’ll find me. Don’t be afraid, if you can’t get any work tomorrow; I shall make you take your bed money then.”

He did not try to induce me to stop and chat; he did not try to accompany me. He left me with a beaming smile and the assurance that he would be on the Embankment if I wanted him.

===Chinese===
「咱们上圣托马斯医院那边遛遛怎么样，姑娘。今晚挺惬意。」

可南伦敦我受够了，圣托马斯医院又显得路途遥远。

「不去啦，」我说。「咱们上堤岸那边看看河吧。」

我说这话时他眼里的神情，我永远不会忘记。

「可别那样，姑娘，」他说。「可别那样看河，啊。」

我没领会他的意思，伏在栏杆上望着灯火，还觉得挺有趣。

「听着，姑娘，」他说。「想跳河一点儿用也没有，因为我不答应。你的日子还没糟到那个地步，对吧？」

他的声音里有一股火热的急切，一种真正的关切，没有半点虚饰。我是一个陷于困厄的女人，男性天生的力量被我的需要唤起了。只要他拦得住，他就不会让我伤了自己。

「哦，我不是那样的人，」我说。「我不会因为手头紧就往河里跳。」

「听着！」他那张厚道的伦敦土腔脸凑近端详我。「让我把你的床位钱给了吧。我出得起。」

「你太好了。」说话有点艰难，「可我今晚不想要床位了。我就去一家我认识的寄宿旅舍，看看他们让不让我在厨房里坐坐。」

「可你是受欢迎的，你知道吧，你是受欢迎的！」

我知道，也这样热热烈烈地告诉了他。

「我看上你了，姑娘，」他说。「我差不多天天夜里十二点来堤岸，明天你要是过来，准能找着我。别怕，明天要是找不着活儿；到时候我非要你把床位钱收下不可。」

他没有劝我留下攀谈；也没有要陪我走。他笑吟吟地离开我，留下一句保证：我要是找他，他准在堤岸上。

===Original===
This is but one of the many instances of chivalry that I have met with, and not only I, but others in my case. I don’t suppose Dick troubled as to the moral character of the woman he was good to. I don’t suppose he asked himself if I were a prostitute or a thief; he took a liking to me because he was not blinded by externals, and found something in a destitute woman akin to himself, and he offered to her the testimony of his belief and the great wealth of his generosity.

On most evenings the Asylums Board Office contains an assortment of derelicts. The most wretched of street sellers go there as a last resource. There also women, stranded in London, find their way. The police all over London have instructions to send here outcasts of both sexes who want a bed. Rarely, however, do you find a prostitute among the applicants. Only once did I meet one of their number. She was a pretty little creature and wore her thin coat with an air. She was in the inevitable sunset stockings, but her patent shoes were deplorably rent. She looked round fearfully and came towards me, wanting reassurance.

===Chinese===
这不过是我遇到的许多骑士风度中的一例——不止我，凡在我这种处境的人都会遇到。我不信迪克为他善待的女人的道德品性操过什么心。我不信他问过自己我是娼妇还是贼；他喜欢上了我，因为他没有被外表蒙住眼，在一个落魄女人身上认出了同他自己相近的东西，于是把自己信任的凭证和自己慷慨的大宗财富，一并奉给了她。

大多数晚上，收容事务局办事处里聚集着各式各样的落魄者。最潦倒的街头小贩走投无路时上这儿来。困在伦敦的女人们也摸到这儿来。全伦敦的警察都奉命把两性落魄者中想讨床位的一律送到这里。然而申请人中难得见到妓女。她们中的一位我只遇见过一回。那是个俊俏的小人儿，一件薄外衣穿出一股气派。她照例穿着那双落日色的长袜，漆皮鞋却破得可怜。她怯生生地四下张望，朝我走来，讨一点安心。

===Original===
“There’s no fear of their sending me to gaol, dear, is there? It was a bobby who sent me here, he said they’d give me a bed. I haven’t been in bed for two nights, business has been too bad. I couldn’t raise the price even for a shakedown. But—but—I can’t risk being sent to choky; you don’t think they’ll run me in, dear, do you?”

I told her there was no fear of that, and being called into the next room to give particulars, she left me with an appealing glance. But she came up to scratch with all the bravery of her type. Through the half-open door I heard the interview. She gave her name, insisted that she was a dressmaker and had been born in Nottingham.

“My parents?” she said. “Oh, my father was a sergeant-major, and my mother was the daughter of a Baptist minister. Anything else you’d like to know?”

The official closed the interrogation and handed her the paper of admission and she went off, with a wave of her hand. Appearances like hers are fugitive, for, at the risk of repetition, I want to make this plain: the little prostitute avoids the institution like the plague.

I have been told by many people that it is she who makes it impossible to run successful women’s lodging houses. It has been said that Lord Rowton, who devoted so much energy and money to providing houses for destitute men, refused even to contemplate the problem of destitute women. I do not know what is meant by the term successful in this connection. From an economic standpoint the results should be quite satisfactory. From a social standpoint they should be desirable, and as I contend, with the moral standpoint, lodging housekeepers have nothing to do. Nor, in my opinion, has Society. For the causes that drive a girl to the street, few people are concerned. To suggest that she often sells her body to give herself a bed, is an explanation very few have heard, but it is a true one. And if we get down to bedrock fundamentals, a homeless woman, whatsoever her moral character, is still a terrible indictment of society. But until Society refuses to act with moral courage, our streets will be full of derelict women, quite as many of them physically as chaste as the most bigoted puritan.

===Chinese===
「他们不会送我进监狱吧，亲爱的，是不是？是个警察叫我上这儿来的，他说这儿给床位。我两夜没沾过床了，生意太坏。连个地铺钱我都凑不出。可是——可是——我可不能冒险蹲局子；你不觉得他们会把我抓进去吧，亲爱的？」

我告诉她不必怕这个；轮到她被叫进隔壁房间登记详情，她留给我一瞥恳求的目光。可她拿出了她们那一行全部的胆气，硬是应付了下来。透过半开的门，我听见了那场问话。她报了姓名，一口咬定自己是缝衣妇，生在诺丁汉。

「我的父母？」她说。「哦，我父亲是军士长，我母亲是一位浸礼会牧师的女儿。还想知道点什么？」

官员结束了盘问，把准入单递给她，她一挥手走了。她这样的人物来去如烟。所以，哪怕冒着重复的危险，我也要把这一点说明白：小妓女们对收容机构避之如瘟疫。

许多人告诉过我，正是她们，使得体面的女子寄宿旅舍办不成。据说罗顿勋爵——他为给落魄男子办旅舍倾注了那么多财力和心血——连考虑一下落魄女子的问题都不肯。我不知道这个语境里的「办成」是什么意思。从经济立场看，成效应该相当令人满意。从社会立场看，这是可欲的；而照我主张的，道德立场同旅舍掌柜们毫无干系。照我看，也同社会毫无干系。至于把一个姑娘赶上街头的种种缘由，关心的人寥寥。说她往往是为了给自己挣一张床才出卖身体——这个解释很少有人听过，但它是真的。而倘若我们追到根底的根底：一个无家可归的女人，无论其道德品性如何，本身就是对社会的一纸可怕的诉状。只要社会一天不肯拿出道德勇气来行动，我们的街头就一天挤满落魄的女人——她们当中许多人，在身体上同最偏执的清教徒一样贞洁。

===Original===
I left the Embankment and went up Whitehall towards St. Martin’s Church. The crypt is always open to the homeless, and on a cold night is fairly full. Sleeping figures huddled on forms are dimly seen, each with their own secrets and sufferings. But, though I was dog-tired, I did not feel I could spend the few remaining hours before the dawn in this particular place. The old horror of sleeping strangers that I had first felt at Mare Street, Hackney, came back to me, and I turned from the door and resumed my tramp.

I don’t distinctly remember the route I took. I was fairly doped by this time, and a curious exhilaration, almost an exaltation of spirit filled me. It seemed to me I had found freedom, the freedom that comes of cutting off those intimate responsibilities, centred in home and friends. I had no call to be back at any hour; I could walk forever, or so long as my strength held out, if I pleased. Imagination released, or stimulated by hunger, reached out to tracks I had never before explored.

It was with a shock that I found myself at St. Pancras Station just after seven.

I went into the waiting room, and curling up on one of the settees, went to sleep. The destitute can sleep when and where the opportunity finds them, and most mercifully I was not disturbed. When I awoke I had a wash and some breakfast and prepared to face a new day.

===Chinese===
我离开堤岸，上白厅，朝圣马丁教堂走去。教堂地下室永远向无家可归者敞开，寒冷的夜里相当满。长椅上蜷睡的人影影影绰绰，各怀各的秘密与苦楚。可是，尽管我累得跟狗一样，我并不觉得能在这样一个地方打发黎明前剩下的几个钟头。我在哈克尼马尔街初尝的那种对陌生睡眠者的古老恐惧又回来了，我从门口掉头，重新上路。

我记不清自己走了哪条路线。这时候我已经相当「麻醉」了，一种奇异的亢奋——几乎是一种精神的昂扬——充满了我。我觉得自己找到了自由，那种斩断一切亲密责任——以家和朋友为中心的责任——之后的自由。我不必在哪个钟点赶回哪里；只要我乐意，我可以永远走下去，走到力气用尽为止。想象力一旦放脱了缰，或者受了饥饿的鞭策，便伸向了我从未涉足过的轨道。

七点刚过，我发觉自己站在圣潘克拉斯车站——这发现吓了我一跳。

我走进候车室，在一张长椅上蜷起身子睡着了。落魄的人，机会在哪儿、什么时候碰上他们，就能在哪儿、什么时候睡；最为慈悲的是，没有人打扰我。醒来后我洗了一把，吃了早饭，预备去迎接新的一天。

## XI Womanhood—In Extremis / 十一 女性——濒临绝境

===Original===

Sunday is a meagre day for the street seller. No one likes to be asked to buy matches, at any rate, in the earlier part of the day. There is a general feeling that the unpleasant things of life should be hidden away until after church time. Churchgoing, as a matter of fact, does not predispose to the giving of alms. I amused myself with experiments in this direction, but never got so much as a penny from any would-be worshipper. After they leave the House of God they are a little softer, and if you attack them at the right moment, between leaving worship and getting home to dinner, you may be quite lucky.

No one with any sense will, of course, try to sell matches or beg coppers from the Sunday crowd in the park. I very much wanted to see what would happen if I asked one of the beautifully gowned women on church parade for the price of a bed, but I felt it was too risky. It was ninety to one that I should have been run in, and that was an experience which during this adventure, at least, I did not want to try.

I did not get much pleasure from watching the people in the park. Generally speaking, I take a vivid interest in clothes, and am always ready to discuss the latest cut or fashion. But this is one of the relaxations that do not appeal to the homeless. I found the joy of smart hats and dainty gowns had left me, with the keen interest in the newspapers which belonged to my other life. Again, flowers, when you are very tired, do not soothe you. You remember dully how much care is bestowed upon them, and in a dumb, unconscious way, resent it. Usually it is not until dusk that the destitute go to the parks in the West End. They prefer the commonwealth of the streets where, every now and then, you may meet an answering eye, and exchange an eloquent glance.

===Chinese===

对街头小贩来说，星期天是清淡的日子。至少在一早，谁也不乐意被人缠着买火柴。人们心里有个笼统的想法：生活里那些不痛快的事，该藏到做完礼拜之后再露面。事实上，做过礼拜并不使人更肯施舍。我朝这个方向做过些试验聊以自娱，却从没从哪位准信徒手里得到过一个便士。等他们出了上帝的殿堂，心肠才会软上一点；若瞅准时机，趁他们离开礼拜、还没回到家吃午饭的当口下手，说不定运气不坏。

当然，凡是有点头脑的人，都不会想星期天到公园的人群里去卖火柴、讨铜板。我很想试试看，向那些盛装赴礼拜的华服夫人中的一位讨一夜床钱，可又觉得太冒险。十有八九我会被抓进局子，而至少在这次冒险里，那滋味我还不打算领教。

看公园里的人，我也没得到多少乐趣。平日里我对衣着兴味盎然，聊聊最新的剪裁和时样新款，向来是乐意的。可无家可归的人消受不起这种闲情。我发觉，漂亮帽子与精巧衣裙带给我的快乐，连同对报纸的那份热心——那属于我的另一段人生——都已离我而去。再说花：人非常疲惫的时候，花抚慰不了你。你只是麻木地想起这些花上倾注了多少照料，于是在心里懵懵懂懂地生出怨气。赤贫的人通常要到黄昏才上西区的公园。他们更中意街头这片大众的天地——在那里，时不时会遇上一只与你呼应的眼睛，交换一个意味深长的眼色。

===Original===

I found that Sunday very dull. Such museums and public galleries as are open do not appeal to the dispossessed. There again you meet the sharp contrast which is unbearable in moments of comparative leisure. For it is in those moments when, for the time being, the fight for bed and board is of necessity suspended, that you touch the bottom of rejection. It is then that woman, no matter what she be, craves for that thing which is called a home.

I tramped about the streets, sat for a while in Trafalgar Square, and had a rasher of bacon and a cup of coffee in an eating house. At the end of the afternoon I roused myself to begin business, and by a stroke of luck I met my friend of the plush coat in one of her favourite bars.

We did some business together. I made a shilling or two, and then my friend suggested we should go to a cinema, she standing treat. Now I have never been keen on films, except when Charlie Chaplin is on the screen, and I felt quite indifferent at the prospect of such enjoyment. She took two of the cheaper seats in a house near Shaftesbury Avenue, and I waited for the show to begin, quite incurious and even depressed. It was a story of the conventional type, in which a poor girl becomes a leader of society, following a round of luxurious enjoyment, but I found myself suddenly watching the pictures with eagerness, positive pleasure! I dwelt with rapture on her dinner with the hero in an expensive restaurant. I noted with extraordinary precision everything she ate. I enjoyed with her the roses he bought, and thrilled to the music the orchestra was playing. I would not have missed an inch of film. I would not have forfeited any one of the thousand mechanical sensations she enjoyed. It was not until it was all over that I asked myself why this change had come about, why it was that I, and the people in the cheap seats around me, had been wrought up to such excitement, almost ecstasy.

===Chinese===

我发觉那个星期天乏味透顶。开着门的那些博物馆和公共美术馆，也引不起被剥夺者的兴致。在那里，你又一次撞上那难以忍受的尖锐对照——在难得的闲暇时分尤其受不了。因为恰恰是在那样的时刻，当床铺与饭碗之争暂时歇手，你才触到被弃绝的最深处。也正是在那时，女人——不管她是怎样的人——会渴求那样一件东西，它的名字叫作家。

我在街上游荡，在特拉法加广场坐了一阵，又在一间小饭馆里吃了一片培根、喝了一杯咖啡。下午将尽时，我振作精神开始做买卖，运气不坏，在长毛绒大衣夫人中意的酒吧里的一家撞见了她。

我们合伙做了点生意。我挣到一两个先令，随后夫人提议一起去看电影，她请客。我从来对电影热络不起来，除非银幕上是卓别林，所以对这桩享受的前景全无所谓。她在沙夫茨伯里大道附近一家影院买了两张廉价座位，我等着开演，兴味索然，甚至有些低沉。那是部老套的故事片：一个穷姑娘经了一圈奢华享乐，最后跻身社交界名媛之列。可我发现自己忽然盯着银幕看得出神，竟是实实在在的愉快！她同男主角在高档餐馆进餐那一场，我看得如痴如醉。她吃的每一样东西，我都记得分毫不差。他买的玫瑰，我陪她一同受用；乐师们奏起的音乐，我也随之荡漾。一寸胶片我也不肯错过。她享受到的那千百种机械的感官刺激，我一样也舍不得放弃。直到全场落幕，我才问自己：这变化是从哪儿来的？为什么我，还有廉价座位里我周围的那些人，竟被撩拨得这样兴奋，几乎到了如癫如狂的地步？

===Original===

And then the solution came. When you are hungry and cold, without a home and without hope, the “Pictures” warm your imagination, heat your blood and somehow vitalise your body. The blank shutters that hem you in from enjoyment are suddenly down, and you look into a world of light and colour, expectancy and romance﻿—that eternal longing for romance which dies so hardly. This is one of the things that I discovered in my experience. For the same reason this is, I think, why the inhabitants of drab homes in mean streets flock to the cinema. I do not think it has any educational value, nor does it generally stimulate the imagination. But it supplies a lack, and to those whose horizon is bounded by the four walls of a room, badly distempered, or hideously papered, the contemplation of the garish hotel, the spacious restaurant, or impossible heroines of the screen is compensation. This also accounts, I suppose, for the unending supply of this kind of picture. Commerce always caters for a steady public, and while the taste of the artistic is soon surfeited, the intelligence of the thinking easily annoyed, the vast residuum of the patient poor, who unendingly bear the burden of monotony, is a sure and certain market in a world of shifting values.

I parted with my friend outside the cinema. She suggested I should go with her to some doss house in the Borough, but I felt I could not endure a second dose of that kind of thing. I had been out for thirty-six hours, and the desire for bed was getting clamorous. I set my teeth and vowed that, whatever happened, I would sleep under a roof that night.

I did not want to return to Kennedy Court. Experience, however, had already shown me how difficult it was to get a bed over the weekend, so I determined to pay my one and twopence, secure my lodging and then try my luck elsewhere, so that if no fresher accommodation were forthcoming I could be certain of a comparatively decent rest.

===Chinese===

随后答案来了。当你又饿又冷、无家可归又无盼头的时候，「电影」会烘暖你的想象，烧热你的血液，不知怎么还给你的身体注入生气。那把享乐隔在门外的暗沉沉的百叶板忽然放了下来，你望进一个光与色、期待与传奇的世界——那份对传奇的永恒渴望，最难死透。这是我在历练中的发现之一。我想，也正是出于同样的缘故，灰暗街道上灰房子里的居民才会成群涌向电影院。我不认为电影有什么教育价值，它通常也激发不起想象力。但它填补了一种匮乏；对那些眼界被四面墙壁框死的人——墙上是劣质的水浆涂料，或是难看的糊墙纸——看一眼那俗艳的旅馆、宽敞的餐馆，或是银幕上不像真人的女主角，便算得一种补偿。我想，这也解释了这类影片何以源源不断。商业永远伺候稳定的客源：风雅之士的趣味很快餍足，爱思考的头脑轻易生厌，而那沉默坚忍的穷苦大众——他们无休无止地背负着单调的重担——在这个价值翻覆的世界上，才是稳稳当当的市场。

我在电影院外同夫人道了别。她提议我随她去博罗区找家宿夜所，可我自觉再也受不住第二回那样的罪了。我已经三十六个钟头没有沾床，想睡觉的欲望嚷嚷得越来越凶。我咬紧牙关发了狠：不管出什么事，这一夜定要睡在屋顶底下。

我不想回肯尼迪大院去。然而经验早已告诉我，周末要弄到一张床何其困难，于是我决定先付掉那一先令两便士，把铺位攥在手里，再到别处去碰碰运气——万一寻不着更新的住处，我至少还能落个相对体面的安歇。

===Original===

But Kennedy Court was full. The weekend, it seems, fills up every available corner in the world that lies beyond the ken of the well-fed. I wasn’t cast down, however, but took a tram to Camden Town, the next public lodging house on the list, where conditions are very similar. But here again I was foiled; there was not a bed to be had. I went back to the Strand, determined to fight with every weapon at my service for a roof. I went to Bow Street Police Station and asked if they could tell me of a lodging house within easy distance.

Now I generally found the police helpful in these emergencies, but on this occasion, though their spirit was quite willing, their knowledge was hopelessly out of date.

“There’s a Women’s Lodging House in Drury Lane, Miss,” said the constable on duty. “You’ll find it quite a decent place.”

===Chinese===

可是肯尼迪大院客满了。周末似乎总会把饱食者视野之外的那个世界里每一个可用的角落都塞满。我并没有丧气，搭上一辆电车去卡姆登镇——名单上的下一家公共寄宿舍，条件相仿。可到了那儿我又碰了壁：一张床也弄不到。我折回斯特兰德，决心用手头一切手段去争一片屋顶。我走进弓街警察局，问他们可否指点一处近便的寄宿舍。

遇上这类急难，我总的说来都觉得警察肯帮忙，可这一回，他们心是热的，情报却陈旧得没救。

「德鲁里巷有家妇女寄宿舍，小姐，」值勤的警察说，「那是个蛮体面的地方。」

===Original===

I went on my voyage of discovery, fired with new hope, and after interminable questioning and considerable doubt and pain, discovered the number he had given me. It did not look at all like a lodging house, and when after repeated pealings at the bell, a man appeared, I learned that it was Baptist Mission.

“It used to be a lodging house twenty years ago,” said the caretaker, mournfully, “but it’s shut up now, and we’re holding service. No, I don’t know anywhere you could get a bed. Sister Etheldreda might tell you, she’s just round the corner.”

But Sister Etheldreda’s domain was bolted and barred, and in despair I held up a woman in the street and asked if she could help me. Time was getting on, and I simply could not face the prospect of a second night in the streets.

===Chinese===

我燃起新的希望，踏上寻访之旅，问了个没完、疑了个够、吃了些苦头之后，才找到他给的那个门牌。那地方怎么看也不像寄宿舍；把门铃按响了一遍又一遍之后，出来一个男人，我这才知道这里是浸信会布道所。

「二十年前这儿是家寄宿舍，」看门人凄然道，「如今关掉了，我们在这儿做礼拜。别处哪儿能弄到床，我说不上。埃塞尔德雷达修女兴许能告诉你，她就在拐角那儿。」

可埃塞尔德雷达修女的门上了闩、加了杠。绝望之下，我拦住街上一个女人，问她能不能帮我。时间越来越晚，我实在无法面对第二夜流落街头的前景。

===Original===

“There’s a decent woman who lets lodgings quite close here; you’ll know the house by the green door and the white steps. She doesn’t charge very much and I think you’ll be comfortable.”

As the proud possessor of a few shillings, I wasn’t afraid of the charge, and I walked up, bold as brass, to the green door and gave the regulation two knocks.

By this time it was dark, and in the dimness of the street, broken by one remote lamp, I hoped my shabbiness would pass unnoticed. But from the lynx eyes of the woman in the white starched apron, and immaculate black dress, there was no escape. One look was quite enough. Before I had time to frame my request I was answered.

===Chinese===

「这附近有位正派太太出租床位；绿门白台阶，你认得出那房子。她收得不贵，我想你住着会舒服的。」

身为几个先令的骄傲主人，我倒不怕她的价钱，大摇大摆走到绿门跟前，照规矩叩了两下。

这时天已黑透。街上一盏远灯照出些昏昧的光，我指望自己那身寒碜行头能蒙混过去。可那位系着浆硬的白围裙、穿一身无可挑剔的黑衣裳的女人，生着一双山猫般的眼睛，从那里是逃不掉的。一眼就够了。我还没来得及把来意说出口，她已经给了我答案。

===Original===

“I haven’t got a room,” she said. “No, I couldn’t possibly take you.”

She shut the door firmly, with precision, and I knew that I might beat my hands against it﻿—she would not reopen.

I think I went a little mad just then. I felt that London ought to be burned, that fire and brimstone should rain down on a city in which a decent woman could not find a bed. I could not go back to Mare Street, Hackney, it wasn’t fair to impose myself upon the Salvation Army as a destitute when I had money in my pocket. Besides, it seemed incredible that such a state of things could be. I returned doggedly to Bow Street and was told of a lodging house at the bottom of Craven Street leading to the Embankment.

===Chinese===

「我没有多余的房间，」她说，「不行，我实在不能收你。」

她干脆利落、毫厘不差地把门关严。我知道，哪怕我把双手在门上捶烂，她也不会再开。

我想那一刻我有点疯了。我觉得伦敦就该烧掉，天火和硫磺该当头砸下，砸到这座容不下一个正派女人一张床的城市。我没法回哈克尼的马尔街去：口袋里明明有钱，再以赤贫者的身份去叨扰救世军，说不过去。再说，这样的事居然当真存在，简直叫人无法相信。我硬着头皮再回弓街，人家告诉我，通到维多利亚堤岸的克雷文街尽头有一家寄宿舍。

===Original===

There was no such place. The lodging house resolved itself into one-of the many private hotels whose price would have been beyond my means, and from whose doors my dilapidated appearance would have barred me.

The Church Army I could not try again and the *Christian Herald* Mission, full the previous night, would obviously still be crowded.

I returned to the charge and interrogated a policeman. He is the one member of the Force who has given me cause for dislike. He is a very superior person, enormously tall, with large and languorous hands that wave imperially towards the traffic at Charing Cross.

===Chinese===

根本没有这么个地方。那家寄宿舍落到实处，原来是无数私家旅馆中的一家，价钱远非我所能负担，何况凭我这身破落行头，人家门口就先把我拦下了。

教会军那边我不能再去了；《基督教先驱报》的收容所前一夜已满员，此刻显然照旧拥挤。

我回过头再战，又审问起一名警察来。他是这支队伍里唯一让我心生嫌恶的一个。此公派头十足，个子高得出奇，一双慵懒的大手朝着查令十字的车流威严地挥动。

===Original===

“Can you tell me of a place, please, where I can get a decent bed for half-a-crown or three shillings?”

He regarded me as if I were a sort of loathsome microbe, impertinently disturbing his contemplation of the universe.

“Six-and-sixpence is the cheapest you can get a bed in this district,” he said, languidly, in a pronounced Oxford accent.

“I can’t pay as much as that,” I answered.

===Chinese===

「劳驾，能不能告诉我在哪儿能弄到半克朗或三先令一张的体面床位？」

他看我的眼神，仿佛我是一枚讨厌的微生物，胆敢搅扰他静观宇宙。

「六先令六便士，是这一带最便宜的床位了，」他懒洋洋地说，一口道地的牛津腔。

「我出不起那么多。」我答道。

===Original===

“If you go across the river you could get it for ninepence.”

“I don’t want that sort of place,” I protested. “I want somewhere respectable.”

“You can’t get respectability for two and sixpence,” he said, as though shocked at the enormity of my demand. “Ninepence, or perhaps fourpence, across the river, or six and sixpence here; there’s no choice between.”

There was nothing more to be gained by talking to this guardian of the public, so I walked off, burning with rage and literally longing for a fight. Here was I, in the possession of money and unable to find a bed within three, four, or even five miles of Charing Cross this side of the river. I rebelled at the thought that I was to be cooped in a doss house and I had no wish to return to the casual ward.

===Chinese===

「到河对岸去，九便士就能弄一张。」

「我不要那种地方，」我抗议道，「我要个体面些的。」

「两先令六便士可买不来体面，」他说，那口气像是被我这一要求的骇人听闻吓着了。「九便士，或是四便士，到河对岸去；要么这儿六先令六便士；此外没有别的可选。」

同这位人民的守护者再谈下去也榨不出什么了，我走开了，怒火中烧，简直巴望打一架。瞧瞧我吧：手里捏着钱，却在查令十字方圆三英里、四英里、甚至五英里之内、河的这一边，找不到一张床。一想到自己要被塞进宿夜所我就满心反叛，临时收容病房我也不想再回去了。

===Original===

I decided to seek information from my own kind, and it being past ten o’clock, I went to the Adelphi Arches already filling up. It is the fashion, nowadays to state that people do not sleep in the streets of London. The Embankment, we are told, has been swept clean of the homeless, while the destitute who used to congregate in the Adelphi, have now migrated to the office under Hungerford Bridge.

This is but the expression of an airy fancy. There are still destitute men and women on the streets of London and, night after night in the cold weather, the Adelphi Arches are crowded with tired souls. They are very silent as a rule, keeping themselves to themselves for the most part, and rarely exchanging confidences. The men there﻿—fewer in number than the women﻿—keep together, a short distance from the other sex, who huddle close, friend and stranger, for the sake of warmth. There is the same tragic resignation in their faces as you find throughout their world, a blind acceptance of fate that has marked them out, for no direct fault or failing, as wanderers of the streets, sentenced to a perpetual walking about, with occasional periods of rest upon the stones, or, rarer still, a night in a lodging house.

Many of these women could afford a few pence if there were accommodation for them. But social reformers, political leaders, charitable workers, do not see the necessity for such provision. They are outcasts﻿—let them sleep in the streets﻿—and the same individual who will fight fiercely to secure man his human rights, will remain unmoved by an urge to do the same for woman.

===Chinese===

我决定向自己人打听消息。这时已过十点，我走向阿德尔菲拱廊，那里正渐渐睡满。如今时兴的说法是：伦敦街头已没有人睡。据说堤岸上的无家可归者已被清扫一空，从前聚在阿德尔菲的赤贫者，如今已迁到亨格福德桥底下那处办公房里去了。

这不过是空中楼阁式的空想。伦敦的街头如今仍有走投无路的男人和女人；寒夜里一夜又一夜，阿德尔菲拱廊挤满疲惫的灵魂。他们照例沉默，大抵独来独往，难得向人交心。那里的男人——人数少于女人——抱作一团，同女人们隔开一小段距离；女人们则紧紧偎作一堆，朋友也罢陌生人也罢，只为取暖。她们脸上有一种你在她们整个世界里随处撞见的悲惨的认命——对命运盲目地领受：并非因了什么直接的过错或亏欠，她们却被挑了出来，判作街头的游荡者，服那无休止的行走之刑，间或在石板上歇一阵，更难得的，才有宿夜所里的一夜。

这些女人里，许多人是掏得出几个便士的，只要社会肯为她们备下铺位。可是社会改革家、政党领袖、慈善工作者，看不见这份供给的必要。她们是弃民——让她们睡街上去吧——同一个人，为给男性争人权可以拼死相争，轮到为女性做同样的事，却无动于衷。

===Original===

I sat down beside an old, old crone, so frail it seemed a miracle that she could bear her slight body on her attenuated limbs. Her face was of the colour that comes of long years of bad feeding and ill-sleeping, but her eyes were bright and her mouth had not lost its humour. She had a wide knowledge of London’s lodging houses and told me that on a Sunday it was hard to get in anywhere.

“I’d advise you to go to Hanbury Street, Whitechapel,” she said, “to the Salvation Army Shelter. If they’ve got a bed, you’ll get in there for fivepence.”

It did not sound alluring, but there was no choice. I could not face the streets the second night, and I got a district train to Whitechapel.

===Chinese===

我在一个老得不能再老的老婆子身边坐下。她衰弱到那步田地，那副细伶伶的骨架居然还扛得住轻飘飘的身子，简直是桩奇迹。她的脸色是长年劣食少眠养出来的那种颜色，可一双眼睛还亮着，嘴上的诙谐也没有丢。她对伦敦各家客栈见多识广，告诉我说，逢星期天，哪儿都难挤进去。

「我劝你上白教堂的汉伯利街去，」她说，「去救世军收容所。那儿要有床，你花五便士就能进去。」

听着并不诱人，但别无选择。第二天夜里再流落街头，我受不了，便搭上开往白教堂的区间火车。

===Original===

The capital of the East End was in full flare. The broad pavements were crowded with well dressed women and sleek young men, talking many and foreign tongues. A number of cafés were open and brilliantly lighted windows showed model hats and dresses, Paris shoes and bags, all in the latest style and at moderate prices. Life in the West End at this hour on a Sunday is stagnant; in Whitechapel the current is strong. The foreign faces gleam, the quick and eager conversation has a vibrant influence; one feels very much alive.

Whitechapel to so many is still the synonym for drab wretchedness, that it is perhaps excusable here to point out that the splendid wide road that bisects the district is one of the finest in the metropolis, and still suggests the imperial straightness of the old Roman road on which it stands. There are no mean dwellings in the main thoroughfare, and spacious and leafy squares lie to the north and south. You have to penetrate into the congeries of alleys and byways before you come across the slum area which, even at its worst, is incomparably better than the purlieus of Benthal Green and Dalston.

I asked my way to Hanbury Street of an attractive little Cockney﻿—one of the few I encountered in this neighbourhood.

===Chinese===

东区的都心正是一片灯火鼎沸。宽阔的人行道熙熙攘攘，尽是衣着鲜亮的女人和油光水滑的年轻男人，操着五花八门的外国话。好几家咖啡馆开着张，明晃晃的橱窗里陈列着时新的帽子衣裳、巴黎鞋子和手提包，一律最新样式，价钱也公道。星期天这个钟点，西区的日子是一潭死水；白教堂的水流却湍急。异国的面孔灼灼放光，急切热络的谈话震得空气发颤；你会觉得自己活得分外起劲。

对许多人来说，白教堂至今仍是灰暗悲惨的同义词，因此这里或许值得补上一句：横贯全区的那条宽阔大道，是全伦敦最气派的街道之一，至今仍透着它脚下那条古罗马大道帝国式的笔直。干道上见不到寒酸的住屋，南北两侧是宽敞葱茏的广场。你得钻进小巷与陋巷纠结成堆的深处，才碰得上贫民窟；而那贫民窟即便在最糟的时候，也远比贝斯纳尔格林和达尔斯顿的角落强。

我向一个俊俏的伦敦本地妞问路——在这一带，这样的人我统共只遇见寥寥几个。

===Original===

“It’s early yet,” she said, “and you’re pretty sure of getting a bed. Come along with me and have a cup of cawfee.”

I was ready for companionship, and eagerly accompanied her to a dingy-looking place in a back street, where they run a sort of club. The room was large and cheery, with whitewashed walls on which were hung attractive posters, Continental, and some clever sketches. There was a bar at the far end of the room, where they served salad and other delicatessen, and strong sweet coffee and chocolate. There was no drink served on the premises; indeed, throughout my wanderings in the underworld, I came across no illicit drinking shop, nor can I think that such an establishment could pay. It is only people with money to burn, who can afford to buy bad liquor at exorbitant rates at illegal hours, and even these do not so much want the drink as to protest against the foolish restriction which seeks to treat adult people like small boys and girls.

The room was fairly crowded with men and women. Most of the former were Jews who had lately come to this country. Their broken English was picturesque, and though they looked revolutionary their sentiments were amiable. There was a strong mixture of the Slav element, both Russian and Polish Jews discussing the respective tyrannies of their adopted countries with animus and emphasis. For the moment England contented them﻿—wages were higher over here, and I gathered they were just a little astonished to find that free speech﻿—within four walls at any rate﻿—still remains free.

It is common knowledge that police spies frequent these places, but the police have a shrewd idea as to where they will obtain evidence sufficiently important to secure promotion. The club where I was, and very many others like it, has no value to the aspirants of Scotland Yard. The happy hunting ground of narks lies elsewhere, more noticeably in the West Central district, than in the East End of London.

===Chinese===

「还早着呢，」她说，「床位你十拿九稳。跟俺来，先喝杯咖啡。」

我正想有人做伴，便热切地随她走进后街一家看着邋遢的铺子，那里开着一处类似俱乐部的所在。屋子很大很欢快，刷得雪白的墙上挂着好看的招贴画——欧陆风格的——还有几幅精巧的速写。屋子顶头有一座吧台，卖色拉和各色熟食，还有又浓又甜的咖啡和巧克力。铺子里不卖酒；说实在的，我在下层世界闯荡一路，从没撞见私自卖酒的地下酒铺，也想不出那样一家铺子怎么可能维持得下去。买得起劣酒、出得起天价、赶得上门禁之外时辰的，只有那些钱多得烧手的人；就连他们，图的与其说是酒，不如说是抗议那把大人当小男孩小女孩管的蠢规矩。

屋里男男女女已相当挤了。男的多数是新近来这个国家的犹太人。他们磕磕绊绊的英语说得有滋有味，模样虽透着股革命气，心思却和善。斯拉夫血统的也占不小的比重，俄国犹太人同波兰犹太人各自数落着他们归化国家的种种苛政，言辞激愤，斩钉截铁。眼下英国还让他们称心——这边的工钱高些；而且我听得出来，言论自由——至少在四堵墙之内——居然还作数，这一点他们隐隐感到吃惊。

众所周知，警方的眼线常光顾这类地方，但警察心里雪亮：什么地方才搜得出够分量、够升官的证据。我所在的这家俱乐部，以及许许多多同类铺子，对苏格兰场的野心家毫无价值。眼线们的乐园在别处——与其说在伦敦东区，不如说更在中西区那一带。

===Original===

The women of the company were a mixed lot. Some of them worked at millinery and dressmaking, slop shops, i.e., establishments where sweated rates are paid, or in more reputable emporiums. There were some prostitutes, but, as I have always found them, they were quite well mannered, and contributed their quota of gaiety. My Cockney friend was very entertaining. A man handed her a Russian cigarette, which she accepted with delighted abandon.

“There now, dearie,” she exclaimed, “with a cup of tea and a slice of lemon I shall be quite Russo Ballo!”

The cup of tea was forthcoming, with a small plate of sausage, brown bread and butter and some chocolollies, a succulent form of sour pickle, much esteemed in the foreign quarters. A large, placid looking woman with Tartar eyes, consumed vast quantities of borscht﻿—a Slav soup made from beetroot and other condiments﻿—conversing the while with a Chinese looking youth, with long, nervous hands, and a black tail coat.

===Chinese===

在场的女人们成分很杂。有的做女帽、做衣裳，有的在血汗裁缝铺——就是按血汗价付工钱的铺子——做工，也有的在更体面的大铺子里。有几个是妓女，但依我一贯的见闻，她们都相当有礼数，也添上她们那一份快活。我的伦敦妞朋友很会逗乐。一个男人递给她一支俄国烟卷，她欢天喜地、毫不推辞地接了。

「你瞧，亲爱的，」她嚷道，「再配上一杯茶、一片柠檬，俺就是地道的罗索·巴洛（Russo Ballo）啦！」

茶端上来了，还有一小碟香肠、黑面包黄油，外加几样乔科洛利（chocolollies）——一种在外侨街区备受青睐、滋味浓厚的酸泡菜。一位相貌温吞的大块头女人，长着一双鞑靼人的眼睛，一面大口大口地喝着罗宋汤——一种用甜菜根和各色佐料做成的斯拉夫汤——一面同一个长相像中国人的小伙子说话；那小伙子一双手细长而神经质，穿一件黑色燕尾服。

===Original===

My Cockney friend, having quite clearly “got off,” to use her own phraseology, I decided that it was time to depart. It would be no act of friendship on my part to interfere with a deal, so I slipped out of the club as easily as I had come in, with, at bottom, a feeling of regret. That little Whitechapel club, of which there are so many in the district, is the nearest approach to the familiar café of the continent. There is always the stir of social life, an air of geniality, and the centre of attraction is not the bar, laden with food and drink, but the conversation that circles round the marble-topped tables; you feel that here you will find someone to listen to, if not to talk with, a response to human interest, that fluttering up towards joy, even in the most poverty-stricken, which is so curiously absent from the English teashop.

There is, as I have cause to know, a camaraderie of the tavern which nothing can excel. But for the most part women are barred from this. They are not encouraged to sit about in public houses and join in the discussions of their male friends. They go in to drink, and having drunk, custom requires them to withdraw, while the notion that social intercourse is in any way associated with Lyons or the A.B.C. is too utterly fantastic for consideration.

I had hardly got to the end of the street when the little Cockney joined me. She had been running hard and was obviously out of breath.

===Chinese===

我的伦敦妞朋友，用她自己的话说，分明已经「成了单」，我寻思该动身了。搅扰人家一桩生意算不得朋友之道，于是我溜出俱乐部，同进来时一样轻巧，心底却留着一分怅惘。白教堂这样的小俱乐部，这一带还有许多，是同欧陆习见的咖啡馆最接近的东西。那儿永远涌动着社会生活的热流，有一股暖融融的和气；而引人围拢的中心不是堆满饮食的吧台，是绕着大理石桌面打转的谈话。你会觉得，在这里总能找到肯听你说话的人——哪怕搭不上话——得到一分对人间趣味的回应；哪怕在最赤贫的人身上，那点向着欢乐扑棱的火苗也还扑闪着；而这，恰恰是英国茶馆里最古怪地见不到的东西。

酒馆里的那份袍泽之情，我有切身体会，天下没有什么能胜过它。但女人大抵被摒在门外。没人欢迎她们坐在酒馆里，加入男性朋友们的谈论。她们进去是喝酒，喝完了，规矩便请她们退场；至于说社交往来同利昂斯或 A.B.C. 茶馆能沾上什么边，那念头荒唐得不值一提。

我刚走到街尾，小伦敦妞就追了上来。她一路猛跑，分明气还没喘匀。

===Original===

“What did you leave like that for, dear?” she asked.

“I thought you’d found a friend,” I said, “and didn’t want me.”

“Oh, that’s all right,” she said. “He can wait. I told yer I’d show yer the wye and I will. I’m going to sleep there myself coming to think of it.”

She led me across the broad road and round to the back of a long, narrow street full of high buildings, engineering works, bakeries, etc. We found the shelter tucked away behind a narrow door, which was opened by a pleasant faced young woman, in Army uniform. The shelter is under the control of the Salvation Army, but it is run on ordinary commercial lines. I mean by this that no religious services are held, and that no enquiries are made. You may be good, bad, or indifferent, clean or dirty, so long as you want a bed and can pay fivepence you are admitted.

===Chinese===

「你干嘛这就走啦，亲爱的？」她问。

「我当你找着朋友了，」我说，「用不着我了。」

「嗐，没事儿，」她说，「让他等去。俺跟你说过要给你带路，说带就带。想起来了，俺今晚自己也睡那儿。」

她领我横过大马路，绕到一条又长又窄的街道背后，那里满是高楼、机器厂、面包房等等。收容所就藏在一扇窄门后头，开门的是个面相和善的年轻女人，穿着救世军制服。这收容所归救世军管辖，但按普通的商业路子经营。我的意思是：不举行宗教仪式，也不盘问来历。你是好人、坏人、不好不坏的人，干净也好邋遢也罢，只要你要一张床、掏得出五便士，就收你。

===Original===

I paid my money and was led down a passage into an enormous hall. My friend was claimed by an old friend in transit, and got separated from me, and I did not see her again. You are forever making transitory acquaintances in the world of the destitute. You may feel you have found a kindred mind and sympathetic soul and long to hold renewed communion, but harsh circumstances, grim and inexorable, force you apart. There is no sustained drama in the underworld, only a series of incidents, beautiful, tragic, heartrending, which dissolve one into the other like figures on a film.

A wide gallery runs round the hall, which, like the floor, is entirely covered with narrow beds, just wide enough to lie and barely to turn in. There is only enough space between the beds to pass by; it is a sea of beds, every one of which, on the night when I was there, was occupied; and the number ran into hundreds. The big gas lamp in the roof burned till dawn, casting fantastic shadows on the sleeping faces. There they all lay﻿—there every night they lie, womanhood *in extremis*; old, young, middle-aged, hopeless, helpless, desperate and courageous.

The thing that hurt me most was the realisation that these women who have managed to gather their few pence to secure a bed, have lost all knowledge of anything remotely like a home. Migratory as any of the tribes of Asia, they know not where they may pitch their tent.

===Chinese===

我付了钱，被领着穿过一道走廊，进了一间极大的厅。我的朋友半路被一位旧相识认了去，同我走散，我再没见到她。在赤贫者的世界里，你永远在结这种萍水之交。你也许觉得遇上了一颗相通的心、一个同病相怜的灵魂，渴望再续这份交情，可是严酷的境况——狰狞而不容分说——把你们生生冲散。下层世界没有连绵的戏剧，只有一连串的事件，美的、惨的、揪心的，一件融进另一件，像胶片上的人影。

厅的四壁环着一圈宽回廊，回廊同厅底一样，密密铺满窄床，只够躺下、勉强翻身。床与床之间只留侧身通行的空当；那是一片床的海洋，我在场的那一夜，每一张都睡了人，数目上百。屋顶的大煤气灯一直燃到天明，在一张张睡脸上投下光怪陆离的影子。她们全都躺在那里——每夜她们都躺在那里——濒临绝境的女性；老的、少的、中年的，绝望的、无告的、亡命的，而又坚毅的。

最叫我痛心的，是意识到这些好不容易凑起几个便士换一张床的女人，已经把任何略似于「家」的东西忘了个干净。她们像亚洲的任何游牧部落一样迁徙不定，不知道下一顶帐篷将在哪里支起。

===Original===

The place is clean, like all the Salvation Army houses, and there is an entire absence of officialdom. The beds are not too hard and the sheets and covering are clean and hygienic, mattresses and pillows are encased in American cloth, for the sake of sanitation, but with an outer cover of calico, and there is a sufficiency of bed clothing, also encased.

It was an eerie night. The sleep for which I longed, the sleep for which body and soul were craving would not come. There was something rather terrible in the presence of this army of the night; I felt myself encompassed by a tide of human desolation which at any moment might overpower and swamp me. I had found the workhouse cell solitary, but there was something worse than solitude in that huge bare ward crowded with beds. Not for one moment was there peace; there was a stirring as of the leaves in a dense forest, to a continual accompaniment of coughing. I never knew there were so many and such variety of coughs. One poor thing hacked hour after hour, her handkerchief soaked with blood. No one slept kindly, no one found rest. When the continual stirring of the leaves was still, there was a sound as of the wind over the sea, and once a woman’s voice screamed out in agony, “I can’t breathe﻿—I can’t breathe.”

The many indescribable noises broke into definite movement. The woman was upstairs in the gallery. With the swift kindliness of the destitute, people rose from their beds and went to her from all over the hall; curious, pathetic figures, some of them clinging to the last rags of what had been a night dress which they had carried with them on their endless journeying. Others, partially undressed, with bare feet, in a skirt and a man’s sweater; others again, fully dressed, ashamed, perhaps, to show their apologies for underwear, or maybe, too proud or cold, to take their outer garments off. The girl continued to cry out, tossing from side to side. Somebody fetched the superintendent, who presently arrived, followed by an anxious queue. She administered drops, and at the second dose the pain subsided.

===Chinese===

这地方干净，救世军的房子全都这样，而且全无官府气。床不算太硬，床单被褥干净卫生；为讲卫生，床垫和枕头都封在美国漆布里，外面再罩一层白布套，被褥也充足，同样封在套子里。

那是个瘆人的夜晚。我渴求的睡眠——我的身体和灵魂都在哀告的睡眠——偏偏不来。这支夜之大军在场，有某种骇人的东西；我感到自己被一股人间荒凉的大潮围住，它随时可能把我压倒、吞没。济贫院的囚室里好歹是独自一人，可这挤满床铺的空旷大病房里，有比孤独更糟的东西。没有一刻安宁；人声像密林中的叶子窸窣不休，底下伴着接连不断的咳嗽。我从不知道咳嗽竟有这么多种、这么多样。一个可怜人一声接一声地咳，咳了几个钟头，手绢都浸透了血。没有人睡得安稳，没有人得到休息。当叶子的窸窣平息下去，又响起风声掠过海面似的呜咽；有一回，一个女人在痛苦中尖叫起来：「我喘不上气——我喘不上气。」

那许多难以名状的声响忽然化作了明确的行动。那女人在楼上回廊里。怀着赤贫者特有的敏捷的善意，人们从大厅四面下了床，朝她围拢过去；一个个好奇又可怜的身影，有的身上还挂着仅剩的最后几缕睡衣碎片——那是她们无尽跋涉中一直随身带着的；有的衣衫半褪，赤着脚，只穿一条裙子和一件男人的毛线衣；还有的索性和衣而卧，也许是羞于露出那身聊胜于无的内衣，也许是太要强，或者太冷，不肯脱去外衣。姑娘还在喊叫，翻来覆去。有人去请所长，所长旋即赶到，身后跟着一串焦急的人。她给病人滴了药水，第二剂下去，疼痛平息了。

===Original===

“I shouldn’t half like some of that,” said one of the watchers, raucously.

“Garn!” was the answer. “It’s only ginger!” and suddenly the whole place shrieked with mirth. The laughter subsided, the sick woman moaned herself to sleep, the hacking cough broke out with less disturbance. It seemed as though at long last the silence of the night was going to descend upon that troubled place. But with the increased stillness I became aware of other barriers from slumber.

The building has central heating and the warm air, heavy with the strong stench of humanity and the odour of stale clothes﻿—hot, acrid, sickly﻿—made me feel faint. I stumbled across the floor into the flag-stoned passage and got a glass of water. My endurance had nearly reached the limit, I did not see how I was going to live through to the morning. Not so much because of the physical discomforts, but by reason of my tribulation of soul. The accumulation of experiences had reached a point when it was difficult to bear any more. The knowledge that I was but one of many hundreds of broken women, and that this hall held but a remnant of the legion of the dispossessed, frightened me. It was something in life that I had not guessed at; and the knowledge made me afraid.

It was piercingly cold in the lavatory and I was compelled to go back into the warm stench of the sleeping hall. I curled under the clothes and tried to set myself counting sheep. But it is difficult to realise placid munchers when you are surrounded by suffering humans. The coughing broke out again, and the woman in the bed next to mine began to cry.

===Chinese===

「俺倒真想也来上一点呢。」围观的人里有一个粗声说。

「去你的！」有人答，「那不过是姜汁！」霎时间整个大厅哄堂大笑。笑声落下去，病中的女人呻吟着睡熟了，咳血的咳嗽又响起来，搅扰得轻了些。长夜的寂静仿佛终于要降临到这个多灾多难的地方。可是四下越安静，我越发觉拦在睡眠前面的还有别的关隘。

楼里有暖气，暖烘烘的空气饱含着浓烈的人体气味和陈衣裳的气味——热的、呛的、甜腻发馊的——熏得我发晕。我跌跌撞撞穿过厅底，走进铺石板的走廊，讨了一杯水。我的忍耐已近极限，看不出自己怎么熬得到天亮。倒不全是肉体上的苦楚，更是灵魂受的煎熬。层层叠叠的经历积到这个份上，再难多承一分。想到我不过是几百个破碎女人中的一个，想到这座大厅装的不过是被剥夺者军团的一小撮余部，我害怕了。那是我在人生里从不曾料想到的东西；这份认识让我恐惧。

盥洗室里冷得刺骨，我只得回到睡厅温暖的臭气里。我缩进被窝，试着数羊。可当你四围都是受苦的人，很难心平气和地去想那些安详咀嚼的羊。咳嗽又炸开来，我邻床的女人哭起来了。

===Original===

“I did so want to sleep,” she said. “I’ve been thinking about getting a bed all day, and now there isn’t any chance of peace. My head feels all light, and I shan’t be fit for anything tomorrow. If only I could get some sleep!”

She was a frail creature with big, bright eyes, and she told me in a whisper that she worked in a slop shop in Bethnal Green. She used to rent a couple of rooms with her husband and three children, but he was a German and had been killed in the war, and she and the children had lost their home.

“The kids are at an institution in the country,” she said. “I used to think that one day I’d be able to get them back, but I’ve given up hoping now. It’s cruel difficult to live. I’m afraid, somehow, they’ll forget me, and I always promised him I’d look after them whatever happened. But, what am I to do?” she asked. “What am I to do?”

===Chinese===

「我是多想睡一觉啊，」她说，「为了弄到一张床，我整整想了一天，这会儿却一点儿安生的指望也没有了。我的头发晕，明天什么都干不成了。要是能睡一会儿就好了！」

她是个瘦弱的人，生着一双又大又亮的眼睛。她低声告诉我，她在贝斯纳尔格林一家血汗裁缝铺做工。从前她同丈夫和三个孩子租着两间房，可丈夫是个德国人，死在了战场上，她同孩子们就没了家。

「孩子们在乡下一家收容机构里，」她说，「我原先想着总有一天能把他们接回来，如今不敢指望了。活下去太难了。我就怕，不知怎么的，怕他们会忘了我；我答应过他，不管出什么事都要把孩子照看好。可是，我能怎么办呢？」她问，「我能怎么办呢？」

===Original===

There was a terrible note of resignation in her voice. Indeed, all these poor women seem to accept their lot as though it was the will of God, rather than the inhumanity of man. Their endurance is heroic, their generosity unending; all they want is to live decent, human lives, with some sort of a home, no matter how poor, no matter how fragmentary the furnishing.

These things are of no consequence, so long as the home is theirs.

Most of the women who frequent this shelter make Whitechapel their headquarters. Some of them are employed in slop shops, others are street sellers on their own. Others again, do odd jobs of charing, and a fair proportion are out of work dressmakers, fur workers, or employed in similar trades. There are one or two prostitutes, like my little Cockney friend, but they are the exception. The majority of the women are British. I came across one or two Scandinavian women, whose white skins and fair hair were unimpaired by hardship, and I also met a Russian, her native love of adventure undimmed by what she had passed through. She was young, however, and by force of character and personality, likely to find a niche somehow in the social framework of comfort and security. Only one Jewess did I meet in this shelter. Indeed, I very rarely encountered a woman of this race throughout my experiences.
