We know, of course, that the percentage of Jews among the population in this country is a small percentage, but even so it is, I think, a testimony to their feeling of racial responsibility that so few among them should be without the means of support.

===Chinese===

她的声音里有一种可怕的认命。的确，这些可怜的女人似乎都在把各自的命运当作上帝的旨意来领受，而不是人的不仁。她们坚忍得近乎壮烈，慷慨没有穷尽；她们要的不过是过像样的、人的生活，有某种形式的家，再穷也好，家具再残缺不全也好。

这些都无关紧要，只要那个家是她们自己的。

常来这收容所的女人，多半以白教堂为落脚点。有的在血汗裁缝铺做工，有的自个儿做街头小贩，有的打零工做帮佣，还有不小一部分是失业的裁缝、毛皮工，或干相近行当的。有一两个妓女，像我那位小伦敦妞朋友，但那只是例外。大多数女人是英国人。我碰到过一两个斯堪的纳维亚女人，艰辛也没能磨损她们白皙的皮肤和金色的头发；我还遇见一个俄国女人，天生的爱冒险，一路磨难也没磨暗这份心性。不过她还年轻，凭她的性子和为人，多半能在安逸与安稳的社会框架里给自己觅得一角安身。这家收容所里，我只遇见过一个犹太女人。说实话，整个历练中我极少撞见这个种族的女人。当然，我们知道犹太人在本国人口中占比很小，但即便如此，他们当中落到衣食无着地步的竟如此之少，我想，正见出这个种族守望相助的责任感。

===Original===

The bell roused us at six o’clock, and from every bed dragged out a tired figure with the morning cough, faced with the problem of living yet another day. The art of dressing underneath the sheets is practised in Whitechapel, and it was curious and fascinating to watch women emerging from the chrysalis of American cloth, booted and hatted.

I did not attempt to wash myself that morning. I had stood all I could endure, and I left the shelter as dirty and begrimed as I had entered it. This, I think, shows what creatures of environment we are. The average middle-class woman is not happy without her morning bath, which is an aesthetic enjoyment as well as physically refreshing. But there was I, after a comparatively short sojourn in a world where baths abound not, dismissing the idea of so much as wiping my face with a damp cloth, or removing the black from my finger nails.

A long course in the underworld would, I am sure, cure the most fastidious of that impulse towards clean lingerie, which most of us delight in. Dirty faces and hands, soiled underwear, matted hair, what are these but trifles compared with the devastating problems of board and bed? You will not have much energy left to trouble about cleanliness when you have been bedless for a few nights, with but scant intervals of food.

===Chinese===

六点钟铃响把我们叫醒，每一张床里都拖出一个带着晨咳的疲惫身影，去面对再活一天的难题。在白教堂，人们练就了钻在被窝里穿衣的本事；看女人们从美国漆布的茧壳里钻出来时已然靴帽俱全，真是又古怪又迷人。

那天早上我没有洗脸。能受的我都受过了，离开收容所时，我同进来时一样灰头土脸。我想，这正好说明人是环境的造物。一般的中产阶级女人，早上不洗个澡就不痛快——那既爽利身体，也是审美的享受。可我呢，在那个澡盆稀罕的世界里只短短盘桓了一阵，竟连用湿毛巾抹一把脸、把指甲缝里的黑抠掉这样的念头，都撂下了。

我敢说，在下层世界泡上一长阵，再洁癖的人也会被治好那股恋慕干净内衣的冲动——我们多数人正以此为乐。脏脸脏手、发污的内衣、纠结的头发，比起床铺和饭碗这些毁灭性的难题，又算得了什么？几夜没床睡、几顿没着落之后再谈清洁，你剩不下那份气力。

## XII A Word to the Well-Fed / 十二 进言饱食者

===Original===

I found myself in an unexpected world that morning. It might have been a continental city, it was so early astir. Whitechapel was already going to work; foreign faces gleamed brightly in the street. Everyone wished each other good morning﻿—mostly in foreign tongues, and from the open doorways and shop fronts, you caught occasional gleams of vivid colour. Most of the people are Jews especially the small shopkeepers, and some of them have retained to this day the externals of their Eastern origins.

I saw one old man with a long, white beard, clad in the gaberdine﻿—a long, black coat﻿—typical of the Ghettos of Eastern Europe﻿—and presently there emerged into the street a fine-looking old woman, of about seventy, draped in a red shawl, heavily embroidered. I have seen such a figure very often in the Jewish quarter of Warsaw. It was not only the shawl that was symbolic; this mother in Israel belonged to the old order, under the dispensation of which a married woman always shaved her head, no matter how young or beautiful she might be. The theory was, that as a wife she must no longer appear beautiful in the eyes of any man, though it seems hard her husband was not the exception. In Warsaw you continually meet an old Jewess with an ill-fitting wig that does not cover her bald head, and it came on me as a startling surprise that I should find such an one in Whitechapel.

Trucks of newly-baked bread, rolls and French twists, and delicious little brioche were wheeled about the streets and a stall was already busy with the morning coffee.

===Chinese===

那天早晨，我发觉自己置身于一个出乎意料的世界。它这么早就已苏动，简直像一座欧陆城市。白教堂已经上工了；街上异国的面孔灼灼发亮。人人互道早安——多半用的是外国话；敞开的门口和店面前，不时闪过一抹鲜亮的颜色。这里的人多半是犹太人，小店主尤其如此，有些人至今还保留着东方出身的种种外部印记。

我看见一位白须长长的老人，穿着长袍（gaberdine）——一件黑色的长外套——正是东欧犹太区的典型装束；不多时，街上又走出一位七十岁上下、气度雍然的老妇人，裹一条绣工厚重的红披肩。这样的人物，我在华沙的犹太区见过许多回。有象征意味的不只是那条披肩；这位以色列的母亲属于旧秩序，按那一套法度，妇人一成婚就得剃去头发，不管她多么年轻貌美。道理是：身为人妻，便不可再在任何男人眼里显得美丽——虽然丈夫竟不例外，未免苛酷。在华沙，你会不断遇上戴着一顶不合适的假发、遮不严秃顶的犹太老妇人；想不到在白教堂也会碰见这样一位，我着实吃了一惊。

一车车新出炉的面包、小圆包、法式麻花辫，还有可口的小布里欧修，在街上推来推去；一个摊子已经忙起早晨的咖啡生意了。

===Original===

It is in Whitechapel also that all kinds of small goods are bought and sold. Here is the market for metal trifles such as I dealt in, and I unearthed a little factory where they turn out cigarette cases and match boxes, the equal of any German products. Here, also, street vendors can buy garters at wholesale prices, powder puffs and boxes of chocolates, etc., all the stock-in-trade of the men and women who work public-house bars and serve the queues outside the theatres. It is too risky to try to get rid of these articles on the kerbstone, unless your position with the police is very strong.

Whitechapel, moreover, is one of those places where goods acquired by mysterious means can, without difficulty, be disposed of. There is a certain public-house I know, where I have seen model dresses change hands for as many shillings as a Bond Street establishment would charge pounds. Here also come Paris hats, the proceeds of either a wholesale robbery or the outcome of a good shoplifting. You will see in the Whitechapel Road many a hat which smells of Rue de la Paix, worn by a girl obviously unable to buy it in open market. Furs, also, change hands in the saloon bars of this district, and the less valuable, and also less traceable goods are occasionally bought by the street vendors, who dispose of them to a special clientele.

This is one of the few points where what is known as the criminal world touches the destitute. Generally speaking, the two are separate and distinct. The lawless spirits who adventure in crime, would not tolerate the conditions which the destitute patiently endure. Sometimes, as we have seen, the prostitute crosses the border line and occasionally steals something and is sent to prison. But this is generally the exception; the two sections are not interchangeable.

===Chinese===

白教堂也是各种小百货买卖的地方。金属小杂货的市场就在这儿——我贩的就是这类货——我还寻访到一家小工厂，专做香烟盒和火柴盒，做工不输任何德国货。街头小贩也在这儿按批发价买吊袜带、粉扑、一盒盒巧克力等等——都是那些在酒馆吧台讨生活、在剧院门口伺候排队人群的男男女女的全部家当。想在大路边上脱手这类东西未免太冒险，除非你在警察那边根基很硬。

再者，白教堂还是那种来路神秘的货色不难脱手的地方之一。我知道有那么一家酒馆，在那儿我亲眼见过新款裙子几先令就换了手——同样的东西，邦德街的铺子要卖几英镑。巴黎产的帽子也流到这里，不是批发盗窃的赃物，就是顺手牵羊的好收成。白教堂路上你会见到许多顶带着和平街气息的帽子，戴它们的姑娘明摆着没钱从正牌市场上买。毛皮也在这一带的雅座酒吧里转手；那些价值较低、也不易追查的货色，偶尔由街头小贩买下，再转卖给一群特殊的主顾。

这是所谓犯罪世界与赤贫者相接的少数几个点之一。总的说来，这两个世界判然有别、互不相涉。那些在罪案里闯荡的不法之徒，可受不了赤贫者耐心忍受的境况。有时候，如我们见过的，妓女越界过去，偶尔偷点什么，被送进监狱。但这通常是例外；这两拨人并不互通。

===Original===

Into these bars, where barter and sale nightly take place, the more dejected of the homeless do not come. These have their recognised houses of call, when they have sufficient money to buy themselves a drink. But this is very rarely. Indeed, so low is their scale of living that when a piece of luck enables them to have a glass of beer it not infrequently overpowers them. We often read of a woman without visible means of support, who has been found drunk and incapable in the street. Her ragged condition has been described, and in nine cases out of ten it is traced to the drink craze, which it is presumed is responsible for her destitution. This, I am convinced, is not the case. What has happened is that the poor thing has been given ale, stout, or even a little whisky, and has been unable to withstand its effects. Quite a number of the destitute have lost the desire for drink; they are so unaccustomed to its taste that they do not desire it. The elder women like tobacco, when they can get it, which they generally chew, and others have a strong partiality for snuff.

The morning had grown apace when I reached Blackfriars Bridge and joined the huge crowd of women of all ages who daily journey to the City. The spectacle of this activity roused again my desire for some form of regular work. I reported myself to a Labour Exchange, and was sent to an eating house to wash dishes. I got paid eightpence an hour, and for three hours stood at a sink at a backbreaking angle, dealing with grease-laden dishes, basins and knives and forks.

It was a noisome occupation, and I could not face the possibility of practising it daily. The space was confined and the ventilation inadequate. By the time I signed off I was physically sick.

===Chinese===

这些每夜进行物物交换与买卖的酒吧，无家可归者中更潦倒的人是不来的。这些人自有他们认可的落脚酒馆——等他们凑得出喝一杯的钱。但这很难得。真的，他们的生活水准低到这个地步：一旦运气来了，喝上一杯啤酒，往往就把他们放倒了。我们常读到这样的新闻：某女子无以为生，醉倒在街头，被人发现。她那身褴褛被描绘一通，十有八九归结到酗酒癖上，说是酒把她拖到了赤贫。我确信事实并非如此。真相是：这可怜人被人请了麦酒、黑啤、甚至一点威士忌，扛不住那点后劲。赤贫者中有相当多的人早已失了对酒的欲望；他们太不惯那味道，根本不想喝。年长的女人喜欢烟草——弄得到的话——她们多半是嚼的；另一些则格外偏爱鼻烟。

我走到黑修士桥时，早晨已长大半，我汇入了每日进城的那一大群老老少少的妇女中间。这番忙碌的景象，又唤起了我对某种正经工作的渴望。我到劳工介绍所报了到，被派到一家小饭馆洗盘子。工钱八便士一小时，我在水池边以折断脊背的角度站了三个钟头，对付那些油糊糊的盘子、盆子和刀叉。

这营生恶心，我实在没法想象天天干它。地方局促，通风又不足。到签退的时候，我人已经吐了。

===Original===

It is, I think, the re-invigoration of the open air that enables the homeless to carry on for so many years. Even in the meanest London street you feel the effects of the open sky and the lack of space within the four walls of a building tries you very heavily. This factor must be taken into account in judging those tramps, of whom it is so often said that they object to regular work. I found the confines of a building pressed very hardly on my spirits after but a short period of life in the streets. And if this was the effect on me, who had but tasted destitution, what must the influence of the open air be to those who can remember no other form of existence? It is one of the most curious facts that even in the depth of physical degradation, there are compensations, and those who find themselves at the close of a long day without the hope of a bed, will feel that they have the endurance to go through the night and start again the following morning.

I have been asked often as to the food for which the homeless crave. When you have but a few pence﻿—and it is very rarely there is any more﻿—you go generally to the nearest fried-fish shop and spend your coppers on a piece of haddock and potatoes. If you can add a dash of sauce or a few pickles, you have an ideal meal. Meat is beyond the means of the destitute, save in the way of sausages, and if it were not so, the quality obtainable would put you off your meal. I have already touched on the nausea which ensues on an attempt to satisfy hunger on dry, stale bread. It is worse than useless to give loaves or slices to any beggar at your back door. But you may always be sure one thing will be welcomed, a cup of hot tea, hot coffee, or even cocoa. But it must be hot; the physical craving for a hot drink is almost overmastering.

I remember on one occasion a young woman marched into a café in the East End and demanded a cup of tea. She was in a parlous state as regards boots and dress generally, but her hair was only comparatively dirty, by which I mean she was not afflicted with those masses of tangled growth that have so tragic a significance. She drank her tea, seated at a table, then, having drained the last drop, she marched up to the counter.

===Chinese===

我想，正是户外空气的滋养，让无家可归者能一年年撑下去。哪怕在伦敦最寒酸的街上，你也感受得到头顶开阔的天空；而四面墙壁之内的逼仄，却重重地磨人。评判那些流浪者的时候——人们常说他们不肯干正经活——这个因素必须算进去。在街上只生活了短短一阵，房屋的四壁就沉沉压上了我的心绪。对我这个只尝了尝赤贫滋味的人尚且如此，对那些记不得另一种活法的人来说，露天的影响又该多深？最奇怪的事实之一是：哪怕身处肉体堕落的深渊，也有补偿在——那些熬完漫长一天、对一张床不抱指望的人，仍会觉得自己撑得过这一夜，第二天早晨还能重新开始。

常有人问我，无家可归者馋的是什么吃食。当你只有几个便士——难得会有更多——你通常去最近的一家炸鱼店，把铜板花在一片黑线鳕和土豆上。若能再添一点酱汁或几样泡菜，那便是一顿理想的美餐。肉是赤贫者吃不起的，香肠除外；即便吃得起，能弄到的那个成色也让你倒胃口。拿干硬发霉的面包充饥之后的那种反胃，我已经说过了。把整条或整片的面包施给后门的任何乞丐，比无用还糟。但有一样东西你尽可放心，永远受欢迎：一杯热茶、热咖啡，甚至可可。但必须是热的；对热饮的生理渴望，几乎压倒一切。

我记得有一回，一个年轻女人大步走进东区一家咖啡馆，要了一杯茶。她的靴子和一身衣着都糟糕透顶，头发却只是比较脏而已——我的意思是，她还不到顶着那种纠结成团的长发的地步，那种头发有着极为悲惨的含义。她坐在桌边喝了茶，喝到最后一滴，便大步走到柜台跟前。

===Original===

“I haven’t got any money to pay for it,” she said; “you can send to the police if you like﻿—I don’t mind. But I’ve had my tea,” she added, “you can’t take that from me!”

The proprietor, a polyglot of many nationalities, protested vehemently. But he did not send for the police, and when she went away he gave her some slices of liver sausage with some pickle and a fresh roll.

It is not often that you come across such understanding, and to risk getting food without money is to chance arrest. When once you have been charged at the police court the difficulties of existence are increased a hundredfold. The destitute woman, like the prostitute, once she has been charged, is liable to be run in continuously. Keep out of the police net, and if you are quiet and decently behaved, you will remain out. Once you are enmeshed, unless you are possessed of great force of will and character, you may as well give up the fight.

It is not often the destitute show any great emotion. The women who now and again get a lodging, relieve their pent-up feelings when they find themselves beneath a roof, but the woman who spends more nights in the open than nights in a shelter, has become so far doped that it is pain to her to cry. I have seen women suddenly give way to what looks like causeless anger and fall to abusing a stranger, take up a stone and throw it at a door. I have myself experienced that overwhelming desire to smash up the smug contentment of the well-fed and well-housed section of society. But of the softer feelings little is shown. When a woman gives way to tears you feel as if you had been present at some secret and terrible exposure.

===Chinese===

「我没有钱付账，」她说；「你尽管叫警察去——我不在乎。可茶我已经喝了，」她补一句，「这个你们抢不走！」

店东是个混过多个国籍、会说多种语言的人，气急败坏地抗议了一通。可他终究没叫警察，等她走时，还给了她几片肝肠、一点泡菜和一个新鲜小圆包。

这样的体谅不常见；身上没钱而冒险讨吃食，等于赌被捕。一旦在违警罪法庭受过指控，生存的艰难就要翻上一百倍。赤贫的女人同妓女一样，一旦被控过，往后就随时可能被抓。躲开警察的网，只要你安分、举止端正，就一直在网外。一旦落网，除非你有极强大的意志和性格，不如干脆认输。

赤贫者不大流露强烈的情绪。偶尔弄到铺位的女人，会在屋顶底下释放积压的情感；可是露宿多于投宿的女人，早已麻木到哭都哭不出——哭对她成了苦刑。我见过女人突然发作看似无名的怒火，破口大骂一个陌生人，捡起石头砸门。我自己也体验过那种压倒一切的冲动，想去砸碎社会上饱食安居者们那副沾沾自得的满足。至于较柔软的情感，流露得极少。一个女人一旦落泪，你会觉得自己目睹了一场隐秘而可怕的剖白。

===Original===

It was at a poor doss house in the North of London that I saw a girl give way to sudden irrepressible grief. She was not like the woman in Kennedy Court, she was nourished on too low a diet for such vigorous display. She sat on her bed in the drab garment, discoloured by wind and weather, which had grown to her like an animal’s skin, and the tears poured down her face. She had not a handkerchief on which to wipe them, and now and again she put up her arm, with its dirty coat sleeve, and mopped her cheeks. At first we did not take any notice. It is not polite to offer sympathy or comment. But when her thin shoulders began to shake, and her hands opened and shut convulsively, we knew the breaking point was reached. She explained that her feet hurt her, and we took off the unutterable pieces of leather, bound together by string, and the rags that had once been stockings. Her feet were a mass of running sores, only the most superhuman courage could have forced her to walk upon them. We got round the old woman in charge of the place and persuaded her to produce hot water, and one of us bathed the poor feet and dried them on an apology for a towel. But we knew that in a very little while the flesh would be as discoloured and as painful as it had been. It was unthinkable that she should again put on those jagged bits of leather, those worn and evil-smelling cotton rags. Instinctively we looked at each other. There was some seven or eight women in the room, and we produced our pence and together raised about a shilling. Then a lodger found another pair of stockings in her bundle, gave them to the girl, and a spirited conversation with the woman in charge induced her to produce an aged pair of men’s boots, whose tops were not broken. We gave her fourpence for these, and another twopence for a cup of tea and a piece of bread and marge.

The girl accepted these offerings quite quietly; she was too dazed to say much, but at least we had the satisfaction of knowing that her feet would not be quite so painful, and that she would set out on her day’s tramp with at least something towards board and lodging.

And here I would beg those of my readers, who feel in any sense the desire to help their destitute sisters, to remember their perpetual need for shoes and boots. If it were possible to have some kind of clearing house, where cast-off foot gear could be deposited and sent out again to women’s lodging houses, conditions might be alleviated. It is, I know, the custom of many households to send their cast-off clothes and other oddments to the Salvation Army and the Church Army, and I have nothing to urge against such methods. I would only say that, generally speaking, the down and outs do not, and cannot, benefit by these gifts, for the big organisations have many recognised outlets for the disposal of clothes, and the destitute do not come within their orbit. If, however, people would remit their cast-off footgear to definite shelters with the request that those women who wanted boots might be served, something might be done to assuage the most poignant sufferings of those street dwellers, whose feet are so rarely at rest.

===Chinese===

正是在伦敦北边一家破宿夜所里，我看见一个姑娘突然抑制不住地悲恸起来。她不像肯尼迪大院那位妇人——她吃得太寡淡，闹不起那样剧烈的发作。她坐在床上，身上那件被风吹雨打褪了色的灰布衫，早已长得像动物的皮一样贴身，泪水顺着脸往下淌。她连一块擦泪的手绢都没有，不时抬起胳膊，用脏袖子抹一抹脸颊。起初我们没有理会。在这里，表示怜悯或发议论都不礼貌。可等到她瘦削的肩膀抖起来、双手痉挛地一张一握，我们就知道到极限了。她说她的脚疼，我们脱下那两块难以言说的皮革——用绳子绑在一起——和那几条从前是长袜的破布。她的双脚是一片溃烂的疮口，只有超人的勇气才能逼着自己在上面走路。我们绕过管事的老妇人，劝她端出热水，我们中的一个替那双可怜的脚洗净，用一条聊胜于无的毛巾擦干。可我们知道，要不了多久，那皮肉又会像先前一样乌青疼痛。让她再穿上那些带锯齿的碎皮子、那些穿烂发臭的棉布条，是不可想象的。我们不约而同地面面相觑。屋里约有七八个女人，大家凑出便士，合起来大约一先令。跟着，一位房客从包袱里翻出另一双长袜，给了姑娘；再同管事的老妇人一场火热的交涉，劝得她拿出一双旧的男人靴子，靴筒还没破。这双我们付了四便士，另出两便士买一杯茶、一块面包抹人造黄油。

姑娘安安静静地收下这些馈赠；她昏昏沉沉，说不出多少话。但至少我们可以满意地知道：她的脚不会那么疼了，她出门开始一天的跋涉时，身上至少有了贴补食宿的一点东西。

在这里，我想恳请读者中凡是多少愿意帮助赤贫姐妹的人，记住她们永久的急需：鞋和靴。倘若能设一种调拨处，让废弃的鞋履存在那里，再发往各妇女寄宿舍，境况或可减轻。我知道，许多人家习惯把旧衣杂物送救世军和教会军，对这种做法我没有异议。我只想说，总的说来，山穷水尽的人从这些馈赠里得不着、也没法得着好处，因为大机构有许多认定的衣物处理渠道，赤贫者不在它们的轨道之内。可是，如果人们肯把废弃鞋履寄给明确的收容所，注明凡需要靴子的妇女均可领取，那么这些街头漂泊者最尖锐的苦痛——她们的双脚难得歇息——多少可以缓解。

===Original===

It should not be difficult to arrange such a distribution at the lodging houses licensed by the London County Council, if and when that body institutes a proper system of inspection. As things are, it seems to me highly improbable that the managers or proprietors of these places would concern themselves as to which of their casual inmates wanted boots.

Another alternative would be to establish a place apart from any lodging house, where, for a few pence﻿—or for nothing﻿—derelicts could get covering for their feet. It is, at any rate, worth consideration, for the outlay would be very small, and many of those who at present devote quite large sums to the support of what are termed organised charities, would find that the reduction of comparatively a few shillings from their cheques, would result in that direct alleviation which is the object of the generous-minded.

I have not yet discovered any shelter or lodging house where such help is forthcoming. The Salvation Army Centre in Mare Street do their best to deal with human down and outs, and if they are employable, will find them work and supply a wardrobe. But, as I have said, a very large proportion of the women who walk the pavements have gone beyond regularised assistance, and before they could fit themselves for work, they would have to be found some tiny home of their own where they could recover their powers of resistance. Meanwhile, they endure physical hardships which could be alleviated by a small expenditure of money and the cost of some trouble and thought. Once you have seen the feet of the outcast, you realise the most burning and most practical thing to do, next to the provision of a bed, is to find boots.

===Chinese===

倘使伦敦郡议会当真建立起像样的查验制度，在这些领照的寄宿舍里安排这样一种发放，本非难事。但照现状看，我认为这些地方的经理或业主根本不会操心她们哪个房客缺靴子。

另一个办法，是在寄宿舍之外单设一处，让流落无依的人花几个便士——或者分文不花——就能给双脚讨到遮盖。这无论如何值得考虑：花销很小；而那些如今把大笔款项捐给所谓有组织慈善事业的人会发现，从支票里减下区区几先令，就能换来善心人追求的那种直接的纾困。

我还没有发现任何一家收容所或寄宿舍提供这种帮助。马尔街的救世军中心尽力收容山穷水尽的人，只要还能受雇，就替他们找活计、发衣装。但正如我说过的，在人行道上走着的女人里，有很大一部分早已够不上正规救助的边；要让她们重新胜任工作，先得给她们寻一个小小的自己的家，让她们在那里恢复抵抗力。与此同时，她们忍受着的那些苦难，本可以靠小小的金钱花费、加上一点操劳和心思就得以减轻。只要你见过弃民的那双脚，你就会明白：紧挨着解决床铺之后，最迫切、最实际的事，就是弄到靴子。

===Original===

The homeless are, I admit, difficult to help through the ordinary channels. People who come under sectional headings﻿—discharged prisoners, convicted prostitutes, unmarried mothers﻿—are more easily assisted. Human nature has a weakness for labels. It is distressed, almost affronted, by the silence of apparently inexplicable human wreckage.

“I always ask one of these women you speak of, what is wrong with her,” said a very kindly friend of mine. “But they never will tell me anything. I give them money and buy their matches or anything they have to sell, and I always try to have a little talk with them, but they won’t answer. I ask them where they’re going to sleep, and if they’ve walked far, and they just mumble something and move away.”

I suppose it is difficult if you have never been within a mile of destitution to realise how completely you are cut off from the common channels of communication. The poor women my friend referred to, very probably, did not know exactly where they had come from; they certainly did not know where they would sleep. Further, they associate interrogation with officialdom, and the never absent fear of the institution governs their mind. If they say nothing, little can be proved against them. Once they give an account of themselves they may be caught out in a lie, and between a false statement and a policeman there is a pitifully short distance. You must never hope to learn by direct question. The only way you can find out the truth is to go down into the depths, lead their lives and endure their privations.

===Chinese===

我承认，无家可归者难于通过寻常渠道得到帮助。那些能归进类别名目之下的人——出狱的囚犯、判过刑的妓女、未婚母亲——容易得到援手。人性偏爱标签。面对显然无从解释的人生残骸的沉默，它便惶惶不安，几乎觉得受了冒犯。

「碰到你说的这种女人，我总问问她怎么了，」我的一位非常善良的朋友说。「可她们从来什么都不肯告诉我。我给她们钱，买她们的火柴、买她们叫卖的任何东西，总想跟她们聊上几句，可她们不搭腔。我问她们今夜睡哪儿、走了多远的路，她们只嘟哝一句什么就走开了。」

我想，若你从未在方圆一英里之内靠近过赤贫，你就很难明白她们与寻常的交流渠道隔绝得多么彻底。我朋友说的那些可怜女人，多半说不清自己从哪里来；她们自然更不知道今夜睡哪儿。况且，她们把盘问同官府联在一起，那种对收容机构永不缺席的恐惧统治着她们的心。什么都不说，就几乎无法对他们立案。一旦交代了自己的来历，就可能被抓住话柄；在一句假话和一名警察之间，距离短得可怜。你永远别指望靠直接提问问出真相。要弄清实情，唯一的办法是沉到深处，过她们的日子，受她们的匮乏。

## XIII The Trap of the Institution / 十三 收容机构的陷阱

===Original===

When I began this account of my experiences, I made the point that it was impossible for a woman without a reference or a friendly recommendation to find employment of a regular and recognised description. I steadily tried to get some sort of daily work, but all I succeeded in obtaining was, as I have said, occasional charing jobs, the cleaning of steps, and washing up in a cheap restaurant. And, meanwhile﻿—and I want to emphasise this point﻿—I had a skilled trade at my fingers’ ends. I can cook sufficiently well to get a situation tomorrow, backed up by a personal character from an employer or a friend. But, because I could produce no such proofs of honesty, I was compelled to earn my bread from a different angle.

It may be, and has been, argued that to take a strange woman into your house is to court disaster, it being the explicit belief of ninety people out of every hundred that unrecommended humanity is inevitably dishonest. Indeed, the faith in what is known as a “character” is almost touching in its innocence. Few women give an entirely truthful estimate of their late employee, a wholesome fear of an action for slander restrains the expression of some of their opinions, while fundamental good-heartedness acts as a similar deterrent. For this reason indifferent cooks get situation after situation, and complacently impair the digestions and spoil the tempers of countless families. It occurs to me that it should not need very great courage to engage a woman on probation, so to speak, and set her to cook a specimen meal. If she is adequate, she might be retained, even if such retention meant keeping a close eye on the silver. It would not call for a very great display of trust to adopt this course, remembering how blindly the majority of housewives accept the statement of their predecessors in regard to a prospective household gem.

I do not suggest that a number of outcasts﻿—using the term in the sense that they are outcast from home and security﻿—are competent cooks. But a certain proportion are skilled, as I have said, in the domestic arts, and if an employer could persuade herself to try them out at a proper wage﻿—none of your ninepence an hour﻿—they would discover unlooked for ability, and at the same time have the satisfaction of feeling that they had offered an opportunity, very rarely to be found.

===Chinese===

开始记述这段经历时，我就指出过：一个既无保荐信、又无熟人引荐的女人，想找一份正规的、被认可的工作是不可能的。我不断尝试找些日工，但如前所说，弄到的不过是偶尔的打杂、擦台阶，和在小饭馆洗盘子。而与此同时——这一点我要强调——我手里明明握着一门熟练的手艺。我的烹调拿得出手，只要有一位雇主或朋友出具的品行证明书作后盾，明天就能谋得一个差事。可是，因为我拿不出这等诚实的凭证，我只好换一个角度去挣我的面包。

或许有人要争辩——也确实有人争辩过——把一个来历不明的女人领进家门，无异于自招祸事；十个人里有九个明确相信：没有保荐的人必然不老实。说真的，人们对所谓「品行证明书」的那份信仰，天真得几乎动人。很少有女人肯对离去的雇工给出全然如实的评价：对诽谤官司的健全畏惧，压住了她们的一部分意见；心底的善良同样起着刹车的作用。因此，平庸的厨娘一差接一差，心安理得地败坏着无数家庭的消化、败坏着无数家庭的脾气。我想到：先试用、让她烧一顿样餐看看，这不需要多大的勇气。若她胜任，就不妨留下——哪怕留下意味着盯紧银器。走这条路并不需要付出多大的信任，想想大多数主妇是怎样盲目地听信前任对某位「家务明珠」的评语吧。

我并不是说，大批被逐出家门与安稳之外的弃民都胜任厨娘。但如我所说，她们中有一定比例的人精于家政手艺；倘若雇主能说服自己，按像样的工钱——可不是你们那九便士一小时——试用她们，她们将会发现意想不到的才干，同时也收获这样一种满足：自己提供了一个极其难得的机会。

===Original===

Clothes are, of course, the great barrier. I have experimented deliberately to test my theory that where a woman is concerned, her social value is estimated on externals. I have gone out of my home in a soiled raincoat and a bedraggled hat, and have immediately been placed in a totally different social strata from that to which I am usually relegated. The same policeman who would touch his hat when I pass him at the corner of the street in my ordinary attire, stares blankly at me when I put on shabby garments. The porter at a block of flats who knows me very well and is always most polite, has failed to recognise me when I turned up in what my friends call my “Annie Turner” clothes. I do not think this is the case where a destitute man is concerned. People have learned that they must look beneath the habit in the case of masculine appraisement, but women still suffer from the old test. Bedraggled garments not only spell destitution, but incapacity, dishonesty, and a total lack of sex morals.

In my own particular case I discovered a means profitably to exploit such personality as I possess. I developed my latent talent for commerce, and evolved a system of attack which brought me, in gradual stages of increase, enough to live on. When I went back to my home I was making sufficient on the commission I received on the sale of metal cigarette cases, etc., to keep me in meals, find me a bed﻿—when there was one available﻿—and to permit me to have an occasional wash and bath. Had I kept on a little longer, by extending my area of trade and increasing my experience, I should have been able, either to rent a weekly bed at a lodging house, or to have taken one of those top attics, which are to be found in the alleys and byways of Soho.

It would have taken me a long time, however, to have accumulated sufficient to renovate my wardrobe. I might have managed a new pair of shoes, and perhaps, have picked up a hat in one of the cheaper stalls at Berwick Market, the New Cut or Petticoat Lane, but a complete renewal of wardrobe could not have been accomplished without considerable difficulty. Still, I formed the nucleus of a livelihood which in the fullness of time, would, I think, have grown into a moderate but stable income.

===Chinese===

衣着当然是最大的关卡。我特意做过试验，验证我的理论：就女人而言，她的社会价值是凭外表估算的。我穿过污渍的雨衣、戴过邋遢的帽子出门，立刻被归入与我平常所属的完全不同的社会阶层。同一名警察，我穿着日常衣裳在街角经过时他会碰一碰帽檐，我一换上寒碜的衣服他便直愣愣地瞪我。一栋公寓的门房同我很熟，素来客气之至，可等我穿上朋友们所谓的「安妮·特纳装」露面，他竟认不出我来。我想赤贫的男人不吃这一套。人们已经懂得，给男人估价必须看外表底下的东西；女人却还在受老尺子的折磨。褴褛的衣衫不仅意味着赤贫，还意味着无能、不诚实，以及贞操的全然沦丧。

就我自己而言，我找到了一条门路，把身上这点个性变成进项。我发掘出潜伏的经商才能，摸索出一套进攻的章法，收入节节增长，足以糊口。到结束这趟历练回家时，我靠卖金属香烟盒等的佣金挣的钱，已够我吃饭、寻床——有床可寻的时候——偶尔还洗上一个澡。若再撑上一阵子，扩大地盘、长些经验，我要么能在寄宿舍按周租下一个床位，要么就在苏荷区的小巷陋弄里赁下一间顶层阁楼。

不过，要攒够翻新行头的钱，还得很久。或许对付得了一双新鞋，或许还能在贝里克市场、新街市或衬裙巷的便宜摊子上捡到一顶帽子，但整套衣装的更新，非大费周章不能办到。话虽如此，我已经攒下了一份生计的底子；假以时日，我想它会长成一份不大但稳定的收入。

===Original===

When I had reached that point in my commercial career, however, I should have been faced by new difficulties. Directly you grow prosperous in this particular walk of life, the police get wise to you. While you remain furtive, dirty, and obviously destitute, so long as you are civilly spoken and quiet mannered you are allowed to rub along. But appear on your beat with the least appearance of well-doing, and keep up that appearance for a week or ten days and you will be pounced upon. For this reason my friend of the black plush coat clung to her unsavoury habiliments. For this reason﻿—apart from her vagrant instinct and her tendency to drug﻿—she would never sleep two nights running in the same house. Admitted, that her appearance militated against her in the sense that customers were not drawn towards her, still this disability was counterbalanced by her employment of younger and less dilapidated women who, like myself, worked on commission for sales.

I should say this woman has a tidy sum put away somewhere, probably sewn in the innermost recesses of her rags, where even she cannot easily get hold of it. I know of more than one case where women carry a sum about with them that would surprise the casual observer, but it is very rarely that they will break into this store. They know that once this happens they may lack the resolution to stay their hand, and so winter and summer the greasy wad of fingered notes, done up in newspaper, sewn into oilskin and packed away with intimate garments next the skin, travels about with the owner, for in no circumstances would my friend or those like her, put their money into any sort of bank.

These cases are by no means numerous, nor do I for a moment want to suggest that it is common for an outcast to have a secret hoard. But such phenomena do exist, and they are generally recruited from the educated classes. They have not always been homeless, but have become Arabs of the pavement through circumstances allied to an inborn dislike of ordered routine. Very often these people disappear from the homeless world and return to the place from whence they came. But I do not think they permanently stay there. There is an urge about street life difficult to resist, and once you have experienced the stimulus of an almost complete isolation, there is a danger that you may indulge in it too often.

===Chinese===

然而，在我的经商生涯走到那一步时，新的困难就会迎面而来。你在这个行当里刚一兴旺，警察就盯上你了。只要你鬼鬼祟祟、邋里邋遢、明摆着一副赤贫相，只要你说话和气、举止安静，就任你将就过活。可你一在自己的地盘上露出一点起色的模样，并把这份模样维持上十天八天，你就等着被扑倒吧。正因如此，我那位黑长毛绒大衣的夫人才死抱着她那身可疑的行头。正因如此——撇开她流浪的天性和吸毒的癖好不谈——她从不接连两夜睡在同一所房子里。诚然，她那副模样影响生意，主顾不会被她吸引；但这点劣势被她弥补掉了——她雇用更年轻、没那么破相的女人，像我这样，按销售拿佣金。

我敢说这个女人有一笔可观的钱藏在什么地方，多半缝在她那身破衣裳最深的夹层里，连她自己都不容易够着。我知道不止一桩这样的事：女人身上带着一笔会让偶然的旁观者大吃一惊的钱，可她们极少肯动用这笔储备。她们知道，一旦破了例，也许就再没有收手的决断了；于是冬去夏来，那一卷被手指捻得油乎乎的钞票，用报纸包着、缝进油布、贴身掖在最里层的衣衫里，跟着主人四处漂泊——因为无论何种境况，我的朋友和像她这样的人，都绝不把钱放进任何一种银行。

这类事例绝不算多，我也一刻不想暗示弃民有个秘密积蓄是什么常事。但这种现象确实存在，而且一般出自受过教育的阶层。她们并非从来无家可归，只是在际遇之外又添了天生的对规整刻板生活的厌恶，才做了人行道上的阿拉伯人。这些人常常从无家可归者的世界里消失，回到她们来的地方去。但我想她们不会在那里久留。街头生活有一种难以抵拒的蛊惑；一旦尝过近乎彻底孤独的那种刺激，就有危险：你会一而再地沉溺其间。

===Original===

Such people as I have indicated, do not offer the problems which assail the rank and file. Personality, which in its ultimate, means resistance, is difficult to destroy, but given a long monotone of semi-starvation and lack of easy sleep, its fibres disintegrate and gradually weaken. Change of suffering is a stimulus; the sudden alternation of prosperity with penury inspires the imagination and revives the spirit. My friend of the plush coat had known such contrasting periods and by such means maintained her capacity to fight. But the overwhelming majority of the destitute work an unending treadmill in which lean day succeeds lean day, varied only by the fitful night.

Such an existence effaces individuality, weighs down the will, clogs the instinct to do battle which is the heritage of man. And so my sisters of the street have not the power to find out newer ways of making pence. They respond automatically to a rebuff and human sympathy has lost for them its interior significance.

Something of this you will meet in a woman who has served a long term of imprisonment. She is, as it were, hypnotised by an unending submission and has lost even the desire to break her bonds. One instinct only remains vital, apart from the desire for food and shelter, and that is the passionate determination not to be trapped into an institution.

===Chinese===

我指的这类人，并不会遇上普通成员所面对的那些难题。个性——究其极致，就是抵抗力——是很难摧毁的；可是，在半饥半饱与难得安眠的单调长河里泡久了，它的纤维也会松散、渐渐衰弱。苦难的变换是一种刺激；贫穷与宽裕的骤然交替，能激发想象、振作精神。我那位长毛绒大衣夫人就经历过这样的起落，靠这个维持住了斗志。而赤贫者中的绝大多数，踩的是一架没有尽头的踏车：瘦日子接着瘦日子，唯有忽而一夜的安眠，聊作变换。

这样的生存抹掉个性，压垮意志，堵塞那股属于人类遗产的战斗本能。于是，我街头的姐妹们已无力去寻新的挣便士的路子。她们对冷遇只有机械的反应，人的同情在她们心里已失去了内在的意义。

在服过长期徒刑的女人身上，你会见到几分这样的情形。她仿佛被无休止的屈服催眠了，连挣断锁链的欲望都已死去。只剩一种本能还活着——除了对食宿的渴望——那就是一腔炽烈的决心：决不被诱进任何收容机构。

===Original===

> “I told one of these women that she was really too ill to be in the streets, and I tried to persuade her to go to a hospital, or to let me get her into the infirmary. I looked round to see if I could find a taxi, so that I could take her right away. But when I went to speak to her she had gone. Now, why do you suppose she did that?”

The question was put to me by a really good woman, who most sincerely wanted to assist the human wreck she had encountered. There was in her mind a suspicion that there was some dark and undesirable reason for this strange evasion.

> “Hospitals are always so clean, and you get plenty of care at an infirmary. She must have known she would be more comfortable there than walking about.”

===Chinese===

> 「我跟其中一个女人说，她病成那样实在不该待在街上，我劝她去医院，或者让我送她进济贫医院。我四下张望想找一辆出租汽车，好马上把她送去。可等我回头同她说话，人已经没了。你说说，她为什么要那样？」

问我这话的是一位真正善良的女人，她一片至诚，想救助她撞见的这个人间沉船。她心里疑心，这奇怪的躲闪背后有什么见不得人的缘故。

> 「医院总是那么干净，进了济贫医院也有人悉心照料。她一定知道自己待在那儿，比在外面走来走去舒服。」

===Original===

I tried to explain that there is a bondage of the soul more difficult to bear than even those privations of the body that are the daily portion of the homeless. But I could not make this kind, good creature understand.

> “She would have been better in an Institution, and I don’t see that it matters even if she doesn’t want to go there. She ought to have been taken.”

Those last words embody the terror of the homeless. It is the trap they always scent, and from which, to the last gasp, they will run away. For there is that in common throughout the whole company of the destitute﻿—workers in slop-shops, street-sellers, tramps and prostitutes, one and all, they will try their strength to the verge of collapse before they will enter the portals of any place within the shadow of state control.

Hospitals, we know, are exempt from constituted authority. But there is always the dread that a case of destitution may be referred to the Poor Law Guardians, and that the unhappy “case” may find herself imprisoned in a workhouse.

===Chinese===

我试着解释：有一种灵魂的枷锁，比无家可归者日日领受的那些肉体的匮乏更难承受。可我没法让这善良的好心人明白。

> 「她进收容机构终究是好些。就算她自己不愿去，我看这也不要紧。就该把她带走。」

最后这几个字，道尽了无家可归者的恐怖。那是他们时刻嗅着的陷阱，不到咽气，他们都要从它身边逃开。因为在赤贫者的整个队伍里有一种共通的东西——血汗裁缝铺的工人、街头小贩、流浪汉和妓女，无一例外——他们宁可把力气耗到崩溃的边缘，也不肯迈进任何沾着国家管制影子的机构的门。

我们知道，医院不归这套权力管辖。可他们总怕一桩赤贫的案子会被转到济贫法监护人那里，怕那个不幸的「案子」发现自己被关进了济贫院。

===Original===

What then is to be done for these women?

In the first place I would enlist the help of the charitable to put up a number of free Shelters to be run on the principles adopted by the Salvation Army, which demands no explanations and institutes no inspection, the lack of money for a bed being the sole requirement for admission.

The next step should be shelters where beds can be obtained for threepence, rising in certain districts to fivepence. All these places should afford facilities for washing and should command continual supply of water, hot and cold. There might be an employment bureau in connection with the shelter where those women who are skilled in any domestic work could apply for jobs. The cost of such a scheme would not be enormous. The erection of even one such shelter would do much to assist the work already carried on at the Shelter in Crispin Street, Bishopsgate. It should not be regarded as in any sense a philanthropic enterprise, for philanthropy is generally associated with the idea of dividends, either in this world or the next. It should be, to my mind, a practical recognition of that sisterhood of which we women prate so much, and in whose cause we do so little. While one woman has to walk the streets at night without a place to lay her head, those of us who possess homes, however small, however poor, should regard it as an occasion of reproach. For in the ultimate﻿—I put it to every woman who may read this﻿—there is nothing in ourselves which has accorded us a happier fate. There, but by the favour of circumstances, might go you or I.

Once the homeless woman could feel there was a place for her, the power of resistance would enlarge, and with that quickness, indestructible in our sex, she might rediscover latent abilities. Only, and this is a point whose importance cannot be overestimated, the homeless must not be subjected to any inquisition. If you want to know how they live you must be patient, and if you are of them they will tell you themselves.

===Chinese===

那么，能为这些女人做点什么呢？

首先，我会发动慈善者的力量，多办几所免费收容所，按救世军采用的原则办理：不求解释、不设盘查，唯一的入院条件，是拿不出一张床的钱。

第二步，应当办几所三便士一张床的收容所，某些地区可涨到五便士。这些地方都该有盥洗设备，冷热水源源不断。收容所还可以附设一个职业介绍处，凡精于任何家政活计的女人都可前来应雇。这样一套方案的花费并不巨大。哪怕只建起一所这样的收容所，也能大大支援主教门克里斯平街那家收容所已在做的工作。这不该被看作什么慈善事业，因为慈善总叫人联想到红利——不在此世便在来世。在我看来，它应当是对那份姊妹情谊的切实承认——这份情谊，我们女人夸夸其谈了那么多，为它做的却那么少。只要还有一个女人夜里在街头行走、没有搁头的地方，我们这些有家的人——家再小、再穷——就该引为切肤之耻。因为归根到底——我这话是说给每一位可能读到这些字的女人听的——我们身上并没有什么东西，配得上赐给我们这份更走运的命运。若非际遇垂青，在那里的，本可能是你，是我。

一旦无家可归的女人能感到世上有一个容得下她的地方，她的抵抗力就会壮大；凭着我们女性身上那种毁灭不了的敏慧，她或许能重新发现潜藏的本事。只是——这一点再怎么强调也不为过——绝不可让无家可归者受任何盘问。你想知道她们怎么生活，就得有耐心；你若同她们打成一片，她们自会讲给你听。

===Original===

Women of the “tramp” category, who, like my splendid Kitty of the Casual Ward, pass their life upon the road, need help of a different kind. What is wanted for them is housing accommodation. It is here that individuals can do very much. Put Kitty, or any of her kind into a room, and she will become a self-supporting citizen. They are splendid workers these women of the soil, who can plant, and hoe and dig with any man, and at the same time possess a talent for the softer things of domestic life. A hostel for women engaged in manual work is what is needed, where at a small rental weekly, each tenant could make her own home.

But such a place must be conserved for women such as these. There are innumerable hostels where members of the middle class, typists, secretaries and the rest, can get ample accommodation. I do not know of any place where rooms are let out to women who, like Kitty, cannot ply their trade because they have no permanent place of abode.

Then we have the question of the common lodging house where, at prices varying from tenpence to one and fourpence per night, a bed can be secured. This is an economic proposition, and as such should engage the attention of the capitalist. As I have said, the London County Council refuses to assume the responsibility of running municipal lodging houses for the female sex on the plea that we are difficult to manage. This means, in effect, that the L.C.C., like the majority of people, confuse lack of means with lack of morals, and are terrified to be associated in the work of providing beds for prostitutes lest they should be accused of countenancing a loose method of life.

Prostitutes, indeed, form a fair proportion of the lodgers in licensed houses, and for this reason I suppose the L.C.C. continues to remain extraordinarily lax in the matter of inspection. It would seem that prostitutes may be put to sleep in soiled sheets and on insanitary mattresses; that their lack of chastity should debar them from the use of baths, and that the process of washing shall assume the form of a penance in the winter and an odorous experience in the summer. Prostitutes, in fact, may be exploited by anyone whom the L.C.C. decides to license, and none of the excellent gentlemen and virtuous ladies, who are elected to a seat on the council, are concerned to raise a finger on their behalf.

===Chinese===

「流浪」一类的女人，像我在临时收容病房结识的出色的基蒂那样，一生都在路上，需要的是另一种帮助。她们需要的是栖身的住房。在这上头，个人能做的很多。给基蒂、或任何像她那样的人一间屋，她就能变成自食其力的公民。这些来自田土的女人是了不起的劳动者，栽种、锄地、挖掘样样不输任何男人，同时又有一手家常生活的软功夫。需要的是一所面向体力劳动妇女的旅舍，房租低廉，按周计租，每个房客都能在那里安一个自己的家。

但这样的地方必须留给这一类女人专用。供中产阶级——打字员、秘书之流——下榻的旅舍多得数不清。可像基蒂那样因为没有固定住处而无法操持本行手艺的女人，我还不知道有哪个地方肯租给她们房间。

再就是普通寄宿舍的问题：在那里，从十便士到一先令四便士一夜，可以弄到一张床。这是一桩经济命题，因此理应引起资本家的注意。如我所说，伦敦郡议会拒不承担为女性开办市立寄宿舍的责任，借口是我们难管。这实际上意味着，伦敦郡议会同大多数人一样，把没钱混同于没德行，而且吓得不敢沾手给妓女供床的事，唯恐落个纵容浪荡生活的罪名。

的确，妓女在领照客栈的房客中占相当比例，我想也正因如此，伦敦郡议会在查验上始终松弛得出奇。在它看来，妓女似乎就该睡在脏床单上、睡在不卫生的床垫上；她们失了贞节，就活该被剥夺用浴缸的权利；洗澡这件事，冬天该是一场苦修，夏天该是一番臭烘烘的经历。事实上，只要伦敦郡议会肯发执照，谁都可以剥削妓女；而那些当选议员的可敬绅士与贤淑女士们，没有一位肯为她们抬一抬手指。

===Original===

There are many women speakers who grow eloquent upon the platform on the subject of equal immorality for both sexes. But while they demand ample scope for the practice of free love, they are quite indifferent to the housing conditions of the freelance in the sisterhood. The fact, apparently, that “a little money passes” is sufficient to shut out the less successful harlot from the smallest amenity of life. Thus, while the male party to a sex transaction in a back alley may straightway depart to a bed in Rowton House, clean, well ordered, with the fullest lavatory accommodation, the partner of his moral lapse must be content with unsavoury surroundings at the same price.

An illogical and indefensible position.

There is yet another category of the destitute. Women, who, as I was, are in need of temporary shelter: who are neither tramps nor prostitutes, but simply down on their luck, and for the time being are without any shelter or unable to get one when they have the money to pay for it. The first named have certain places where they can go free of charge. Of these the largest and most kindly run is a Catholic Shelter in Crispin Street under the auspices of the Sisters of the neighbouring Convent of Mercy. This place will be dealt with in a chapter to itself. I have already referred to it, and I mention it now as being on the list of refuges for the penniless, without distinction of creed, and regardless of record. Other shelters include Mare Street, Hackney, and the *Christian Herald* Mission in Union Street, Southwark, which I shall also describe. A third and smaller shelter is in King’s Road, Chelsea. Primarily a home for discharged women prisoners, there are a few beds reserved for the homeless, and even when these are filled it is rarely that anyone is turned away. When we add to these the casual ward of Southwark Workhouse, we cover the free lodgings to be found in London, with the exception, possibly, of some small charitable houses known only to the few.

For the woman who can pay, there are only the public lodging houses, and when these are full, she must either walk the streets, or claim a corner in one of these free shelters.

===Chinese===

有不少女演说家在讲坛上大谈两性道德平等，口若悬河。可她们一面为自由恋爱争取广阔天地，一面对自己姐妹中那些自由职业者的居住条件漠不关心。显然，「有一点钱过手」这个事实，就足以把那混得差些的妓女关在生活最起码的舒适之外。于是，后巷里一桩皮肉交易的男方，抬脚就能去罗顿旅舍睡上一觉——干净、井然、盥洗设备一应俱全；而他那次道德失足的搭档，出了同样的价钱，却只能将就龌龊的环境。

一个不合逻辑、无从辩护的立场。

赤贫者中还有一类：像我当年那样只需暂时栖身的女人——既非流浪汉也非妓女，只是一时倒了运，眼下没有住处，或虽有付床钱的钱却弄不到铺位。前一类人有几处可以白住的地方。其中办得最大、最厚道的是克里斯平街的一家天主教收容所，由邻旁仁爱会修道院的修女主持。这个地方将单辟一章细谈。我前面已经提到过它，此刻再提，是把它列入分文不取的避难所名单：不分教门，不问履历。其余的收容所包括哈克尼的马尔街和萨瑟克联合街的《基督教先驱报》布道所，我也将一一描述。第三所较小，在切尔西的国王路。它主要是收容出狱女犯的之家，也留几张床给无家可归者；即使床住满了，也很少把人拒之门外。再加上萨瑟克济贫院的临时收容病房，伦敦的免费宿夜处就算数尽了——或许还有几所只有少数人知道的小慈善屋，不在此列。

至于付得起钱的女人，就只有那些公共寄宿舍；等这些也满了，她要么在街头走夜路，要么去那几所免费收容所讨一角之地。

===Original===

The last category of the destitute can be described as rovers. Unlike my friend of the plush coat, they do not suggest a romantic, or a criminal past. Neither are they heavy drinkers, nor addicted to dope. They are born with that migratory instinct which prevents them from permanently settling to anything or in any place. They preserve their personality undimmed, for the reason that when times get too bad, they have sufficient resilience to emerge from the underworld and do profitable work.

The rovers are invariably artistic. Men and women, and I have met both, they always possess the gift of expression. They are musicians, singers, and can spontaneously dramatise a situation or a story. Some of them are clever draughtsmen and can dash off a pencil portrait of an onlooker with a sureness of line that is amazing. Their tastes are not expensive, they can be as charming and as content in a shelter as in a restaurant or studio. The one thing they cannot and do not bear is any measure of routine.

Outcasts of this type then find their way to the Crispin Refuge. One such case I remember where a woman of undoubted genius continually returned to claim a bed. She was a brilliant pianist, with exceptional execution, and when she touched the notes you sat up at attention, recognising a master hand. A handsome woman, with a fine head, her accent was cultured, and she could talk on any subject. I was told that this woman’s daughter came to see her at the refuge. She was in a good position, with a husband and children and was always trying to persuade her mother to settle down. It seems that the latter was the widow of a clergyman, who, apparently unable to bear parochial restraint after his death, went forth into the wide world. There was not any money forthcoming from the deceased gentleman, but the daughter explained that she could give her mother a home, and that friends would be pleased to help, and in any case there was always her gift for music.

===Chinese===

赤贫者的最后一类，不妨称作漫游者。她们不像我那位长毛绒大衣夫人，暗示着一段罗曼史或犯罪史；她们也不酗酒，不吸毒。她们生来带着迁徙的本能，什么都拴不住、什么地方都留不住。她们的个性完好无损，因为一旦时局太坏，她们有足够的韧劲钻出下层世界，去干挣钱的活儿。

漫游者无一例外都有艺术气质。男人女人我都见过，个个天生善于表达。他们是乐师、歌手，能把一个场面、一个故事信手演成一出戏。有些人是高明的画师，给旁观者勾铅笔头像，笔触之稳，令人惊叹。他们的趣味花不了几个钱；在收容所里，他们同在餐馆或画室里一样迷人、一样自得。唯一不能忍受、也确实不去忍受的，是任何形式的刻板规律。

这一类弃民最终都汇到克里斯平收容所。我记得其中一桩：一个毫无疑问有天才的女人一次次回来讨一张床。她是出色的钢琴家，演奏技艺超群；她的手指一触琴键，你就不由自主坐直了身子，认出这是一双大师的手。她是个俊美的女人，头颅生得好，谈吐有教养，什么话题都接得住。听说这女人的女儿来收容所看过她。女儿境况不错，有丈夫有孩子，一直劝母亲安定下来。原来这位母亲是位牧师的遗孀；牧师死后，她显然受不了教区的束缚，便走向了广阔的世界。亡故的绅士没有留下什么钱，但女儿说，她可以给母亲一个家，朋友们也乐意帮忙，况且她还有音乐这门本事。

===Original===

The “rover” allowed the daughter to take her out to dinner and to buy tickets for a concert. But further than that she would not go. She stayed her allotted time at the refuge and then departed to play at a cinema in the East End until, growing tired of her paid monotony, she drifted off again, and in the fullness of time came back to Crispin Street.

These types are, of course, few and no social system could cater for them; nor need we waste sympathy upon them, for they possess the thing they desire most﻿—freedom from material responsibilities and from that burden of possessions which so often blocks the way to the kingdom of the spirit.

There will always be wanderers to and fro on the face of the earth, and you may know them by their clear gaze which seems to look upon horizons far beyond our sight. They are largely indifferent to hunger, cold or exposure because﻿—and this is the secret of their content﻿—they can always turn the exigence of the moment to account. Their wants being but small, they have but to do a hand’s turn to find their outstretched palm holds money.

===Chinese===

这位「漫游者」允许女儿带她出去吃饭、买音乐会的票。但再进一步，她就不肯了。她在收容所住满该住的日子，便动身去东区一家电影院弹琴；等弹厌了这份拿钱的单调，又漂了开去；时候一到，再回到克里斯平街。

这一类人当然很少，任何社会制度都伺候不了他们；我们也不必为他们浪费同情，因为他们已经握有自己最想要的东西——免于物质的牵累，免于那副常常堵死通往精神王国之路的财产重担。

大地上永远会有这样来来往往的漫游者；你可以凭那清澈的目光认出他们——那目光仿佛望见了远在我们视野之外的天地。他们大抵不在乎饥饿、寒冷和露宿，因为——这正是他们知足的秘密——他们总能把眼前的窘境变成进项。欲望既小，只需随手一做，摊开的手掌里就躺进了钱。

## XIV At St. Crispin’s / 十四 在圣克里斯平

===Original===

The refuge in Crispin’s Street was founded sixty years ago by one John Gilbert, D.D. It is supported entirely by voluntary contributions, and the work of administration and general management is performed by nuns of the Convent of Mercy. The refuge opens on the first of November and closes on the first of May. At five o’clock each night in these winter months the doors are opened, and the long waiting queue of homeless women let in.

Each applicant is asked her name and nothing else. The sole qualification necessary is her need; nor do the women come alone. The majority of them bring their children, small infants, little toddlers and young boys and girls. There is nothing formal or official in the great, big dining-room. Long trestle tables ran down each side, there is a fireplace at each end from which a cheery blaze leaps out infinitely heartening of a bleak night. One hundred and twelve women are admitted. Beyond that number accommodation cannot go.

There is a steady competition for the privilege of staying at the refuge, and for this reason its whereabouts is not generally discussed in the underworld. Those who know, not unnaturally want to keep the knowledge to themselves, for once you are in the refuge you may rely on fairly consecutive accommodation through the worst months of the year.

===Chinese===

克里斯平街的这所收容所创办于六十年前，创办人是约翰·吉尔伯特神学博士。它完全依靠自愿捐输维持，行政与日常管理由仁爱会修道院的修女担任。收容所每年十一月一日开门，次年五月一日闭门。冬季的这些月份里，每晚五点开门，放门外排长队的无家可归的妇女进来。

每个申请人只被问一句姓名，别无其他。唯一的资格就是她需要栖身；而且来的不止是女人自己。她们多数带着孩子——怀抱的乳婴、蹒跚的幼童、半大的男孩女孩。那间大大的饭堂里没有一点官样文章。长长的条案顺着两边排开，两头各有一座壁炉，欢快的火苗从中跃出，在凄寒的夜里格外暖人心。收容一百一十二名妇女。超过这个数，容纳不下。

能在收容所过夜被视作一种特权，竞争稳定而持续，因此它的下落在下层世界里不常被谈起。知道的人自然想把这知识留在自己心里，因为一旦进了收容所，一年里最难熬的那几个月，你就有望得到接连不断的铺位。

===Original===

When the quota of women is complete the outer doors are shut. A bell rings and everyone troops down to the lavatories and bathrooms. Here is ample accommodation for washing and bathing, with a copper for the boiling of soiled clothes. Clean towels are provided and good soap, and the women revel in the opportunity of cleansing their bodies from the dirt and grime inseparable from their life. There is a foot-bath which runs the length of the wall, and pails, one for each woman, filled with hot water, for those who do not desire total immersion. They wash, not only themselves, but their children and their clothes, which dry speedily before a roaring fire. Some of these poor women have literally no underwear, being garbed only in those rags which form a kind of outer garment. These are provided with chemises and petticoats with a kindly sympathy as healing as the fresh linen.

Ablutions finished, the hundred and twelve, with their attendant children, troop up again to the dining-room, where they have a comfortable meal of cocoa and fresh rolls. Some of the women bring in with them a little butter or jam, which they are allowed to spread upon the bread.

“I always feel I should like to give dripping﻿—butter’s quite impossible at the price,” said one of the Sisters, “but it’s not only the cost of the dripping, it would mean that we should have to provide somebody special to spread it. We haven’t a moment to spare as it is.” On Sunday morning, however, butter is served with the coffee and rolls which every inmate has for breakfast.

===Chinese===

额度一满，大门便关上。铃一响，大家列队下去上盥洗室和浴室。这里洗漱沐浴的设备充足，还有一口大铜锅，专煮脏衣裳。干净的毛巾、上好的肥皂都备着；女人们尽情享受这个机会，把那种与她们的生计分不开的尘垢泥污从身上洗掉。沿墙有一溜洗脚池，还有提桶，一人一只，装着热水，给不想整个下浴的人用。她们不但洗自己，也洗孩子、洗衣裳，衣物在熊熊炉火前干得飞快。有些可怜的女人简直没有内衣，身上只挂着那几片破布，权充外衣。修女们发给她们衬衣和衬裙，那份慈和，同崭新的亚麻布一样有疗效。

洗漱完毕，这一百一十二人带着各自的孩子再列队上饭堂，吃一顿舒坦的晚饭：可可加新鲜小圆包。有的女人随身带一点黄油或果酱，也获许抹在面包上。

「我总想着该供点滴油（dripping）——按现在的价钱，黄油是想也不用想，」一位修女说，「可这不光是滴油的价钱问题，那还得专门雇一个人来抹。我们眼下已经一分钟也匀不出了。」不过星期天早晨，每位收容者的早餐咖啡配圆包之外，还有黄油。

===Original===

The evening meal over, the women sit and talk. Somebody plays the piano﻿—it is an excellent instrument﻿—or sings, or recites, and on occasion the company is moved to dance﻿—middle-aged mothers with big families, elderly grandames and girls in their teens.

There is an atmosphere of cheerfulness, but those who wish can pour out their sorrows, or discuss their prospects with the Sister-in-Charge.

Each inmate is entitled to spend five nights in the refuge, going out during the day to look for work, or follow her own devices. The children, however, stay indoors and go to the school attached to the Convent. Before the expiry of the fifth day enquiries are made as to whether the woman can give any reference. The name of a former employer, however casual, is sufficient, and if this is forthcoming, the length of her stay is increased to another eight days. If, during that time, she is able to get ever so little work, she gets a further invitation for sixteen days, and then goes back to five and so on.

===Chinese===

晚饭吃罢，女人们坐着聊天。有人弹钢琴——那是件上好的乐器——有人唱歌，有人朗诵；兴之所至，大家还会跳起舞来——拖家带口的中年母亲、上了年纪的老太太、十几岁的姑娘。

满屋子是快活的空气；愿意的话，也可以向主管修女倾吐愁苦，或商量今后的打算。

每位收容者可以在所里连住五夜，白天出去找工作或自谋营生。孩子们则留在屋里，上修道院附设的学校。第五天期满之前，会有人问这女人能否出示任何保荐。从前雇主的姓名，哪怕只做过零工，也就够了；拿得出来，居留便延长八天。这期间她若能找到哪怕一星半点的活计，就再获邀十六天，然后又回到五天，如此往复。

===Original===

This system works out to the benefit of what we may describe as a small corporation of families, who otherwise would pass the winter in a state of utter wretchedness, if not exposure. There are, of course, a certain number of casual women who depart after their five nights; some of them do not even stay the whole length of that period. The Sisters are able to find employment for a fair percentage. The newspapers are available each morning and applications are received at the refuge for domestic servants and charwomen. Girls who desire to take up domestic service have a period of training in the convent, and every effort is made to keep in touch with as many refugees as possible.

The dormitory is a huge room with a series of bunks ranged side by side. One hundred and twelve bunks, each one lined with leather for the sake of cleanliness and covered with American cloth; a flock pillow, also covered; and a counterpane of quilted leather. Huge fires burn in the dormitory, and the case and comfort of the warmth takes the stiffness from the limbs, relaxes the overstrained muscles and softens the skin.

The women sleep very placidly and awake really refreshed. Breakfast is served in the dining-room, after which the inmates disperse for the day. The Sister in Charge of the refuge, like most other thoughtful and observant women who help the poor, is of the opinion that destitution has enormously increased since the war owing to the housing famine. It often happens that a woman will come to her in infinite distress, and explain that, like her husband, she is out at work, and that at the end of the day they have returned to find their bits of furniture put on to the landing and the door of their room locked. Another tenant has been found at a higher rent, and the landlord has simply and forcibly ejected them, flinging father, mother and children into the street.

===Chinese===

这套制度惠及的，可以说是一小群以家庭为单位的人；若非如此，她们整个冬天即便不露宿街头，也要陷在彻底的凄惨里。当然，也有一部分过客住满五夜就走，有的连五夜都待不满。修女们能为相当比例的人找到工作。每天早晨有报纸可看，招家政女仆和打杂女工的应征就在收容所里接。愿意干家政的姑娘可到修道院受一段训练；所方还尽力同尽可能多的收容者保持联系。

宿舍是一间大屋，一排排铺位并肩而列。一百一十二个铺位，为求洁净每个都内衬皮革、外覆美国漆布；碎毛枕头同样封着套子，外加一床绗缝的皮面盖被。宿舍里炉火熊熊，那份暖意的安适，把四肢的僵硬化开，把过度劳损的肌肉放松，把皮肤养软。

女人们睡得安稳，醒来真的解乏。早饭在饭堂里开，饭后大家各自散去打发白天。收容所的主管修女同大多数体贴而善观察的助贫女士一样，认为自开战以来，住房饥荒使赤贫大大增加。常有大受煎熬的女人来向她诉说：自己同丈夫一样在外做工，一天下来回到家，发现那点家具被撂在楼梯口，房门上了锁——房东找到了肯出更高租金的房客，干脆强力撵人，把父亲、母亲和孩子一并扔到街上。

===Original===

It is very easy to point out that such cases can appeal to the law, but while the law is being put in motion, the furniture disintegrates or disappears, or, maybe, has to be sold to defray expenses, so that in the ultimate the law does not help at all. If it were possible to pass an act which should bring such a proceeding within the immediate jurisdiction of the police court, so that an officer could straightway go to the landlord and bring him up before the magistrate, something might be done; meanwhile rents go up and up, rooms are more difficult to get, and the landlord of the house seizes every opportunity of increasing his income. In Spitalfields a family thus dispossessed can go to the shelter, the husband is given house room in the men’s quarters, and the wife and children are looked after with the rest. Some few cases there are, the sister admitted, where destitution has been induced by drink. Such cases are always advanced in life; young and middle-aged women cannot and do not drink, as I have myself observed.

It will be seen that for the most part the hospitality of the Crispin Shelter is enjoyed by a selective group, and this, not through any exercise of favouritism, but because the old habitués line up early and get in first.

During the winter evenings, entertainments are organised at the shelter; professional singers, pianists and actors come down and give turns. Indeed, the whole atmosphere is stimulative, and the homeless look forward to the social, quite as much as to the material, benefits provided. On Sunday also, a dinner of hot soup is provided, to which all who so desire may come. They gather in from the street to enjoy the welcome fare. It is good soup, hot, strong, and clean, and it is prepared in large boilers, from which great ladles pour the liquid into huge tureens. No one is turned hungry away. There is a full attendance, but, like the kindly deeds for which this refuge is noted, the fame is not blazoned abroad. The destitute have their own secrets, and as I have said, they may quite possibly preserve a discreet silence, lest they should be crowded out. Refuges run on these lines near the centre of London, and in the outlying suburbs, would do very much to solve the problem of the destitute woman unable to pay for her bed. The working expenses of St. Crispin, as will be seen, are not excessive, and if a registry of employment were kept at each establishment, a certain proportion would emerge from the ranks of the outcast into self-support. Thousands of pounds every year are subscribed for the erection of hostels, where business women and girls engaged in offices may live in decent surroundings, at a figure considerably below the average rate demanded by a boarding house.

===Chinese===

指出这样的人可以诉诸法律，自然容易；可等法律转起来，家具已经散了架或者没了影，或许还得卖掉抵诉讼费，到头来法律一点忙也帮不上。倘若能立一部法，把这种行径直接划归违警罪法庭受理，让警官径直去找房东、把他扭到法官面前，事情或有可为。与此同时，房租一涨再涨，房子越来越难租，房东抓住一切机会增加收入。在斯皮塔佛德，这样被扫地出门的一家人可以去收容所：丈夫安置到男部，妻子孩子同其他人一起照看。修女承认，确有少数赤贫是酒造成的。那样的案子总在人生晚年；年轻和中年女人不会也做不到酗酒——这是我自己观察到的。

由此可见，圣克里斯平的款待大半被一个经筛选的群体享用着；这并非出于什么偏私，只因老熟客们排得早、进得先。

冬天的夜晚，所里组织文娱：职业歌手、钢琴师和演员来客串表演。整个氛围实在振奋人心，无家可归者所期盼的，社会之乐不下于物质之惠。星期天还供应热汤正餐，愿意来的都可以来。他们从街上聚拢进来，享用这份受欢迎的吃食。那是好汤，热的、浓的、干净，大锅熬就，大勺舀进巨大的汤盆。没有人饿着肚子被打发走。场场客满；但正如这所收容所著称的种种善举一样，名声并不外扬。赤贫者有自己的秘密；如我所说，他们很可能审慎地守口如瓶，唯恐人满为患。按这个路子在伦敦市中心和远郊多办收容所，对解决付不起床钱的赤贫女人问题大有可为。如后所见，圣克里斯平的运营开支并不大；若每所再设一个职业登记处，就会有一定比例的人从弃民的队伍里走出来，自食其力。每年都有成千上万英镑的捐款，用于建造职业妇女旅舍，让写字楼里的女职员们体面地栖身，价钱远低于寄宿公馆的行市。

===Original===

I am very glad to feel that business women and girls have these advantages, but it is not for them I am concerned. It is for those others, who, save for the helping hand extended by the Crispin Refuge and one or two similar places, have no hope of a bed unless they can collect the necessary pence.

I have mentioned that those women who wish to spend more than five days are asked to produce some kind of a recommendation. Many of these are very rudimentary. In one case a woman who had been turned out of her room, to make way for a more profitable tenant, asked to be allowed to stay for another week. She was in work and proudly produced a letter from her employer, which ran as follows: “Mary Brown has worked for us for a year, and we ain’t missed nothing yet.” The employee exhibited this testimonial with glowing rapture, for it would seem, in this quarter of London at any rate, dishonesty is the unforgivable crime. This particular inmate stayed on the whole of the season, for no objection is made to the inmates getting paid employment, and in no case is a charge made.

“It helps them to get boots and a few clothes,” said the sister; “if not for themselves for the children. And they want to collect all they can in the winter, or they may have nowhere to go when we close down.”

===Chinese===

职业妇女和姑娘们能有这些便利，我很欣慰；但让我挂心的不是她们。我挂心的是另一些人——若没有圣克里斯平收容所和一两个类似地方伸出的援手，她们除非凑得出那几个便士，便没有睡到床的指望。

我前面提过，想住满五天以上的女人须出示某种保荐。这类保荐许多都简陋得很。有一桩：一个女人被撵出房间，好腾给更有油水的房客，她请求再住一星期。她有工作，自豪地拿出雇主的一封信，信上写道：「玛丽·布朗在我们这里干了一年，到现在没短过我们什么。」这雇工捧着这封荐书，欢喜得放光——看来在伦敦的这一角，至少不老实是无可赦免的罪。这位收容者住满了整个季度；因为所方不反对收容者外出挣钱，而且分文不取。

「这能让她们添双靴子、几件衣裳，」修女说，「自己不添，也是给孩子添。她们得趁冬天多攒些，不然我们一关门，她们就没处可去了。」

===Original===

All sorts and conditions of women go to Crispin Street. I have already spoken of the “rover” type, but this does not exhaust the unexpected.

“We’ve had B.A.’s here,” said the Sister; “women who have taken the highest degrees in science. We’ve also had women doctors and a distinguished artist.”

There wasn’t anything specific to account for their declension in the social scale; none of them seemed to have been in prison, and there was no doubt as to their brains. Probably some shock, a big emotional strain, had broken them. They none of them attempted to get back to their professions, and when suggestions were made that they might be found work, they disappeared. They are distinctly not rovers, for they have no initiative. Something in their nervous or emotional machinery seems to have broken, and they have lost the desire to have it put right.

Most of the women who claim refuge in the shelter are British, the percentage of foreigners is very small, and there are but few Jewesses. There is no distinction as to sect. If a woman be hungry and without a home, to take her in, as the little Sister said, is but a Christian act:﻿—“It is not our business what religion she professes.”

===Chinese===

到克里斯平街来的，各种境况的女人都有。「漫游者」一类我已说过，但意外还不止于此。

「我们这儿来过文学士，」修女说，「有拿过最高理学学位的女人。还来过女医生，和一位颇有声望的画家。」

她们的社会阶梯上这场跌落，说不出具体缘由：似乎谁也没坐过牢，脑子的好更是毋庸置疑。多半是某种打击、一场大的情感重负把她们压断了。她们谁也不设法重回本行；有人提议替她们找活计时，她们就消失了。她们分明不是漫游者——她们没有那份主动。神经或情感的机器里像是有什么断了，而她们已失去把它修好的欲望。

来所里求庇护的女人以英国人居多，外国人比例很小，犹太女人寥寥。教派上毫无区别。用那位小修女的话说，一个女人若又饿又无家可归，收下她不过是件基督徒的本分：「她信什么教，与我们无干。」

===Original===

I have already pointed out that one of the stock arguments against running lodging houses for women, on a charitable or commercial basis, is that they are difficult to manage. And here it is important to note that the nuns who run this shelter are not of this opinion. Mostly, the refugees are very tractable, and observe the simple rules which hospitality requires, without the slightest difficulty. Sometimes there is a battle of words between the occupants of neighbouring bunks in the early hours of the morning, but the entry of a sister﻿—there is always a nun within call﻿—with a patient and courteous request to know the rights and wrongs of the dispute, soon restores peace. Moreover, and this is a point I would urge on the Metropolitan Asylum Board for due consideration, the door of the dormitory remains unlocked, and its occupants are free, if they so wish, to walk about in the cold. Strange to say, they do not seem to desire this peculiar form of recreation, and unless one of them be ill, they all stay in the bunks till the morning bell rings.

The women are not called upon to work for their board and lodging. The house is kept clean and orderly by a small staff engaged for the purpose. It would be no part of Christian courtesy to set five women to scrub the same table or to repolish door handles already burnished, while the degrading task of picking oakum is quite outside the Crispin scheme of things. It is true that the same women return again and again, unlike the postulants for the casual ward; but in one case the aim is to keep the destitute alive, in the other the required object is quite obviously to force them to die, or, at any rate, so to intimidate them that they do not come back.

From the economic point of view, however, as distinct from the human, it would cost the State less to contribute to a shelter run on the lines suggested, than to upkeep the casual ward. For so dire is the discipline by law exacted that it is but rarely that the women’s quarters are full. I have been told by several casuals that the diet served in prison is infinitely better than the food provided in the workhouse, and that, take it for all in all, a term in Holloway is far to be preferred to a sojourn in Southwark, where, outside the personal kindliness of certain officials, nobody cares whether you live or die.

===Chinese===

我已指出，反对以慈善或商业方式开办妇女寄宿舍的老生常谈之一，是说女人难管。而这里值得注意：办这所收容所的修女们不这样看。收容者大抵非常驯顺，招待之道所要求的那几条简单的规矩，她们毫不费力地遵守。清早，相邻铺位之间偶尔起一场口角，但只要一位修女——总有一位修女随叫随到——进来，耐心而客气地问问谁是谁非，安宁很快恢复。而且——这一点我要提请大都会收容事务局认真考虑——宿舍的门夜里并不上锁，想吹冷风的人尽可以起来走动。说来奇怪，她们似乎并不想消遣这一门，除非谁病了，人人都在铺位里待到晨铃响起。

女人们不必为食宿做工。房子由一小批专职雇员保持整洁。让五个女人去擦同一张桌子、把已经锃亮的门把手再抛一遍光，算不得基督徒的礼数；而拣麻絮那种辱人的苦役，更完全在克里斯平的章程之外。不错，同样的女人会一再回来，不像临时收容病房的申请者；可一边的目的是让赤贫者活下去，另一边要达到的目的，明摆着是逼她们死，或者至少吓得她们不敢再来。

然而，从经济而非人道的角度看，资助一所照此办理的收容所，也比维持临时收容病房更省钱。法律苛索的纪律严到这个地步，女部难得有住满的时候。好几个老收容者告诉我：监狱里的伙食比济贫院的好出无数倍；总的算下来，蹲一期霍洛韦，也远胜在萨瑟克熬一段——在萨瑟克，除了个别官员的私人心善，没人管你死活。

## XV The Whole Conclusion of the Matter / 十五 整件事的结论

===Original===

There are two other shelters run on much the same lines as the Providence Night Refuge in Crispin Street, though their accommodation is much smaller.

I have already referred to the Refuge in Union Street, Southwark, which is run under the auspices of the *Christian Herald*, and supported by voluntary contributions. The shelter is an unpretentious-looking place at the corner of a street. It has some thirty-eight beds, for which those who have the money pay from ninepence to a shilling. Those who are destitute are admitted free of charge. This place, like others, is used for residential purposes by those who are able to make it a permanent home. Thus, we find some Old Age Pensioners living there; also a small number of girls engaged in factories in the neighbourhood. There are again a proportion of regular itinerants, if one may use the term, who come to Union Street every little while.

There remains the residuum of those waifs and strays of larger growth, who night and day cover the arid wastes of the London streets. These are admitted free of charge, and are given a meal of cocoa and bread. The refuge closes at ten o’clock, but applications at a later hour are answered by the matron, who, if there should be a bed available, lets the latecomer in. The women go straight into the kitchen in the basement, which, like other rooms of the lodging-house type, is set round with benches. A big coke fire is burning always, at which the women may cook such food as they have brought in. There is a little shop close at hand which does a considerable trade with the destitute, serving cold meats, pickle, vegetable salads and those other articles of diet which make a savoury repast.

===Chinese===

还有两所收容所，办的路数同克里斯平街的天意夜间避难所大同小异，只是规模小得多。

萨瑟克联合街的那所我已提过，它由《基督教先驱报》主办，靠自愿捐输维持。收容所坐落在街角，是一处不起眼的地方。有三十八张上下的床位，有钱的人付九便士到一先令；赤贫者免费收留。这地方同别处一样，也有人把它当长住的家：我们就见到几位养老金领取者住在里面，还有一小批在附近工厂做工的姑娘。另有一定比例的、姑且称之为定期过客的人，隔三差五就来联合街一趟。

剩下的，就是那些年纪大些的流浪儿流浪女——他们日夜散布在伦敦街道这片荒瘠的旷野上。这些人免费收留，还给一顿可可加面包。收容所十点关门，但更晚来叩门的，女管事照样应门，若有空床，便放迟来者进去。女人们径直下到地下室的厨房——同寄宿舍一类的其他屋子一样，四周摆着长凳。一大炉焦炭火终日燃着，女人们可以在这儿做她们带进来的吃食。近旁有一家小铺子，同赤贫者做着不小的生意：冷肉、泡菜、蔬菜色拉，还有各样能把一顿饭弄得有滋有味的吃食。

===Original===

After the women have had their evening meal they go to the lavatories and bath rooms. Plenty of wash basins are here, and a continuous supply of hot and cold water, foot-baths, and an inviting-looking bath, which one is entitled to use free of charge.

Ablutions over, they go upstairs to the sitting-room, provided with a harmonium and a piano. There is a more chastened atmosphere here than at Bishopsgate, but it is a very human place, and the restrictions are not oppressive. Drink is not allowed to be brought on the premises and any woman obviously intoxicated is not allowed to enter. The rooms are spacious and airy, and the beds beautifully clean. There are an average of eight beds in a room, with a different coloured counterpane matching the walls to each room. The mauve dormitory, I am told, is the favourite. And it is certainly attractive, with gentle-coloured bedspreads and softly-tinted walls.

If a woman has her baby with her, she brings it in, and there is a special basket cradle ready for the infant’s reception.

In the morning the destitute women are given a meal of coffee and bread and margarine. The residents, at a payment of twelve shillings a week, get board and lodging. There is no sectarian distinction, and, where possible, work is found for the destitute, either in factories or as domestic servants.

===Chinese===

女人们吃过晚饭，便去盥洗室和浴室。这里洗涤槽很多，冷热水长流不断，有洗脚池，还有一口看着就诱人的浴缸——尽可免费享用。

洗漱完毕，上楼到起坐间，屋里有脚踏风琴和一架钢琴。这里比主教门的气氛收敛些，却是个很有人情味的地方，规矩也不压人。酒不许带上楼，明显喝醉的女人不许进门。房间宽敞通风，床铺干净漂亮。每间屋平均八张床，每间的床罩各配一种颜色，与墙壁相衬。听说淡紫那间最受欢迎。它确实招人喜欢：色调柔和的床罩，配着温淡的墙色。

女人若带着吃奶的婴儿，也可以抱进来；一只专门的篮状摇篮，随时等着接纳小家伙。

早晨，赤贫的女人有一顿咖啡、面包加人造黄油。长住的人每周付十二先令，食宿全包。这里不分教派；凡有可能，就替赤贫者找工作——进工厂，或者做家政。

===Original===

But here, as elsewhere, there remains that section which, for the time being, is unable to be employed. As I have said, only a gentle course of kindly treatment can restore their self-confidence, without which nothing in life can be attempted.

In Union Street the time allowed for the homeless to recover themselves is, of necessity, too short to accomplish any definite alteration in their status. They drift out as they drifted in, washed to and fro on the sea of destitution.

It will be seen that there are present two distinct problems, one of which is in a fair way of being solved﻿—the problem of the woman whose income, earned, or received as a pension, is too small to allow her to start a home for herself. This type in the main is catered for, but for those others, my sisters of the bleeding feet and broken wills, but little has been done. An occasional free night’s lodging at one of these shelters, is the sum total of what they may expect in the present; and for the future, bounded by the terror of an institution, life holds no sort of hope.

There is this distinction between the Refuge in Union Street and the Salvation Army Shelter in Hackney. The last named receive and look after unmarried and expectant mothers. The activities of the first do not cover this particular problem. The funds at command are not sufficient. Moreover, it is felt that there are already organisations ready and willing to assist women in this pathetic predicament. And, in the main, I think this argument is true. There are homes for expectant mothers; homes for unmarried mothers; homes for old age pensioners; and those who can secure a weekly certainty, can always find a bed. The army of the night, however, still remains at the outposts of security; disinherited, unclassed, they stand, an eternal reproach to well-fed womanhood.

===Chinese===

但这里同别处一样，总有那么一部分人，一时还受不得雇用。如我所说，唯有温和善待的慢慢调理，才能恢复她们的自信；没有自信，人生什么都做不成。

在联合街，留给无家可归者恢复自己的时间，必然短得不足以让她们的处境发生任何实质改变。她们漂着进来，又漂着出去，在赤贫的海面上冲来荡去。

可以看出，这里摆着两个判然不同的问题，其中一个已颇有解决的眉目——那就是那种女人：她的收入，无论是挣来的还是养老金，都少得不够安一个自己的家。这一类人大体有人照应；可对另外那些人，我那些流着血的脚、折断了的意志的姐妹，做得还几乎等于零。眼下她们全部的指望，不过是这几所收容所偶尔免费的一夜铺位；至于未来，被机构的恐怖框死，人生不抱任何指望。

联合街收容所同哈克尼的救世军收容所之间有这么一点区别：后者收留并照料未婚母亲和待产母亲，前者的业务不涵盖这个特殊问题。手头的经费不够。再者，人们觉得已有现成的组织，乐意救助处于这种凄惨境地的女人。总的说来，我认为这个说法成立。有待产母亲之家，有未婚母亲之家，有养老金领取者之家；凡能保证按周付钱的人，总找得到床。然而，夜的大军依然驻在安全的边哨之外；被剥夺了继承权、没有名分的她们站在那里，永远是对饱食终日的女性世界的一纸控诉。

===Original===

The small shelter in King’s Road, Chelsea, is run upon lines of such kindliness and understanding that if it were possible to apply these on a larger scale very much would be accomplished. It was founded fourteen years ago, as a home for discharged prisoners, and for police court cases under remand. Some twenty-four women are accommodated, of which three are recruited from the destitute. Here, also, there are some semipermanent inmates, girls who have obtained daily work and have come to regard the hostel as their home. But so long as a bed is available, no one is turned away at any hour of the night, and very often the matron will make up a shakedown for those who otherwise would have to tramp until the morning.

I found no trace of officialdom in the refuge. The beds are clean and comfortable, while the washing accommodation is all that can be desired. The homeless are given a meal of cocoa and bread and margarine for supper, and tea and bread and margarine for breakfast, and a dinner on Sunday. The matron makes it her business to keep in touch with every kind of employer. Mistresses ring her up for domestic help daily, but if the homeless women cannot obtain work she arranges for their reception at various convents, where the unemployable are fed and tended until they become fitted, morally and physically, to enter the labour market once again. It will be seen that this particular home has gone a considerable distance towards the solution of the problem raised above, but of necessity it is circumscribed by the smallness of its establishment﻿—three destitute women a night﻿—supposing that one of the three is permanently put upon her feet, makes but little difference to the vast battalions of the forlorn. But, though it be small, the home is extraordinarily efficient; and I do not use the word merely in its mechanical significance. It is efficient in dealing with spiritual as well as material hurts, and it is good to know that those women and girls who have been placed out under the matron’s kindly help, keep in touch with the shelter, and make it their headquarters for their holidays and recreation hours.

There may be, as I have said, other places similar to this, but they have not come under my notice, nor have I heard of them in conversation with my fellow down and outs. The homeless woman gets a keen scent for places where she receives human treatment, and, save in instances where self-interest is involved in the keeping of the information private, a tramp can always tell a tramp where to get food and shelter.

===Chinese===

切尔西国王路的那所小收容所，办得那样厚道、那样善解人意，倘若能把这套做法推广到更大范围，成就会大得多。它创办于十四年前，是收容出狱女犯和候审的违警罪法庭案件的家。收容约二十四名妇女，其中三名从赤贫者中招。这里同样有半长住的房客——找到了日工的姑娘们，已把旅舍当作自己的家。但只要还有空床，夜里无论几点也没人被拒之门外；女管事还常常就地铺个临铺，给那些不然就得走到天亮的人。

我在所里没有寻到一丝官府气。床铺干净舒适，盥洗设备无可挑剔。无家可归者晚饭有一顿可可、面包加人造黄油，早饭是茶、面包加人造黄油，星期天有正餐。女管事把同各式雇主保持联络当作自己的本分。太太们天天打电话向她要帮佣；无家可归的女人若找不到工，她便安排各修道院接收，那些做不得工的在那里得到饮食和照护，直到身心重新适合进入劳动市场。可见这座小家朝着上文提出的那个问题的解决已经走出很远，只是囿于规模之小——一夜三名赤贫女人——纵使三人中有一人被长久扶起了脚，对凄凉大军而言也不过是杯水车薪。然而家虽小，效率却非凡；我用「效率」一词，不只是机械的意义。它医治精神的创痛同医治物质的一样在行。知道那些经女管事善意安顿出去的女人和姑娘们仍同收容所保持联络，把这里当作假日和闲暇的总部，是件令人欣慰的事。

这样的地方或许还有，如我所说；但它们不曾落入我的视线，在同难友们交谈时也不曾听说。无家可归的女人对哪儿待她有人味儿，鼻子尖得很；除非把这消息藏着符合自己的私利，流浪者总会告诉流浪者，哪儿有吃有住。

===Original===

It is my aim and hope to enlist sufficient support to start a number of these small homes, where the destitute can unite mind, body and estate, and, given time, to shed their sense of inferiority. My other ambition is to awaken public opinion to the shameful inadequacy of public lodging houses for women﻿—inadequacy not only of numbers but of accommodation.

In regard to my first ambition, it is interesting to note that a shelter for the accommodation of say﻿—twenty to twenty-five women, could be run for an astonishingly small amount. I have before me the report for 1924 of the hostel at 497, King’s Road, Chelsea. The upkeep of the hostel, including the discharged prisoners and the destitute women, for the year ending December 31st, amounts to £609 1s. 2d. This sum covers subscriptions, monies paid by women residing in the hostel, and gifts from those who have already stayed there. The total includes salaries and wages, which amount to the amazingly small sum of £125 9s. 4d. The whole work of the house is done by the two matrons, assisted by the inmates, who take their share in the domestic duties in return for the advantages of a home.

It is to me such an amazing result for so comparatively small a sum, that I herewith append an account of the expenditure as recorded in the balance sheet.

===Chinese===

我的目标和希望，是募集足够的支持，多办几座这样的小家，让赤贫者把身、心与仅有的一点家当重新聚拢，假以时日，脱去那份自卑。我的另一个抱负，是唤醒舆论，正视妇女公共寄宿舍可耻的匮乏——既缺数量，也缺设备。

关于第一个抱负，有趣的是：办一所收容——譬如说——二十到二十五名妇女的收容所，花销小得惊人。我手头就有切尔西国王路 497 号旅舍 1924 年度的报告。截至十二月三十一日的一年，旅舍连同出狱女犯与赤贫妇女的维持费，总计 609 英镑 1 先令 2 便士。这笔数目涵盖捐款、住院舍女人缴纳的钱、以及住过的人回赠的礼物。总支出里含工薪，区区 125 英镑 9 先令 4 便士，小得惊人。全宅的活计由两位女管事承担，房客们也搭手——以家务换一个家的种种好处。

这么小的一笔钱竟有如此成绩，在我看来实在惊人，故将资产负债表上记载的支出账目抄录如下。
